**Passage from the vectors of finite type to the smooth row.** To use the above computation in the period the continuity at each point already included in IsMultMap.cont suffices. We give the argument because it avoids specifying a Sobolev constant that works for all the points of the quotient at once. At one place, realise the maximal compact K in unitary coordinates of dimension d≥1, with Haar measure of mass one, and take

    h(k)=(1+Re tr(k)/d)/2,          η_N(k)=h(k)^N / ∫_K h(t)^N dt.

One has 0≤h≤1 and h=1 only at the identity. Outside any neighbourhood of the identity the maximum is a<1. In a smaller neighbourhood of positive measure one has h>b>a; thus the mass of η_N outside the first neighbourhood tends to zero. For smooth v put v_N=∫η_N(k)π(k)v dk. The left translates of η_N lie in a finite space of polynomials in the entries of k and their conjugates, so that v_N is K-finite. It is smooth, since on differentiating π(gk)v every derivative can be passed under the integral over K. Also v_N→v in norm by continuity of the orbit.

Let T be an automorphic row of the space MultT and fix the other components of the tensor. At each point g, the functional v↦T(v)(g) is continuous for a finite number of seminorms of derivatives in v. It can be passed through the integral that defines v_N: the sums approximating that integral converge in those seminorms because the derivatives of the integrand are continuous on K. Using the archimedean equivariance there results

    T(v_N)(g) = ∫_K η_N(k) T(v)(gk) dk.

Since the function on the right before averaging is continuous in (g,k), T(v_N) converges to T(v) uniformly on each compact set of g. For several places the averages can be carried out successively. For a fixed tensor, the resulting formula is the integral of T(v)(g∏k_τ) over the product of the compact groups with the weights η_N. This gives the same uniform convergence with all the factors of finite compact type.

The Bessel period used here is continuous for that convergence: for the fixed column and each fixed translation the row is integrated over a set with representatives in a compact set, with weight of finite absolute integral. Concretely, the quotient of the unipotent group has finite volume and compact representatives, and the same holds for the definite unitary group of the column. In the semidirect product one first reduces the unitary component by a rational point and then the unipotent one; the product of the two compact sets gives compact representatives. The continuous column is bounded there and the character has modulus one. A finite sum of translations requires only a finite union of compact sets. This is the domain of Alpoge's lemma per_add_bound. Thus, if the period vanishes on the vectors of finite compact type, it vanishes also on all the smooth ones and by linearity on their algebraic tensors. This proof does not need to move a limit through the non-compact integrals that appear before unfolding the period.

**Consequence for the chain of the base tuple.** To prove the terminal descent of a row, in the unfolded identity of LastX it suffices to evaluate at I₁(P₁)I_T(P₂)w with the same w and the same P₂ chosen above. The terminal reading, against the fixed row, and the identity of two lifts are only needed on those tests. Varying P₁ runs through H, and the argument of the averages concludes for all the smooth ones. In an interior step one does the same once the smooth vanishing at the upper stage has been obtained: under the integral there appear archimedean translates of w that remain smooth and are therefore already covered. The choices can be made per pair of stages; in the interior step the translation variables of the integral remain free. This reduction eliminates two test quantifiers in each pair and keeps the conclusion on the complete row.

The two infinitesimal identities, the non-vanishing of I and the compact set of integration come from Alpoge's material (LinksChar, IsVacFOfIsVacVec, KFiniteSpanLeSpanRealIntertwinerValues and BesselPeriodRightTranslateSobolevSepContinuous). The common-kernel argument also uses the action of the upper stage. It remains to establish in the reconstruction the value of the unfolded integrals of the tests kept in the hosts of 46; the formalisation treats them in SeeSawLastTwoStepsDoubleLiftPeriodValue and SeeSawFiniteIntegralsDoubleStepValue. The simplification does not by itself establish those identities nor the upper bound of G.

## The vanishing on the definite base of item (O3)

The complete item refers to the row space before taking the quotient. Let Q be the rational matrix of projection to the good rows at the tuple μ₀, and bᵤ(φ,F) the rational pairing at that tuple. To obtain the vanishing on ker Q from MI*₂, fix the embedding σ:K→ℂ of the datum. The compatibility of the reading of the rows with the quotient (theta_eV in the coordinates of the datum) writes the theta row associated with F as i_C(σ(QF)), with the prescribed linear identifications. The period identity of MI*₂ at μ₀ has tuple factor equal to one. Thus σ(bᵤ(φ,F)) is a multiple of the inner product of the image of i_C(σ(QF)) with the transformed spectral column. If QF=0, the first vector is zero, the inner product vanishes, and the injectivity of σ gives bᵤ(φ,F)=0 in K. The proof holds for all columns and all words. In particular, under those hypotheses bᵤ defines a functional on the quotient independently of the representative. This is the proof of the first vanishing **conditional on MI*₂** and on the row dictionary of the realisation, as the second member of the item requires. It corresponds to base_tuple_vanishing_of_hypotheses and PairingDefiniteBaseVanishOfMiStar2 in Alpoge's file.

There are two distinct inclusions of kernels in this stretch. To compare ranks in the quotient it suffices that an arithmetic annihilator be transported to the spectral one. For the arithmetic pairing itself to descend from the original rows one needs ker Q⊆ker bᵤ(φ,·). A rank implication does not automatically give the latter: in K², the identity as arithmetic map and the projection onto the first coordinate as spectral map satisfy the first inclusion of annihilators, but the arithmetic map does not annihilate the second coordinate. For this reason the one-sided comparison is only used after justifying the quotient.

The second vanishing, on the division rows, uses in addition the rational readings of those points and the boundary law BkLaw of the datum. The concrete application is in divisionRows_pairing_vanishes_of_rational_pairing_law (BkLawEntries) and AtHDivGOfMi. In it one employs the rationality of the pairing at rational points and the compatibility of their division readings before applying the vanishing on the definite base. The argument on ker Q proves exactly the part prior to that transport.

## Formula for the pairing in division coordinates

At the base tuple, let Q:X→Y be the surjective matrix of coordinates over the field K of the realisation, with embedding σ:K→ℂ. Q may be rectangular. The reading of rows is a σ-semilinear map J:X→V₀ whose image spans V₀ over ℂ. The reading j:Y→E has injective complex extension and satisfies ιJ=jQ. For each rational column φ and word u, the reading of points supplies a complex functional ℓ on V₀ with ℓ(Jx)=σ(p(φ,x)), where p takes values in K. In the formulas we suppress the complex inclusion of φ. The pairing Bᵤ(φ,ιF) evaluates ℓ at the representative of ιF orthogonal to U=ker(ι|V₀). Assume the vanishing of p(φ,·) on ker Q deduced from MI*₂ in the previous section.

Let S:Y→X be a K-linear section of Q, chosen by lifting a basis of Y. Then

    Bᵤ(φ,jy) = σ(p(φ,Sy))    for all y∈Y.

Indeed, if v∈U we write v=Jℂw, using the complex extension of J and that J(X) spans V₀. One has jℂQℂw=0, so that Qℂw=0. The complex extension of I−SQ fixes w. Its columns belong to ker Q, so that v is a complex combination of vectors Jx with Qx=0. Thus ℓ(v)=0. The vector JSy and the orthogonal representative of jy have the same image under ι. Their difference belongs to U and evaluating ℓ gives the formula. Changing the section S changes Sy by an element of ker Q and preserves the value.

This gives at once rationality and descent once MI*₂ is known. BuRatOfCoordinates, in Alpoge's development, obtains rationality before that vanishing by means of a rational orthogonal projection. For this use in (O3), one can dispense with the computation of the matrix of that projection. The boundary law says that, when Bᵤ has values in σ(K) at rational points, the form Bₖ of the datum satisfies σ(Bₖ(φ,y))=Bᵤ(φ,jy). The injectivity of σ therefore gives

    Bₖ(φ,y) = p(φ,Sy).

This is the equality of columns (colsBk) of the division rows. The contribution that vanishes is that of the change of lift. The form on the quotient may take non-zero values. If there is freedom of choice, the formula determines uniquely the compatible rational form. For a form already fixed one has to establish the boundary law on that same datum. For example, X=Y=K, Q=I and p(φ,x)=φx admit the zero rational pairing as an alternative form, but this fails the equality at (1,1). OpenOrbitOfUnramifiedExponents obtains the law for each witness point of its enlarged datum (extensionWithLaw); applying the argument to the realisation of 46 requires that datum. The remaining components of DivRows3G include the compatibilities of metrics, spaces of lifts and numerical data.

Alpoge's sources for these identities: RationalPairingEqColumnPairing (bk_eq_of_coordinates and pairing_eq_colPairing), SpanRangeInfKerLeOfBasis (perCol_eq_pairing_of_kills), WeightDegreeSizes (RationalMapValues, BkDef and buR_iff) and IrrationalRotationBk (bk_unique and exists_bk_iff).

## Which diagonal estimate (O3) uses

In MainTheoremPublishedInputsThird, the last Siegel–Weil input enters through diagonalRow_of_siegelWeil_unitary. Its conclusion is an estimate on the diagonal Gram block Kᵤᵤ. In the admissible data of the theorem let d be the dimension of the column, D* the positive normalisation, ξ=k f log k and H=‖Kᵤᵤ−I‖²_HS. The inequality used is

    H ≤ 4(Γ+1)d²/D* + (4 exp(−ξ/2) + 2 exp(−ξ))d.

Write δ=d²/D*, E=exp(−ξ), E₂=exp(−ξ/2). The separation of the expansion of the kernel gives H≤3N₁/2+3N₂. The estimates of the degenerate terms, of collisions and of the pairs of corank one (generic and non-generic), together with the bound of the local product P, give

    N₁ ≤ 2P[(s+Γ₁+Γ₂)δ+E₂d],   N₂ ≤ dE,   0≤s≤1,   0≤P≤6/5.

Here Γ₁ and Γ₂ are non-negative. With Γ≥Γ₁+Γ₂ there results H≤(18/5)[(1+Γ)δ+E₂d]+3Ed. Since ξ≥2, E≤(2/5)E₂ by e>5/2. Substituting one copy of Ed by (2/5)E₂d one obtains the stated bound. To combine two majorants exp(Cᵢ y) with y=m⁵ f L≥1 it suffices to take Γ=exp((max(C₁,C₂)+1)y), since 2≤exp(y). The thresholds of the four estimates are taken by maximum, together with 29 in the two closure parameters. The auxiliary counting width is fixed at 3. The Weil radius T is another variable.

This deduction corresponds to DiagonalTheoremOfFourPointBounds and DiagonalRowBoundFromWindowWidthThree. The self-contained proof still requires the four starting estimates for the relevant realisation. In i3:S-prop-NG and i3:str-NG (NG2–NG4), the annex distinguishes the classical evaluation of masses from the estimate of a sum by class of pairs of corank one. To reuse the latter here one has to identify the system of terms, its normalisation and its thresholds.

## The arithmetic sum of minors in (NG4)

The annex already identifies the normalisation for the spectral selection it considers. In i3:prop-diag-rows (lines 12050–12096), on taking the two words equal, the reference mass is δ=dᵤ²/D*. The sum of non-generic pairs of corank one is bounded by δ·2^{−(k−m−3)}exp(C₄m⁵f log b), with m=2r_S+2. The written proof checks the counting conditions in width 3, uses Proposition i3:S-prop-NG and takes the maximum of the thresholds of the rows. Thus, for that selection, the identification of the sum and its normalisation are already done. The result imported at that step is the bound for the non-generic row.

An arithmetic part of the proof of that bound admits the following independent proof. The notation of minors and the use of their fibres come from Alpoge, UnipotentStabiliserCharacterisation and BlockMinorSumCounterexample. The argument that follows replaces the estimate of the integrals and the sum over compositions.

**Lemma.** Let E be a totally complex number field of degree 2f. Choose one embedding σᵥ per conjugate pair. For n≥1, let s=(s₁,…,sₚ) be any ordered composition of n into positive parts and t∈Eᵖ. Write Δ₀=1 and

    Δⱼ = Δⱼ₋₁ (−1)^{sⱼ(sⱼ−1)/2} tⱼ^{sⱼ},
    w(s,t) = ∏ⱼ ∏ᵥ (1+|σᵥ(Δⱼ)|²)².

Then, summing over all compositions and all t whose Δⱼ are non-zero algebraic integers,

    ∑ 1/w(s,t) ≤ (5ᶠ+1)ⁿ ≤ 6^{fn}.

**Proof.** Put h(z)=(1+|z|²)⁻². For z=a+ρ exp(iθ), the angular average of 1+|z|² is 1+|a|²+ρ². By convexity of x↦x⁻², the angular average of h(z) is at least (1+|a|²+ρ²)⁻²≥h(a)(1+ρ²)⁻². Consequently,

    ∫_{|z−a|<1/2} h(z) dA(z) ≥ (π/5) h(a),
    ∫_ℂ h(z) dA(z) = π.

The constants are obtained by integrating 2πρ(1+ρ²)⁻² up to 1/2 and to infinity, respectively. The open polydiscs of radii 1/2 centred at the images (σᵥ(α))ᵥ of the integers α∈O_E are disjoint. If two of them met, all the distances between their centres would be less than one, but for α≠β one has ∏ᵥ|σᵥ(α−β)|²=|N(α−β)|≥1. Integrating the product of the f weights over any finite union of those polydiscs and passing to the supremum there results

    ∑_{α∈O_E} ∏ᵥ h(σᵥ(α)) ≤ 5ᶠ.

The term α=0 equals one, so that for non-zero minors the bound is K=5ᶠ−1. For fixed s and prescribed non-zero values of all the Δⱼ, each quotient Δⱼ/Δⱼ₋₁ determines tⱼ^{sⱼ} up to the prescribed sign. There are at most sⱼ possibilities in E. Hence the sum corresponding to s is bounded by ∏ⱼ(sⱼ K). To sum over all compositions let a₀=1 and aₙ=K∑_{j=1}ⁿ j aₙ₋ⱼ, separating the first part j. With B=K+2 one has

    K ∑_{j≥1} j B^{−j} = KB/(B−1)² = 1−1/(K+1)² < 1.

By induction aₙ≤Bⁿ. Finally B=5ᶠ+1≤6ᶠ because f≥1. All the series have non-negative terms. This proves the lemma, also for n=1.

**Application to the points of Theorem B.** Here n=r_S+1. A canonical point consists of the list of block lengths and of their scalars, so that the map to the pair (s,t) is injective. The end of the j-th block gives the principal minor Δⱼ. Indeed, in the block form each block is antidiagonal with scalar tⱼ, and its determinant is the signed factor of the recurrence. Multiplying on the left by a lower triangular unipotent and on the right by an upper triangular unipotent preserves the leading principal minors: on restricting to the first l rows and columns the product there is that of the three leading submatrices. If an integral representative exists, these minors are algebraic integers. At the points considered the scalars are non-zero. This verifies the integrality, the injection and the identity of the weight that enter theorem_B_second_bound_64 of BesselShellMomentBounds. The lemma replaces 64^{fn} by 6^{fn} at that step.

**Scope in the weighted sum.** In StatementPN, PointSumInput requires, for *each finite set of points that appear*, the sum of the weight of minors multiplied by the density, a power of the height and the square of the Jacobian amplitude. The preceding lemma controls the sum of the **inverse** weights. To indicate where it is used, let W(γ) be the non-negative summand of the rotated archimedean model BSumR. In Alpoge's reduction, the estimate at each place, multiplied over the f places, gives

    W(γ) ≤ w(γ)⁻¹ b^{C m⁵ f} J,    m=2n,

where J is the product of the squared norms of the model and C is uniform in its admissible data. In that multiplication the local denominators of an algebraic integer G≠0 are controlled by ∏ᵥmax(1,|σᵥG|⁻¹)≤∏ᵥmax(1,|σᵥG|), a consequence of |N(G)|≥1. The estimate of the factors of the right-hand side by the local minors is also necessary (absV_G_le). Summing the pointwise inequality one obtains at once ∑W≤6^{fn} b^{C m⁵ f}J. For b≥2 the first factor fits in b^{(3/32)m⁵ f}, since 6ⁿ≤2^{3n} and 3n≤3(2n)⁵/32. The arithmetic lemma holds uniformly without hypotheses on Bessel coefficients. To complete (NG4) the local estimate of those coefficients and the comparisons that identify the weights, the amplitudes and J with those of the row remain necessary. Alpoge's concrete steps are OnePlaceR, term_le and pointSum_of_B3R. This result only replaces the cost of summing the points once the pointwise domination has been obtained.

**Control of the denominator at the points with non-zero amplitude.** It is possible to prove the local inequality used above by means of the inertia of a hermitian matrix. Write n=r+1 and Mⱼ for the leading principal minor of order j of the matrix of the arithmetic point (M₀=1). The definition of the boundary points requires that G=Mₙ·conj(Mₙ)−TT'Mᵣ·conj(Mᵣ) be a non-zero algebraic integer of the CM field. T and T' are here positive parameters of the two columns.

For an orbit with a representative, one considers the two lists of n vectors that define its frames. Each list begins with r totally isotropic vectors, orthogonal to the last one, which has squared hermitian norm −T and −T' respectively. The group that moves the second frame preserves the ambient form, whose negative index is n. If Z is the matrix of cross products of both frames and Eₙₙ the elementary matrix in the last coordinate, the joint Gram matrix is

    H = [ −T Eₙₙ       Z       ]
        [    Z*      −T' Eₙₙ   ].

Its determinant is (−1)ⁿ(|det Z|²−TT'|det Z₍ᵣ₎|²), where Z₍ᵣ₎ is the leading r by r block. In the expansion by permutations one uses both special diagonal entries or neither, since a permutation sends as many rows of the first block to the second as rows of the second to the first. Without both entries one obtains the determinant of the antidiagonal block, (−1)ⁿ|det Z|². On using both there remains the antidiagonal block of size 2r and the sign (−1)ʳ.

If H is singular, the difference between the squares is zero. In the non-singular case it has at most n negative eigenvalues by the ambient index. It also has at most n positive ones, since the space of the first n coordinates is non-positive and every subspace of dimension greater than n intersects it. Since its size is 2n, it has exactly n negative ones and (−1)ⁿ det H>0. Thus |det Z|²−TT'|det Z₍ᵣ₎|²≥0 at every represented point.

In the local dictionary, the relevant minors are those of the canonical point: the unipotent operations leave the leading minors invariant, and the rotation multiplies the entry (i,j) by (−1)ʳ(−i₀)ⁱ(−i₀)ʲ, where i₀ is the imaginary unit. The row and column factors have modulus one, hence the modulus of each leading minor is preserved (GPoint_turnPt). The localisation of its matrix at v multiplies row i and column i by αᵢ=rd(E)^{i−r} for 0≤i≤r, besides applying σᵥ to the entries. Since αᵣ=1, the two minors acquire the same positive factor (∏_{i<r}αᵢ)². On cancelling it, the Gram inequality gives

    0 ≤ σᵥ(G) = |σᵥ(Mₙ)|²−TT'|σᵥ(Mᵣ)|² ≤ |σᵥ(Mₙ)|² = dᵥ.

If the squared orbital amplitude is non-zero at v, there is a representative there by its definition as a supremum over representatives and compatible measures. Outside that case the global summand is already zero. At the other points, ∏ᵥ|σᵥG|≥1 gives ∏ᵥmax(1,|σᵥG|⁻¹)≤∏ᵥmax(1,|σᵥG|)≤∏ᵥ(1+dᵥ). The inequality can be raised to the integer exponent required by the local estimate without introducing a constant depending on the point. This part uses the definition of Alpoge's frames in StatementG, the identity GPoint_turnPt and the localisation leadMinor_loc. The bound for the size of the Bessel coefficients remains a distinct input.

## The local Bessel model and the normalisation of its operator

The annex leaves explicitly open in i3:str-GKin the identification with the theorem of Jiang–Sun–Zhu. For the data of i3:def-bessel that identification is exact. We work over ℂ with conjugation, in the hermitian space V of signature (k+r,r+1) of the annex. Let Xᵢ be the space spanned by the first i vectors e₀,…,eᵢ₋₁ for 0≤i≤r, and Xᵣ₊₁=Xᵣ⊕ℂz. One can normalise z by √T(z) without changing this flag. In the notation of Jiang–Sun–Zhu, the isotropic dual Yᵣ is spanned by the e* and E₀ is the complement of z in the central block, positive definite of dimension k. Taking the conjugate hermitian form allows one to pass between the linearity conventions of the two texts without altering the group.

The subgroup satisfying (g−1)Xᵢ₊₁⊂Xᵢ for 0≤i≤r acts on Xᵣ by unipotent upper triangular matrices. Its action on Xᵣ^⊥/Xᵣ is unitary and fixes the class of z, so that its image lies in U(E₀). Each element of U(E₀) is lifted by acting trivially on the e, the e* and z. After removing that lift, g induces the identity on the central block modulo Xᵣ. These two conditions on the columns are exactly the matrix definition of N in the annex (lines 14973–14977). Hence Sᵣ=N⋊U_z, also for r=0 when only z is fixed. The successive quotients of the flag have complex dimension one. The components of g−1 between two consecutive quotients are gₐ,ₐ₊₁ and, for the last one, (gz)ᵣ₋₁, in the bases of the annex. Using the normalised vector z only multiplies this last coordinate by a positive scalar. On each of the r complex factors the restriction of the character is t↦exp(i Re(c t)) with c≠0, and is therefore generic. For r=0 the condition is empty.

Let H∞ be the space of smooth vectors of the irreducible unitary discrete series σ, with its seminorms of derivatives; it is its Casselman–Wallach globalisation. The irreducible finite-dimensional representation π of U_z is smooth, as is its dual π∨. To B:H∞→W there corresponds the bilinear form (v,η)↦η(Bv) on H∞×W∨. The bound by a finite number of seminorms of i3:def-bessel is here equivalent to continuity. With the diagonal action of Sᵣ in which W∨ carries π∨ inflated from U_z, the form transforms by ψ. Conversely, evaluating on a basis of the dual one recovers B with the same continuity and equivariance. Theorem A of Jiang–Sun–Zhu gives dimension at most one for this space. Thus one obtains i3:prop-bessel for all the discrete series of the statement. The separation bounds between parameters still intervene in the decay used below. References: D. Jiang, B. Sun and C.-B. Zhu, *Uniqueness of Bessel models: the archimedean case*, Theorem A and §§2.1–2.2, https://arxiv.org/pdf/0908.1728 ; J. Bernstein and B. Krötz, *Smooth Fréchet globalizations of Harish-Chandra modules*, https://arxiv.org/abs/0812.1684 .

**The integral form in the case used by i3:prop-amp.** Fix a non-zero smooth Bessel map B for one of the two local data, as the hypothesis of non-zero coefficient in that proposition requires. Let G=U, S=Sᵣ, τ(nh)=ψ(n)π(h) and β(v,w)=∫ₛ ⟨w,σ(s)v⟩τ(s)⁻¹ ds. The inner product is linear in the second variable. We use the measures of i3:def-beop and the Haar measure dg for which dσ>0 is the formal degree. G and S are unimodular: in S the compact group U_z acts on the Haar measure of N by a positive modular character, necessarily trivial.

The convergence and the continuity in both vectors follow from i3:lem-decay and i3:lem-vol. In detail, the first bound gives Cν(v)ν(w)‖s‖⁻ᵃ, with a=4n³ and ν a continuous seminorm. To pass the integrability from G to S, take in S\G an open set of positive measure with local section t in a compact set. The integration formula on the quotient applied to ‖g‖⁻ᵃ, and ‖st‖≤‖s‖sup‖t‖, give ∫ₛ‖s‖⁻ᵃ ds<∞. Consequently, for each fixed w,η, v↦β(v,w)η is a smooth Bessel map in the sense of the annex, by change of variable on the right in S. The inversion of s gives β(w,v)=β(v,w)*. One infers that there exists a unique real scalar c with

    β(v,w) = c (Bv)⊗overline(Bw)     for all smooth v,w.

Indeed, by uniqueness, β(v,w)η is a scalar depending on w and η multiplied by Bv. Choose v₁ and ξ with ⟨ξ,Bv₁⟩=1. That scalar is ⟨ξ,β(v₁,w)η⟩=⟨β(w,v₁)ξ,η⟩. By the same uniqueness β(w,v₁)ξ is a fixed multiple of Bw. The hermitian symmetry forces the scalar of the formula to be real. This argument a priori admits c=0.

**Sign and non-vanishing of c.** Let w₀ be a unit vector of the minimal compact type. Write F(y)=B(σ(y)w₀). The annex proves the boundedness of F in i3:prop-orbbd. Schur orthogonality and the passage through smooth cutoffs i3:lem-cutlim give, for every smooth v,

    Bv = dσ ∫_G ⟨σ(y)w₀,v⟩ F(y) dy.

The integral converges absolutely by the decay of the coefficient, the volume lemma and the boundedness of F. Disintegrate it with dy=ds dẏ on S\G. In the inner integral one can substitute s by s⁻¹. By equivariance of F and unitarity of σ, the integrand resulting on integrating over S is precisely β(v,σ(y)w₀)F(y). We thus have

    Bv = dσ ∫_{S\G} β(v,σ(y)w₀)F(y) dẏ.

If c=0 the right-hand side vanishes for every v, contradicting B≠0. Choosing v with Bv≠0, the factorisation and Fubini show that ∫_{S\G}‖F(y)‖² dẏ is finite; ‖F(y)‖ is independent of the representative. The same equality gives

    1 = dσ c ∫_{S\G} ‖B(σ(y)w₀)‖² dẏ.

In particular c>0. The map l=√c B satisfies the factorisation (A1) on all the smooth vectors. The Harish-Chandra module of σ is generated over the complexified enveloping algebra by w₀. Indeed, this submodule is stable under the connected maximal compact group: each vector is of finite compact type and the infinitesimal stability generates the action on its finite compact space. The irreducibility of the module gives the assertion. The vectors of finite compact type are dense in H∞, so that there is a vector x in that module with Bx≠0. Then β(x,x)≠0, as (A2) asks. The same argument is applied to B'. This is the non-vanishing for the data in which the amplitude formula is used. To assert existence of a non-zero map B in an abstract datum that still lacks one, an independent existence statement is needed.

**What is saved for (NG4).** The preceding normalisation determines exactly the constant of β in terms of a single coefficient of B and a formal degree. It replaces the Gelfand–Kazhdan separation part in the computation of (A1)–(A2) for smooth vectors, under the decay and orbit bounds mentioned. For the pointwise coefficient there remains (A3): the comparison of the orbital of the cell with the integral of the two normalised Bessel functions. The disintegration used above is only over S\G. The cell of (A3) introduces in addition the relative stabiliser of two subgroups and its quotient measure; its convergence is a distinct obligation. Even with (A3), the pointwise quantitative bound of NG4 requires estimating the normalised integral as a function of its minors. In the source it is cell_factor, cell_small and WLoc1 that distinguish the three steps.

**The pointwise estimate that it suffices to check.** In the normalised local coordinates of WLoc1 (Alpoge's WeightedUniformRotatedOfExpansion), put m=2r+2, xⱼ=|det Z₍ⱼ₎| for the leading minors of orders 1,…,r+1 and H=max(1,maxⱼ xⱼ). With the admissible parameters and a section of the big cell fixed, write O(F,γ)=orbSq for the supremum of the squares of the orbitals of F on the cell γ, and J(F)=jNorm2 for the squared norm integrated on the big cell. With q(γ)=ptWeight≥0 on the represented support and g(γ)=Gpt≠0, the right-hand factor of WLoc1 is

    R(γ)=b^{C m⁵} max(1,1/g(γ))^{40(r+1)²}.

For the range 0≤a≤Cₐ m³ of integer exponents, all the pointwise inequalities xⱼ^{2a}qO≤R J are equivalent to a single one:

    H^{2a₀} q(γ) O(F,γ) ≤ R(γ) J(F),     a₀=⌊Cₐ m³⌋,   Cₐ≥0.

If all the minors have modulus ≤1 one uses the inequality of exponent zero. In the other case one uses a maximal minor with a=a₀. Conversely H^{2a₀} dominates all the weights of the range, including those of exponent zero. Thus the two cases are combined without loss in C and without a factor that grows with the number of minors. In the notation of the reduction of Theorem B one takes Cₐ=2(11+A_w), with A_w≥0 the parameter of the global weight.

Moreover, when the factorisation of the orbitals over both classes of cells is available (HasBData of OneBesselTor), the two sides are homogeneous in the same scalar. Indeed, if F has scalar Λ, each orbital integral is ΛΨ and on the big cell it is ΛΦ. Taking Hilbert–Schmidt squares, the supremum over compatible measures and the integral of the big cell gives O(F,γ)=|Λ|²O(Ψ,γ) and J(F)=|Λ|²J(Φ) for Λ≠0; with Λ=0 the two families of integrals are identically zero. If there is a coefficient of non-zero scalar, verifying the inequality for one of them verifies it for all the coefficients of that same local model. The choice may vary with the model, while C has to serve for all of them under the separation hypotheses. The preceding reduction identifies the required inequality; the uniform constant and the convergence of the cells that allow the factorisation are still pending in this reconstruction.

### Convergence on each cell and amplitude formula (A3)

The scheme i3:str-amp of the annex identifies absolute convergence as the content (N) of (A3). It can be proved by means of the coordinates of the orbit. We use the Frobenius norm and the convention of the inner product linear in the second argument. To fix the order of the vectors we take Alpoge's exact coefficient in coefF, ⟨v,σ(g)v'⟩. The verbal description immediately preceding i3:prop-amp writes ⟨σ(g)a,b⟩. With these conventions that second expression gives the coefficient with g inverted; the order does matter on a fixed cell.

**Volume lemma.** For γ∈U(H), let Ω be the orbit of γ under X↦aXb⁻¹, with (a,b)∈N×N. For every orbital measure obtained as a quotient of Haar by Haar of the stabiliser, there exist d≤2n² and finite Cγ such that

    μΩ{X : ‖X‖≤L} ≤ Cγ (2L)ᵈ     (L≥1).

In the Witt basis of the annex, N is unipotent upper triangular. Indeed, it preserves the isotropic flag and its orthogonals, acts by the identity on the central block of the graded object, and its action on the last r quotients of dimension one is dual to the action on the first r. On each quotient of dimension one that action is the identity. Order the entries by rows of decreasing index and, within each row, by columns of increasing index. On separating real and imaginary parts we obtain D=2n² real coordinates. Under X↦aXb⁻¹ each coordinate changes by a linear combination of the preceding ones: in the entry (i,j) there intervene Xᵤᵥ with u≥i, v≤j, and the coefficient of Xᵢⱼ is one.

Write H₀=N×N and Hᵢ for the stabiliser of the first i coordinates of γ. On Hᵢ₋₁ the increment of the coordinate i is an additive homomorphism to ℝ, with kernel Hᵢ. If it is non-zero, there is a one-parameter subgroup hᵢ(t) with increment t. To see this, choose an element h with increment c≠0. The real powers exp(u log h) are polynomials in u by nilpotency, in each of the two factors. The real equations of N and those of stabilisation hold for integer u and hence for all u. The increment for this family is uc, first on the integers and then as a polynomial identity. We take u=t/c. At the indices of identically zero increment one has Hᵢ=Hᵢ₋₁.

Let i₁<⋯<i_d be the indices with non-zero increment. Extracting successively on the left the factor with the prescribed increment, every element has a unique expression modulo the final stabiliser of the form hᵢ₁(t₁)⋯hᵢ_d(t_d)H_D. The coordinate iⱼ of its image in the orbit is tⱼ plus a polynomial in t₁,…,tⱼ₋₁: the later factors fix the first iⱼ coordinates and the earlier ones act by triangular transformations with diagonal one. The coordinates before iⱼ depend only on t₁,…,tⱼ₋₁. From this it results that the orbit is a polynomial graph over its d selected real entries. In those entries, the action of any element of H₀ has triangular Jacobian with diagonal one. The Lebesgue measure of the d entries is invariant. By uniqueness of the invariant measure on the quotient it is a fixed positive multiple of the given orbital measure. The stabilisers are unimodular: the same argument of real powers makes them connected and their Lie algebras are nilpotent. A ball of radius L projects into the cube [−L,L]ᵈ, which proves the inequality, also for an orbit of a single point.

**Application to the majorant of the cell.** Write S=N⋊U_z, S'=N⋊U_z', with product Haar measure as in i3:def-beop. Let R=N∩γNγ⁻¹, embedded in S×S' by u↦(u,γ⁻¹uγ). For s=n₁h₁, t=n₂h₂, the matrix of the majorant is A=s⁻¹γt=h₁⁻¹Xh₂, where X=n₁⁻¹γn₂. The inversion in both factors N identifies the left quotient of N×N by the stabiliser with the orbit of the lemma. The hᵢ are integrated over compact sets of finite measure and ‖X‖≤C‖A‖ with C independent of h₁,h₂. By compatibility of the quotient measures, the volume of {‖A‖≤L} in R\(S×S') is Oγ(Lᵈ). This holds equally for the image of any measure of representatives that satisfies IsOrbQuot: the disintegration identity determines that image uniquely. In detail, the polynomial section of the lemma, followed by inversion, gives a measurable section in the two variables of N. On each fibre multiply a function of the quotient by a non-negative density of integral one on R in that section. The disintegration applied to the product determines its integral on the quotient. A translation of the representative preserves the integral of the density by unimodularity.

Since A∈U(H), ‖A‖≥1. Summing over the annuli 2ʲ≤‖A‖<2ʲ⁺¹ one obtains

    ∫_{R\(S×S')} ‖s⁻¹γt‖⁻ᵇ d(s,t) < ∞,     b=2n²+1.

Here Cγ may depend both on γ and on the Haar measure chosen on R. This suffices for every cell of the statement CellData, which requires that Haar measure. The step that identifies a measure described initially in root coordinates with that Haar measure, if one starts from IsRootMeas instead of CellData, keeps its own geometric hypothesis.

**The identity.** Put a=4n³. By i3:lem-decay, under the separation h≥120n³, the two coefficients of v,w₀ and of v',w₀ are bounded by C‖g‖⁻ᵃ, with constants for the fixed vectors. For A=s⁻¹γt, Schur orthogonality with ‖w₀‖=1 gives

    dσ⁻¹ ⟨v,σ(A)v'⟩ = ∫_U ⟨v,σ(Ax)w₀⟩ overline(⟨v',σ(x)w₀⟩) dx.

In the chosen basis H is hermitian unitary, so that ‖x⁻¹‖=‖x‖. Using ‖A‖≤‖Ax‖‖x‖ and ‖Ax‖≥1, the absolute value of the integrand is bounded by C‖A‖⁻ᵇ‖x‖⁻⁽ᵃ⁻ᵇ⁾. The second factor is integrable on U by i3:lem-vol, since n=p+q≥2 and a−b≥2pq+1. The first is integrable on the quotient by the lemma. The twists τ(s), τ'(t) are unitary, so that the same product, multiplied by ‖ξ‖‖ξ'‖, dominates the norm of the operator-valued integrand. This justifies Fubini.

To make the measures precise on changing the order, let R'=γ⁻¹Rγ with the Haar measure that is the image of that of R. After y=tx, the diagonal action of u∈R on (s,t,y) is (us,u't,u'y), with u'=γ⁻¹uγ. The product of the two twisted coefficients in the new variables is invariant under it: the arguments s⁻¹γy and t⁻¹y remain fixed, and the phases of u and u' coincide by IsRelStab. An integration over R\(S×S') followed by that of x∈U, and one over R'\U followed by that of (s,t)∈S×S', compute the same integral on the diagonal quotient. Indeed, disintegrating first in the pair or first in y one recovers in both cases ds dt dy on integrating over the fibre du. The change y=tx preserves the Haar measure of U for each t. The uniqueness of the disintegration on the quotient gives the equality with those normalisations.

Let F(y)=l(σ(y)w₀), and analogously F' for the second datum. The integral in s of the first vector is

    ∫_S ⟨v,σ(s⁻¹γy)w₀⟩ τ(s)ξ ds = β(σ(γy)w₀,v)ξ = F(γy)⟨lv,ξ⟩.

Here s has been inverted in the definition of β and the factorisation for smooth vectors already obtained for the datum with non-zero Bessel map has been used. For t the same formula gives F'(y)⟨l'v',ξ'⟩. Since that vector enters the conjugate factor of the rank-one operator, there results

    dσ⁻¹ Orb(v,v',ξ,ξ') =
      ⟨lv,ξ⟩⟨ξ',l'v'⟩ ∫_{R'\U} F(γy) ⊗ overline(F'(y)) dẏ.

The integral on the right converges also without the scalar: for the two non-zero maps one can choose vectors of finite compact type and ξ,ξ' with both factors non-zero, by density. The absolute argument above for that choice proves the integrability of the same function of y. The identity then holds for all choices, including those that make the scalar vanish.

This proof writes out item (A3) in the case needed for i3:prop-amp, starting from the decay of the annex, the factorisation above and the compatible measures of the datum. The corresponding definitions in Alpoge's development are CellData and coefF (AmpOrbOfAmpDD), IsOrbQuot and IsRelStab (RootMeasBig), ampJ (RelevantZeroGramTerms) and the identity amp_core' (FlipOneTwo). The volume estimate only gives finiteness cell by cell. For (NG4) one must still bound the size of the orbital with a uniform constant and the prescribed dependence on the minors and on Gpt.

### Transfer of the Pieri identity to the boundary cells

For (NG4) it is convenient to make precise what has to be uniform. In Alpoge's local model, let Vγ(F) be the vector-valued orbital value and Eγ(F)=qγ‖Vγ(F)‖² its density, with qγ≥0 at the represented point. Let N(F)² be the integral of E on the big cell in the coordinates of jN2. The factor qγ is common to all the coefficients of the parameters that we are going to shift: the frames, their compact types, the measures and the section are kept. The comparison sought is with N(F)², and the orbital volume constant of (A3) by itself does not give that comparison. We give a different reduction for the weighted part of wBp_of. The representation-theoretic inputs used in the reduction are specified below.

**An equality on the big cell determines a certain pointwise equality.** Fix γ and a finite sum of spaces of coefficients of distinct parameters μ. Assume that their images in the space of the big cell are orthogonal for the product that defines N², and that in each space the unweighted estimate

    Eγ(U) ≤ Kγ N(U)²,     Kγ < ∞.

has been proved. The estimate holds for any linear combination within one and the same space. Write Lγ(U)=√qγ Vγ(U), linear for coefficients with absolutely convergent orbital. If F=∑μ Fμ has zero image almost everywhere for the weighted measure of the big cell, then 0=N(F)²=∑μ N(Fμ)². The unweighted estimate implies Lγ(Fμ)=0 for every μ. In particular Lγ(F)=0. This implication uses only the constant of the individual summands; in the passage from one equality to the other there is no cardinality factor. It refers to a finite sum and does not require continuity of the orbital on approaching the boundary.

**Application to a minor.** Let D(g) be the leading minor of order l, 1≤l≤r+1, of the matrix of products of the frames. At the point γ it equals Dγ. On the whole relative orbit it is constant: the initial frames are transformed by unipotent triangular matrices within their first block of l vectors; the compact factors fix those vectors. Hence Vγ(DJ)=Dγ Vγ(J) wherever the integrals converge. The product can also be decomposed into coefficients of discrete series. Indeed, each entry of the minor is y*Hg x, linear in the entries of g (StatementG, Zhat and Dmin). The determinant is a homogeneous polynomial of degree l in those entries. For an entry gᵢⱼ, the formula of the annex i3:lem-split with tensors supported on the coordinates i,j expresses the product of gᵢⱼ and a coefficient of parameter μ as a sum of coefficients of parameters μ+eₛ; the sign of the coordinate i is absorbed into the scalar. The change of basis to the diagonal model is linear and fixed. On multiplying l entries, and iterating term by term, all the shifts are non-negative and of sum l. The separation hypothesis is maintained in the iteration if the initial margin over the threshold of the lemma exceeds the total degree used, since each step reduces the separation by at most one. In wBp_of one has h≥Cg Y, with Y=gapY≥max(n³,m⁴), m=2(r+1), and Cg≥10⁶+100|Cₐ|. Since la≤|Cₐ|m⁴, there remains h−la≥120n³, sufficient also for the decay that (A3) requires. No conjugate entries of g nor tensoring by the dual intervene here. The convergence on the cell of each summand is deduced from the decay for the shifted parameters and from the volume lemma above.

In the normalised family of ProductMoment, Jν is the coefficient of parameter λ+ν and χ_I is the indicator vector of I⊂{1,…,n}, with n=2r+k+1. On the big cell, supplied_pieri gives

    D V(Jν) = ∑_{|I|=l} τν,I V(Jν+χ_I).

It is an equality of vectors at the regular points with Gpt>0. The other points have zero energy density in the definition of jN2 (ew_zero_of). For a ν reachable in fewer than a steps, apply the above result to DJν−∑ τν,I Jν+χ_I. We first group all the coefficients of the same parameter. By the polynomial description, each grouped parameter is λ+η with η non-negative and of sum at most la. It is therefore in the ball of radius (r+1)a where the unweighted bound hB is used in wBp_of. The orthogonality of distinct parameters is that of the product β of the big cell used in wsp_orth; the identification of its squared norm with jN2 is normSq_eq and jN2_eq_normSq_base. Consequently for each γ with the unweighted estimate in that ball one obtains

    Dγ Lγ(Jν) = ∑_{|I|=l} τν,I Lγ(Jν+χ_I).

The conclusion is kept when qγ=0, with both members zero. In making this transfer the same measures and the same model of cells are used for both sides. The comparisons that lead from that model to orbSq in WLoc1 remain necessary.

**Pointwise iteration.** Write Mν=N(Jν). On an edge ν→η=ν+χ_I with τν,I≠0, sup_norm of the family gives Mν²=c d(λ+ν) and Mη²=c d(λ+η), with one and the same c>0 and the formal degree d. For Jν=0 the outgoing edges have coefficient zero. There is a direct proof of Mη²/Mν²≤nˡ from the degree formula. For μ=λ+ν strictly decreasing, with integer differences, the Vandermonde formula gives a quotient of factors (μᵢ−μⱼ+χ_I(i)−χ_I(j))/(μᵢ−μⱼ) for i<j. At the admitted parameters all are non-negative. They can exceed one only for i∈I, j∉I; for each i their product is bounded by ∏_{j>i}(1+1/(j−i))=n−i+1 in indices from 1 to n. Multiplying one obtains the inequality. The degree formula and the common normalisation are the inputs of this computation.

Take X=b^{r+1}, b≥n, and assume |τν,I|≤H for all the edges that are used. There are at most nˡ≤X subsets of size l, and Mη≤√X Mν. At the leaves at depth a we have ‖Lγ(Jη)‖≤√Kγ Mη by the unweighted bound. An induction towards the root in the transferred identity, using the triangle inequality, gives for t remaining steps

    |Dγ|ᵗ ‖Lγ(Jν)‖ ≤ √Kγ (H X^{3/2})ᵗ Mν.

Indeed, the sum of |τν,I| Mν+χ_I is bounded by HX^{3/2}Mν. The proof includes the leaves or branches of zero energy and does not divide by Dγ. On squaring at the root there results

    |Dγ|^{2a} Eγ(J₀) ≤ Kγ (H² X³)ᵃ N(J₀)².

In Alpoge's notation, H=[Hsq(r,T,b) Hsq(r,T',b)]^{(r+1)/2}, Hsq(r,T,b)=max(1,T)b^{20(r+1)}. This is the bound of tau_bound, which still requires its branching proof. If 1≤T,T'≤b^{C m} and m=2(r+1), then H²≤b^{(C+10)m²}. Moreover X³=b^{3(r+1)}≤b^{2m²}. Hence (H²X³)ᵃ≤b^{(C+12)m²a}. For a≤Cₐ m³ one obtains exactly the order of cost b^{(C+12)Cₐ m⁵}. With N(f)>0 one uses J₀=t f, t>0, from suppliedData_existsW and cancels t². With N(f)=0 it suffices to apply the unweighted inequality directly to f. The case a=0 is that same inequality.

This gives a path from the unweighted estimate to the final weight without introducing momL, without estimating an integral norm of order a and without the additional factor (2n+1)^{2(r+1)a} of weighted. For this deduction one uses from Alpoge's development the Pieri identity on the big cell, the orthogonality of the parameters, the family with norm fixed by degrees, the bound of τ and the unweighted estimate for the shifted coefficients. The two alternatives of compact types that supply the latter in wBp_of are EndDiffW and EndEqW, with their own expansions of derivatives. The reduction proves how to transfer and iterate the identity under those inputs; merely writing it down does not prove the unweighted bound nor (NG4).

### A direct estimate for the growth of a block

In the unweighted step of (NG4), the local parameter γ is a list of blocks (s,z), with s a positive integer, z a non-zero complex number and sum of sizes r+1. In Alpoge's reduction the energy E of a block of size s is compared with energies of size s−1 by means of a kernel of two variables. For s≥2 we give a proof starting from the local expansion of that kernel, with the area normalisation dA of ℂ. Only the expansion on compact annuli away from zero will be needed.

**Lemma.** Let ψ(w)=exp(i Re(βw)), with ψ(ηw)=exp(2i Im w) for η fixed in {1,−1}. Let |ε|=1, s≥2 and H a complex Hilbert space. For X≠0 let u_X∈L²(ℂ,H); let v be a strongly measurable function, with s|z|^{s−1}v(z) locally square integrable away from zero. Assume that on each compact annulus B of positive radii one has, for |X| sufficiently large and almost every D∈B,

    u_X(D) = ∑_{zˢ=εD} ψ(s X z) v(z) + O_B(1/|X|)   in H.

Define U_X(c)=|X|^{−(s−1)}u_X(c/X^{s−1}). Let κ₁>0, κ₂≥0, M≥0, Λ>0 and K>2. Write m(d)=min(1,(Λ/d)^K) for d>0 and m(0)=1. Assume that

    κ₁ |⟨U_X,U_Y⟩_{L²}| ≤ M m(|X−Y|)

for non-zero X,Y (it suffices for X=x+h, Y=x+h' and every sufficiently large real x, for each fixed pair of translations h,h'). If E(z)=κ₂ s²|z|^{2(s−1)}‖v(z)‖², then

    E(z) ≤ (κ₂/κ₁) [K/(π(K−2))] Λ² M

almost everywhere and at each non-zero point where E is continuous.

**Proof.** Put F(z)=s²|z|^{2(s−1)}‖v(z)‖². For translations h₁,…,h_d and scalars c₁,…,c_d form the finite sum of the U_{x+hᵢ} with coefficients cᵢ. Its squared norm is at most (M/κ₁)∑ᵢⱼ |cᵢcⱼ|m(|hᵢ−hⱼ|), by the Gram hypothesis. The complete norm dominates its integral over the region where c=x^{s−1}D and D runs through B. With that change of variable, in the restricted integral each vector becomes

    |ρᵢ|ˢ u_{x+hᵢ}(ρᵢˢ D),      ρᵢ=(x/(x+hᵢ))^{(s−1)/s},

using the branch close to one. For x→+∞ the points ρᵢˢ D lie in a single, slightly larger compact annulus. The expansion there replaces the vector, with error o(1) in L²(B), by

    ∑_{zˢ=εD} ψ(sxz) ψ(hᵢz) v(z).

In detail, the roots of ερᵢˢD are exactly ρᵢz. One has s(x+hᵢ)ρᵢ=sx+hᵢ+O(1/x), by Taylor in hᵢ/x for fixed hᵢ. The error of the phase tends uniformly to zero on B. Moreover v(ρᵢz)→v(z) in L² on the preimage of B under z↦ε⁻¹zˢ. This is seen by cutting v off on a slightly larger annulus and approximating it in L² by continuous functions of compact support: the dilations have bounded norms for ρᵢ close to one. The Jacobian of the change of variable is bounded above and below on the annulus, and there are s roots. These facts control the errors of the finite sum and of the scalars |ρᵢ|ˢ.

Let P(z)=∑ᵢ cᵢ ψ(hᵢz). In the square of the restricted norm, use for each root z as second root ζz, with ζˢ=1. The change D=ε⁻¹zˢ in the sum over the first root has Jacobian s²|z|^{2(s−1)}; it integrates exactly all the sheets. For ζ≠1 there remains the phase ψ(sx(ζ−1)z), multiplied by a fixed integrable function, by Cauchy–Schwarz on the annulus. The frequency tends to infinity, so that the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma annihilates those terms in the limit. For ζ=1 the contribution is exactly the integral of F|P|² over the preimage of B. Exhausting ℂ without the origin by annuli, one obtains

    ∫ F(z)|∑ᵢ cᵢ ψ(hᵢz)|² dA(z)
        ≤ (M/κ₁) ∑ᵢⱼ |cᵢcⱼ| m(|hᵢ−hⱼ|).                         (1)

The step rests on restricting the square of a norm before taking the limit; it does not require controlling the mass of u_X that might leave the annuli. With a single translation, (1) also gives ∫F≤M/κ₁.

For p continuous of compact support, approximate ∫p(h)ψ(hz)dA(h)=Tp(z) by Riemann sums in (1). On the left they are dominated by a constant times F, and on the right the continuous kernel m allows the limit of the double sum. By Cauchy–Schwarz for p and its translates,

    ∫ F|Tp|² ≤ (M/κ₁) ∫∫ |p(h)p(h')|m(|h−h'|) dA(h)dA(h')
              ≤ (M/κ₁) ‖p‖₂² ∫ m(|u|) dA(u).

The Fourier convention matters: with the character exp(2i Im(hz)) the change to ordinary frequency has absolute determinant π⁻² in the convention exp(2πi ξ·z). By Plancherel ‖Tp‖₂²=π²‖p‖₂², and the Tp are dense in L². The radial integral of the kernel is πΛ²+2πΛ²/(K−2). Applying the inequality first with min(F,L), which is a bounded multiplier, it extends by density to any function of L². Taking indicator functions of sets of finite measure and then L→∞ there results F≤(M/κ₁)KΛ²/[π(K−2)] almost everywhere. Multiplying by κ₂ concludes the proof. Continuity extends the inequality to each required point.

**Application and constants.** In CutGermParts, GermDataAt identifies E(z) with κ₂F(z) and the modulus of the kernel of the smaller block, at t=X⁻¹, t'=Y⁻¹, with κ₁|⟨U_X,U_Y⟩|. In StatementTBB, KernelArgumentHypothesisScalar bounds that modulus by m(|X−Y|) multiplied by the half-sum of two earlier energies. If M dominates these energies for all the allowed extensions of the word and for every t≠0, this is precisely the Gram hypothesis of the lemma. The continuity needed is GermContAt. At the lower level CutMainCpt, germDataLowAll_of_partsCpt takes κ₁=π^{2(s−1)}, κ₂=1. At the upper one germDataTopAll_of_partsCpt takes κ₁=1, κ₂=π^{−2(s−1)}. The **quotient** is the same. There remains, on enlarging from s−1 to s at both levels,

    E_s(z) ≤ π^{−2(s−1)} [K/(π(K−2))] Λ² M.                       (2)

To start a block of size one, HeadContinuityAtZero and KernelFourierIntegralBound give a continuous integrable function whose transform in that same convention has modulus bounded by M m. One takes M as the maximum of the energies of the prefix among the admissible extensions of the word; the set is finite and contains the initial word. Fourier inversion has factor π⁻², so that the initial cost is KΛ²/[π(K−2)]. To enlarge the block, the induction gives M uniformly in t and in the extensions of up to K letters; the maximum is taken over the accumulated extensions of the prefix. If the total available is N≥K(r+1), at each level there are enough letters. Thus, for a point γ of sizes s₁,…,s_d, m₀=∑sᵢ=r+1, and an admissible word w' of length at most K m₀, one obtains, with the coefficient and the normalisation nn fixed,

    E_{m₀}(γ,[]) ≤ [KΛ²/(π(K−2))]^{m₀}
                   π^{−∑ᵢ sᵢ(sᵢ−1)} E₀([],w').

Indeed, forming the block of size sᵢ costs sᵢ steps, and 2∑_{u=2}^{sᵢ}(u−1)=sᵢ(sᵢ−1). The estimate includes the case K=3 used in the branch. It applies first to the compact coefficients with the expansion and the kernel estimates above. On passing through the approximations of CutDec, corS_of_apx, γ remains fixed, and the maximum of the level-zero energies commutes with the limit since it is a finite family, if one has LimTopN and LimZeroN. The energies of all the words are not summed.

This deduction replaces, for the pointwise estimate, the passage from the germ to an invariant mean, the positive correction on the dual lattice and the Poisson summation used in CutGermMean, CutGerm and MinLeTruncatedExponential. It uses Alpoge's local data (PBK.GermD and GermDataAt), the Fourier controls of the kernel and the continuity. The realisation of those inputs for the coefficients of the theorem and the estimate of level zero by jN2, with its passage to the limit, remain obligations for a self-contained proof of (NG4).

**Cancellation on returning to the arithmetic form.** In the normalisation of the character cStd, Alpoge's tieTopXk converts the square of the amplitude of the model into orbSq by multiplying it by π^{2pc}. By definition (BigCellDensity, pc), pc counts the pairs i<j of indices situated in the same block of γ. Each block of size s contributes exactly s(s−1)/2. Since the weight qγ is the same in both models (WeightTie), for every represented point with Gpt>0 to which these identifications apply there results

    qγ orbSq(F,γ) = π^{∑ᵢ sᵢ(sᵢ−1)} E_{m₀}(γ,[]).

The empty word has normalisation one for any nn. Hence the geometric factor of the last inequality cancels **exactly** with that of the orbital measure. For example, a block of size three requires two enlargements, with factors π⁻² and π⁻⁴, and contains three pairs of indices, which contribute π⁶. On the big cell all the sizes are one and both factors equal one. On continuing the reduction for a fixed γ this scalar can be kept at each step that compares linearly energies at γ with integrated energies of the big cell; on the latter the scalar always equals one.

Moreover, in NormalizedMeasureScaling the proof of energy_identity first establishes the equality jN2=jNorm2 and only at the end relaxes it. There jN2 is the integral of the Hilbert–Schmidt square of the orbital on the big cell with its density. Once the two frames, actions and Haar measures have been identified, the same integral defines jNorm2: changing the section leaves fixed the square of the norm on the support of the density by norm_JF_eq, and outside that support the integrand is zero. Under these identifications, in the transport to WLoc1 it is not necessary to absorb into the uniform constant either of the two auxiliary powers of π that were used in wLoc1N_of. The common factor [KΛ²/(π(K−2))]^{r+1}, the spectral estimates and the control of level zero remain present.

## The level-zero energy before removing the cutoff (C, NG4)

Let β be the Gram form obtained by integrating on the big cell the product of the orbitals, linear in the first variable. For a smooth coefficient F with Alpoge's decomposition (EigStr), write F=∑ᵢhᵢ with finite sum, β(hᵢ,hⱼ)=0 for i≠j and C(y)hᵢ=eᵢ(y)hᵢ for the admissible central operators. The decay hypotheses of the decomposition ensure the existence of the pairings. Let s be the sign of the place, |s|=1. The level-zero identity expresses R(w) for the word w=(y₁,…,yₜ) as β(sC(y₁)⋯sC(yₜ)F,F). By linearity, orthogonality and positivity of Gram,
    
    R(w) = ∑ᵢ β(hᵢ,hᵢ) ∏ⱼ s eᵢ(yⱼ),       ∑ᵢ β(hᵢ,hᵢ) = β(F,F).

If |eᵢ(y)|≤P(y), with P(y)>0, it follows that |R(w)|/∏ⱼP(yⱼ)≤β(F,F), even if β(F,F)=0. For the empty word there is equality. In the model eᵢ(y)=∏ₐ(y+μᵢₐ) and |μᵢₐ−λₐ|≤c allow one to take P(y)=∏ₐ(|y+λₐ|+c) when it is positive. The geometric normalisation of the empty point is one, since nAux equals one on the empty list. Thus the constant of this inequality is exactly one for any number of summands and any admissible length. In particular, the bound of the real part required in LimBd holds without accumulating losses on taking the maximum over words.

This step writes out Alpoge's argument in CutLimBdPf (limBd_of_eigStr), Assemble of ZuckermanDuality (norm_capP_le, eigStr_of_steps) and HeadContinuityAtZeroOfStandard (ew_zero_nil). For the coefficients of the theorem one still needs the corresponding orthogonal decomposition, the comparison of β(F,F) with jN2 and the limit for F after the cutoff. In particular the multiplication by χ(G/ε) alters the eigenvalue equation. The above inequality is applied to the coefficient without cutoff; applying it directly to each truncated coefficient would leave a gap in the proof.

## The mass of a coincidence with a fixed element (C, item III)

We give the proof of i3:prop-W-loc (III), stated in annex.tex, lines 14784–14786. The result and the count by degrees come from Alpoge (StableGradedCounts/LocalEstimateThird, count, weight_le and local_estimate_third). We keep the notation of section C. Let K be the field of g elements. Let q,d be distinct odd primes, with 16qd a divisor of g−1 and max(q,d)≥16. Fix c₀∈K×. For N≥1, the mass sq of the monic, separable polynomials of degree N and of non-zero constant term in which some root shares with c₀ its prime-to-ℓ part for some prime ℓ≠g is at most

    1/(4 min(q,d)) + (1+log N)/(g−1).

In a finite cyclic group the projection onto the part of order prime to ℓ has as kernel the subgroup of order a power of ℓ. Hence a root x counts exactly when y=x/c₀ has order a power of some allowed ℓ (order one included). For an upper bound we may allow every prime. The property is invariant under Frobenius, since c₀∈K.

Let bⱼ be the number of monic irreducibles of degree j, of non-zero constant term, with some root of that class. For a marked irreducible of degree j, the mass of the polynomials that contain it is at most 1/(gʲ−1). Indeed, on removing it there remains a polynomial of degree N−j and the mass factorises. The total mass of the possible remainders is at most one by i3:lem-sqtype(b), also in degree zero by the empty product. The sum sought is therefore at most ∑_{j=1}^N bⱼ/(gʲ−1).

For j=1 we pass from K× to its cyclic quotient of order 16qd. In the three coordinates of orders 16,q,d, the image of an element of prime-power order has at most one coordinate different from the identity. This gives at most 1+15+(q−1)+(d−1)=q+d+14 possible images. The fibres are equinumerous and translating by c₀ preserves the count. With M=max(q,d) one has q+d+14≤4M, whence b₁/(g−1)≤1/(4 min(q,d)).

For j≥2, write A=g−1, H=gʲ−1 and D=H/A. The j roots of each irreducible counted are distinct in the cyclic group of order H and all of them count. If ℓ does not divide A, every element of order a power of ℓ belongs to the unique subgroup of order D. This gives at most D elements among all those primes. If ℓ divides A, write Aℓ=ℓ^vℓ(A) and jℓ=ℓ^vℓ(j). The ℓ-primary subgroup has order Aℓ jℓ. Indeed, raising an integer u≡1 modulo ℓ to an exponent prime to ℓ preserves the valuation of u−1 by the geometric sum. Raising it to ℓ increases that valuation by one by the binomial theorem; for ℓ=2 one uses u≡1 modulo 4, guaranteed by 16|g−1. Iterating proves the identity. Since A≥16 and the Aℓ are integers ≥2, ∑_{ℓ|A} Aℓ≤∏_{ℓ|A} Aℓ=A (by ab≥a+b for a,b≥2, with trivial equality between sum and product for a single factor). Counting even with repetitions, these subgroups contribute at most jA elements. Hence j bⱼ≤D+jA, and

    bⱼ/(gʲ−1) ≤ 1/[j(g−1)] + (g−1)/(gʲ−1)
                ≤ 1/[j(g−1)] + g^{1−j}.

One sums ∑_{j=2}^N 1/j≤log N and ∑_{j=2}^N g^{1−j}≤1/(g−1). Together with the term of degree one one obtains exactly (III). The proof covers N=1 by empty sums. Items (II), (IV) and (V) are distinct assertions of the same mixed proposition.

## Coincidences between roots and divisors of prescribed degree (C, items II, IV and V)

We complete i3:prop-W-loc of the annex (lines 14778–14792). We use its comparison i3:lem-sqtype(b) of the masses of types with the probabilities in the symmetric group. Item (I) is proved there and (III) was proved above.

**Item (II).** If g≥4096, the mass of the polynomials with a coincidence of prime-to-ℓ parts between two distinct roots for some prime ℓ outside B(g,N) is at most **3 log(g)/√g**. The annex asks for 23 log(g)/√g. In Alpoge's definition BB one excludes g and the primes ℓ such that for some 1≤j≤N the ℓ-primary power of Hⱼ=gʲ−1 exceeds g^{j/4}. Denote that power by Lⱼ. The projection that removes the ℓ-primary part in the multiplicative group of the field of gʲ elements has fibres of size Lⱼ and commutes with Frobenius. As in (III), the mass of the separable multiples of a marked irreducible of degree j is at most 1/Hⱼ. Two distinct marked irreducibles, of degrees j,k, cost at most 1/(Hⱼ Hₖ), by multiplicativity and by the bound one for the mass of the remainder.

If the two roots belong to one and the same irreducible of degree j, one has j≥2 and ℓ divides Hⱼ. The Frobenius orbit of the prime-to-ℓ part of any of its roots has size strictly less than j. This size divides j, hence the projection falls in one of the subfields of degree a proper divisor of j. There are at most j/2 of these degrees and each subfield has at most g^{j/2} elements. On lifting through the projection and dividing by the j roots per irreducible, there remain at most g^{j/2}Lⱼ/2 ≤ g^{3j/4}/2 irreducibles for that prime.

If the roots are of two distinct irreducibles, order their degrees as k≤j. The prime ℓ divides Hⱼ Hₖ, since otherwise both projections are the identity on those groups. Fix the irreducible of degree k and a root y₀ of it. Every coincidence with another of its roots is carried by Frobenius to a coincidence with y₀. In the field of gʲ elements there are at most Lⱼ roots whose projection is that of y₀, even if the fibre is empty. Each eligible irreducible of degree j contributes at least one root to that fibre, and the sets of roots are disjoint. Hence there are at most g^{j/4} choices. On summing over the irreducibles of degree k their weight 1/Hₖ is absorbed, since their number is at most Hₖ/k. For fixed j,k,ℓ the mass of this second class is at most g^{j/4}/Hⱼ.

To verify the constant, put u=g^{−1/4}≤1/8. One has Hⱼ⁻¹≤(8/7)g^{−j}. For each j there are at most j log(g)/log 2 prime divisors of Hⱼ, and for k≤j there are at most 2j log(g)/log 2 prime divisors of Hⱼ Hₖ. Since 1/log 2<3/2, the sum of the two classes is bounded by

    log(g) [(6/7) ∑_{j≥2} j uʲ + (24/7) ∑_{j≥1} j² u^{3j}].

The second term includes the j possibilities for k. By the differentiated geometric series, the first sum is u²(2−u)/(1−u)²≤(5/2)u² and the second is u³(1+u³)/(1−u³)³≤(5/4)u³. The quotients are increasing by their series of positive coefficients, and it suffices to evaluate at u=1/8. In total there remains at most (15/7+15/28)u² log(g)<3 log(g)/√g. This write-up follows Alpoge's separation in SquareSetMemExceptionalUnion and CrossFactorCount and keeps the factor gained by counting orbits in the first case.

**Item (IV).** Let σ be uniform in the symmetric group on N letters, and let X be the number of invariant subsets of size a, with 0<a<N. Each one has probability of being invariant equal to the inverse of the number of such subsets, so that E X=1. Count in Y the invariant subsets A of that size for which σ has a fixed point in A and another in its complement. Conditionally on A being invariant, the restrictions are independent uniform permutations. On any non-empty set, a uniform permutation has at least one fixed point with probability ≥1/2. For size m≥2 this follows from the first lower bound of inclusion-exclusion, m/m−binom(m,2)/(m(m−1))=1/2, and in size one the probability is one. Summing over A there results E Y≥1/4. If Y>0, exchanging those two fixed points gives a second invariant subset of the same size. Hence Y≤X and, where Y>0, X−1≥X/2≥Y/2. On the whole space one obtains X−1_{X>0}≥Y/2. Taking expectations there results P(X>0)≤7/8. A divisor of a separable polynomial selects some of its irreducible factors and their degrees sum to a. The comparison of types transfers the bound to the mass sq. This is Alpoge's argument of the two fixed points in IRRLemmaP, written as a double count.

**Item (V).** If N≥100 and N/2<p≤N−3 is prime, a permutation has a cycle of length p with probability 1/p. Indeed, there are binom(N,p)(p−1)!(N−p)!=N!/p pairs of a permutation and a cycle of it of that length, and each permutation has at most one. The events are also disjoint for distinct p in that interval. The number of eligible primes is π(N−3)−π(N/2). By Corollary 1, formulas (3.5) and (3.6), of Rosser and Schoenfeld, π(x)>x/log x for x≥17 and π(x)<1.25506 x/log x for x>1. Here N−3≥97, N−3≥0.97N and log(N/2)≥(5/6)log N, since N≥64. Thus,

    π(N−3)−π(N/2) > [0.97−(3/5)1.25506] N/log N > N/(5 log N).

Each prime contributes at least 1/N to the probability. Taking the complementary event and applying i3:lem-sqtype(b) one obtains the bound 1−1/(5 log N) of the annex for every N≥100. This deduction eliminates the check by finite intervals of IRRPrimeRecipSum. [Rosser and Schoenfeld, Illinois Journal of Mathematics 6 (1962), p. 69](https://doi.org/10.1215/ijm/1255631807).

With these proofs the five items of i3:prop-W-loc can be applied in the deduction of i3:prop-W. This proposition concerns the factorisation types in the finite fields of the construction. The archimedean estimates of (NG4), the automorphic realisation and the other inputs of the families theorem enter through other propositions.

Concretely, in i3:prop-W (annex.tex, 11650 and 14837) the second summand can be replaced by 3|ι| log(g)/√g. One applies (II) in each coordinate under the product law and sums the |ι| exceptional masses. In the application to i3:prop-irr-row, the balance of i3:rem-c1-status (12264–12268) identifies the other two results of formal type that accompanied this local proposition: i3:prop-counting, the count of automorphic rows with relative error, and i3:prop-allrows-built, the construction of the labelled rows in the realisation. The local count proved here presupposes neither of the two.

## From the labels to the automorphic multiplicity (C, R1–R5)

There are two distinct questions in i3:prop-allrows-built and its application to i3:prop-irr-row. The first is an exact equality between a dimension and a count with labels. The second is to approximate that dimension with uniform relative error for each selection of labels. We give the proof of the equality and make precise the scope needed for the estimate.

**The archimedean projector and the evaluation.** Let σ be the irreducible discrete series representation of the product of the archimedean groups, w the product of the chosen unit vectors, d the product of the formal degrees, with the corresponding measures. One needs the integrability of the diagonal coefficient of w; in the construction it is obtained from the decay under the condition of separation of parameters. We take the scalar product linear in the first variable. For u,v in the algebraic space V spanned by the translates of w, put

    a(v,u)(h) = d ⟨v,σ(h)u⟩.

They are integrable functions because they are finite combinations of left and right translates of the diagonal coefficient on a unimodular group. They satisfy a(v,u)* = a(u,v) for the involution of the convolution algebra and

    a(v,u) ∗ a(v′,u′) = ⟨v′,u⟩ a(v,u′).

Indeed, the integrand of the convolution at h is d²⟨v,σ(x)u⟩⟨σ(x)v′,σ(h)u′⟩; Schur orthogonality with formal degree d gives the identity. The orthogonality formula of the product is obtained first for elementary tensors by Fubini and then by linearity on V. Likewise σ(a(w,w))w=w by the same orthogonality.

Let R be the unitary representation on the automorphic space and R(a) the integrated action of the archimedean factor. Put Q=R(a(w,w)), an orthogonal projector. For any orthogonal projector E of the finite factor, the evaluation of the bounded intertwiners T of σ into E·L², given by T↦Tw, is a linear bijection onto im(EQ). Indeed, R(a(w,w))Tw=Tw, and w is cyclic by irreducibility. Conversely, given ξ∈im(EQ), define Tξ(v)=R(a(v,w))ξ on V. The convolution identity gives ‖Tξ(v)‖²=‖v‖²‖ξ‖². There is a unique bounded extension to the completion, which intertwines the two actions because the left translate by h of a(v,w) is a(σ(h)v,w). Its image is in im E by commutation, and Tξ(w)=ξ. This proves the equality of multiplicities with the dimension of the evaluation space even for finite projectors obtained by cutting a level.

**The rows with fixed label.** Fix the realisation and the three compatibilities that (AT) requires in the annex. Let H=im(EQ) for the complete projector of the level. In the finite level quotient, let zₜ be the product of the central idempotents of series at all the places of the two primes, for the tuple t. The zₜ are orthogonal projectors, have sum the unit of the quotient and commute with E and Q. On H there results H=⊕ₜHₜ, with Hₜ=zₜH. By the preceding bijection, for every set S of tuples, the number of intertwiners counted by dimension at the cut level is ∑_{t∈S}dim Hₜ. The unit of the quotient acts as the identity on H, since the projector of the level factors through that quotient.

The operator of good labels is the sum of zₜ over the good tuples, restricted to H. The row identification of (AT) takes the prescribed basis, with the conjugation included in the dictionary, to a basis of the fixed space of that operator. The conjugation preserves independence and spanning on conjugating the coefficients of any relation. The compatibility of labels places each vector of that basis in its Hₜ and ensures that its label is valid and good. Applying zₜ to an expansion in that basis one sees that the vectors of label t form a basis of Hₜ for each good t. For each remaining t we choose a basis of Hₜ and adjoin the chosen ones; the spaces corresponding to invalid labels are zero because their idempotents are. Thus the complete basis keeps each prescribed row and its label. For every S, the number of vectors with label in S is the dimension of the cut level, and those of good label are exactly the image of the initial inclusion. This proves the faithfulness, including the repeated multiplicities and the case of a summand of dimension zero. It is the obligation (R1) of i3:str-allrows under the hypotheses of realisation, finiteness and integrability indicated. The identifications are those of Alpoge's RowDictTie, LabTie, RowsBij, RowsGoodRed and AllRowsLab.

**Expected value and shape of the cut.** Write K for the compact stabiliser, L for the kernel of the level quotient, and H₀×H₁ for the quotient at the two completely split primes. The reduction of GL over each local ring is surjective: lifting the entries of an invertible residual matrix gives a unit determinant. The simultaneous reduction in the adelic stabiliser allows the local components to be chosen independently. In the coordinates of the level datum, Hⱼ is the product of the residual linear groups above gⱼ. In this paragraph Hⱼ denotes a finite group. A coefficient A of the group algebra is lifted as A(ρ(x))/vol(L) on K and as zero outside K. Each fibre has measure vol(L), so that the lift preserves the convolution product and the involution.

In the adapted frames of the level, the base idempotent is the lift of ⊗ᵥaᵥ, where aᵥ is the average by the character of J at that place. This concrete local description of the level is the result KppShape of the development. Taking S in the labels of a single prime gₖ, we sum zₜ over its tuples, with the unit at the complementary places. For the resulting idempotent eₛ, the evaluation at the identity factorises:

    eₛ(1)/e(1) = ∑_{t∈S} ∏_{v|gₖ} (aᵥ z_{v,tᵥ})(1)/aᵥ(1).

One expands the tensor product, evaluates each factor at the identity and cancels vol(L) together with all the factors of the places above the other prime. Conjugating the frames preserves the coefficient of the identity of aᵥz because z is central. Changing the non-trivial local additive character to the standard one multiplies its argument by c≠0. In the function φ_J of the datum, the superdiagonal terms, including the jump from s−1 to s+k, form two disjoint chains. In each chain one assigns diagonal weights 1,c,c²,… in order. Conjugating u by that diagonal in the form D⁻¹uD multiplies each entry of φ_J by c and preserves the conditions of zeros and of unit diagonal of J. It holds also for chains of length zero. This gives the same normalised coefficient for the standard character. Each quotient in the formula is the weight wt of the annex, non-negative because aᵥz is a self-adjoint projector and its coefficient at 1 is the squared norm of its coefficients. Since D* is volume times formal degrees times e(1), the formula proves the identity of the expected value for each cylinder with the local description indicated.

**Scope of the counting theorem used.** In annex.tex, i3:prop-counting (11589–11600) restricts the statement to an average by a character of a subgroup. In the application (11892–11897) eₛ is used for every S. In the quotient with the factors ordered as auxiliary and selected, its expression is exactly

    a_aux ⊗ (zₛ a_sel),

where a_aux and a_sel are averages by characters, and zₛ is a central idempotent. On the auxiliary factor the earlier unipotent support still holds; the multiplication by zₛ occurs entirely in the other factor. The product is idempotent and self-adjoint, and is of positive type: its matrix of values at xᵢ⁻¹xⱼ is a Gram matrix of translates of the projector. After lifting it keeps the support in K and the bi-invariance under L. Hence it is admissible in the sense of IsAdmIdem. The format with zₛ has content of its own. Already in the cyclic group of order three, the sum of two of the three idempotents of distinct characters has coefficient 2/3 at the identity, whereas an average by a single character of a subgroup has coefficient 1 or 1/3. This example distinguishes the algebraic classes; the empty selection in the datum itself gives in addition the zero projector and a zero count.

In Alpoge's source the precise application is AllRowsPCount.isCountIdem_cyl, followed by the second alternative of CountE.PCount in AllRowsAtRecord.allRows_at_record. CountConstantsOfSiegelWeil and CountTypedOfVolumeStep obtain the count with c=1/16 from the CountingTheorem for admissible projectors, in particular for these cylinders. In phase 214 the statement that must be proved on paper is thus identified. The restricted count that appears in the annex, by itself, does not justify that application. The exact equality of dimensions above does not use the asymptotic estimate.

## Unipotency and distance to the identity in the count with projectors

The input NearUnip of the above count identifies which rational elements can contribute near the compact group. One can prove the metric conclusion with distance to the identity **linear** in the Cartan radius. We use the normalisation of Alpoge's near_arch and near_unipotent (NormExpSubOneLeExpNorm and ConjPowOfMulEqOne).

Let E be a number field and γ an automorphism of a full lattice over its integers, of rank n≥1. Let p>4n be a rational prime completely split in E, and assume that the reduction of γ is unipotent at every prime above p. At each complex embedding write A for a matrix conjugate to the image of γ, in the coordinates that measure the Cartan distance. Assume that A=U exp X, with U unitary, X hermitian and Hilbert–Schmidt norm ‖X‖ at most ε, where nε≤1/4. It suffices to verify this hypothesis for one embedding of each conjugate pair, taking the conjugate matrices for the other. Then γ is unipotent and, in each of these coordinates,

    Hilbert–Schmidt ‖A−I‖ ≤ √2 exp(ε) ε ≤ 2ε.

To see this, fix 1≤j≤n. The trace of γʲ is integral: at each finite localisation the lattice admits a basis and the matrix has local integer coefficients. Reduced at a prime above p, the trace is n, because all the characteristic roots of the reduced matrix are one. The ideal (p) is the product of those distinct primes; hence bⱼ=(tr(γʲ)−n)/p is an algebraic integer. Every embedding satisfies |tr(Aʲ)−n|≤n(exp(jε)+1)<3n using operator norm ‖A‖≤exp(ε). If bⱼ were non-zero, its norm would be a non-zero integer, but all its conjugates have modulus <3n/p<1. Hence tr(γʲ)=n for j=1,…,n. Newton's identities determine in characteristic zero the characteristic polynomial from these n traces: it coincides with that of I. Cayley–Hamilton gives (γ−I)ⁿ=0.

Write xᵢ for the real eigenvalues of X. Unipotency gives tr A=n and |det A|=1; this last equality and |det U|=1 imply ∑xᵢ=0. Hence

    Hilbert–Schmidt ‖A−I‖²
       = tr(A* A) + n − 2 Re tr A
       = ∑ᵢ (exp(2xᵢ) − 1 − 2xᵢ)
       ≤ 2 exp(2ε) ∑ᵢ xᵢ² ≤ 2 exp(2ε) ε².

The inequality is Taylor's formula in each xᵢ, since |xᵢ|≤ε. For ε≤1/4 one has exp(2ε)<2. The cancellation of the linear part uses the determinant of the same matrix whose trace has just been determined.

In the adelic application the lattice is provided by the point x: one prescribes the chain of local lattices x_f Λ_v in the finite part (one per place), which coincides with the reference one except on a finite set. Its intersection in Eⁿ is a full lattice with those localisations, by approximation at a finite number of ideals, or one can directly carry out the argument of integrality and congruence place by place. The support condition of eₛ says that the finite conjugate belongs to the stabiliser and is unipotent modulo the auxiliary prime. This gives precisely the two properties of γ used above; the selection of labels at the other prime does not intervene. For ε=c/(n⁸f²), 0<c≤10⁻⁵, the new bound is in particular ≤3√(nε), the radius required in NearUnip. It is a control on each nearby term. The sum of their contributions in the automorphic quotient requires in addition estimating how many lattices possess the relevant isotropic flags; the metric conclusion alone does not estimate that sum.

The linear order is the correct one, even in U(1,1). For real t take the matrix A with rows (1+it,t) and (t,1−it). It has trace 2, determinant 1 and preserves diag(1,−1), as one verifies by multiplying. One has Hilbert–Schmidt ‖A−I‖ =2|t|. Its singular values are exp(h),exp(−h), with h≥0, and the trace of A*A is 2+4t². Hence sinh h=|t| and the Cartan radius is √2 arsinh(|t|). The quotient of the two distances tends to √2 as t tends to zero, just as the asymptotic constant of the lemma allows.

## The constant of the short flags in (C3)

In the counting proposition of the annex, (C3) asks for a bound on the measure of the points with a short isotropic flag. There are two distinct assertions: a formula that dominates the measure of each type by an explicit density, and a bound for the constant of that density. We give here the proof of the second and the integration of the density. The precise statement that this discharges is `ConstantBound` in `Statements/CuspFrameWeights/SameKindIsometric.lean`, with the normalisations of Alpoge's `Definitions/SemilocalFrames/DefClassNum.lean`. In the annex it corresponds to the [breakdown of the counting proposition](../../04-papers/46-row-d-sign-radial-intersection-form/annex.tex#L16647).

Let E be the CM field of the construction, of degree 2f, absolute discriminant d and root discriminant r=d^(1/(2f)). Let n=p+q, q²≤n and log n≥15. A type consists of positive integers j₁,…,jₐ with sum J≤q. Write S₀=1, Sₘ=∏ₗ₌₁ᵐ 2πˡ/(l−1)! and R=Resₛ₌₁ ζ_E(s). With D=2J(n−J)−∑jᵢ², Alpoge's constant is

    κ = r^(−fD/2) 2^(−f(∑jᵢ²+J)/2)
        [Sₚ S_q / (S_{p−J} S_{q−J} ∏ S_{jᵢ})]^f
        R^a (∏ᵢ ∏_{l=2}^{jᵢ} ζ_E(l)) / (∏_{M=n−2J+1}^n L_M).

Here L_M is the product of the local factors specified below. The power of two results from substituting vInv(E)=r/2 in the first two factors of the definition. The dimension written there is 4∑_{i<k}jᵢjₖ+2J(n−2J)+∑jᵢ²=D.

**Proposition.** For all these fields and types one has κ≤exp(−5Jnf log(n)/6). In particular one obtains the exponent 1/2 required in (C3), even without imposing upper bounds on r or f.

**Proof.** For J=0 all the products are empty and κ=1. For J>0 we group R with the power of r. Theorem 1(1) of Louboutin gives R≤[e log d/(2(2f−1))]^(2f−1). Since 2f/[2(2f−1)]≤1 and e log r≤r for r≥1, there results R≤r^(2f−1). Moreover D/2≥J(n−2J)≥2a. Thus r^(−fD/2)R^a≤1. The other initial factor, a power of two, is also ≤1. [S. Louboutin, *Explicit bounds for residues of Dedekind zeta functions*, Theorem 1(1)](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/S0008414X00028418)

At a prime of E of norm Q fixed by conjugation, the factor of 1/L_M is 1−(−1)^M Q^(−M/2). At the remaining ones it is (1−Q^(−M))^(1/2). Both are positive and ≤(1−Q^(−M/2))⁻¹. All the indices M that appear are ≥4. By absolute convergence, 1/L_M≤ζ_E(M/2)≤2^(2f). The last inequality follows from ζ_E(s)≤ζ(s)^(2f) by local comparison and ζ(2)<2. The same bounds each ζ_E(l) of the numerator. There are at most J factors above and 2J below. The total cost is ≤2^(6fJ).

For the J factors that come from Sₚ/S_{p−J}, write m=l−1. One has n−2√n≤m≤n−1. The inequality log(m!)≥m log m−m and the bound of the derivative of t log t on [m,n] give

    log(2πˡ/m!) ≤ −n log n + n(1+log π) + 2√n(1+log n) + log 2.

Each factor of S_q/S_{q−J} has logarithm ≤log 2+√n log π. For those of the denominator, log(1/Sⱼ)≤j² log n≤j√n log n, using (l−1)!≤n^(l−1). After summing we obtain
