    log κ/(fJ) ≤ −n log n + n(1+log π)
                  + √n(2+3 log n+log π) + 8 log 2.

If x=log n≥15, the correction divided by n is ≤2.2+(3.2+3x)e^(−x/2)+8e^(−x). The last two terms decrease on that interval and their sum at 15 is <0.05. The correction is, therefore, less than nx/6. This proves the proposition.

**Exact integral.** For ordered sizes 0<t₁<⋯<tₐ<h≤1/2, let s₀=0 and sᵢ=j₁+⋯+jᵢ. Alpoge's product of layers Lⱼ(G) multiplies, for i<k, G(tᵢ/tₖ) and G(tᵢtₖ), both to the power 2fjᵢjₖ, and for each i multiplies G(tᵢ) and G(tᵢ²) to the powers 2fjᵢ(n−2J) and fjᵢ². The density is Lⱼ(id)∏ᵢ 2fjᵢ dtᵢ/tᵢ. Since (tᵢ/tₖ)(tᵢtₖ)=tᵢ², the exponent of tᵢ in Lⱼ(id) is βᵢ=2fjᵢ(n−2sᵢ₋₁−jᵢ). Its partial sums are αᵢ=2f sᵢ(n−sᵢ)>0. Integrating from t₁ towards tₐ, each integration contributes the divisor αᵢ. There results

    Iⱼ(h) = h^[2fJ(n−J)] ∏ᵢ jᵢ/[sᵢ(n−sᵢ)].

Assume now the measure inequality of (C3): for every measurable part of the simplex, the probability of the type with sizes in that part is ≤κ times its density integral. If every non-empty short flag possesses one of these types, its probability is ≤∑ⱼ κ Iⱼ(1/2). Each Iⱼ(1/2)≤1. There are exactly 2^(J−1) compositions of J, because one can cut at any subset of the J−1 gaps. Putting u=exp(−5nf log(n)/6), the result is ≤u/(1−2u). In particular it is ≤exp(−nf log(n)/3), as `NonzeroFlagBound` asks. It remains to justify on paper the domination of measure: the small value of κ by itself does not prove it.

**Application to (C5).** For a non-empty type, in the moderate zone ℓ≤t₁<⋯<tₐ<1/2, Alpoge's pointwise counting bound (`PowLePowRatio.near_count`, theorem `GlobalTheory.NearCount.near_count`) is Lⱼ(G) with G(v)=1+12δ/v, where 0<δ<1/4. Each nearby summand of the kernel has modulus ≤M, where M is the pointwise contribution of the identity. We use here as inputs the pointwise counting bound, that of the coefficients of the kernel and the measure inequality above. The latter passes from indicators to positive integrands by monotone approximation. After multiplying by the density there appears the cancellation

    Lⱼ(G)Lⱼ(id) = Lⱼ(v ↦ v+12δ) ≤ (1+12δ)^(fD) ≤ exp(24δfnJ).

All the arguments of G are in (0,1]. If δfnq≤1/100, the last factor is ≤2. The volume of the ordered region for ∏ dtᵢ/tᵢ is [log(1/(2ℓ))]^a/a!, by symmetry in the variables log tᵢ. Let z=2f log(1/(2ℓ)). The contribution of the type, for the probability measure, is then ≤2M u^J z^a ∏jᵢ/a!. On extending the sum to all the non-empty compositions, the total upper limit is

    2M [exp(z u/(1−u)²) − 1],

since ∑_{j≥1} j u^j=u/(1−u)². Here one sums the types that can have nearby terms different from the identity. The assertion that outside those types only the identity contributes is an additional property of the lattices.

In the application ℓ=(nfrgb)^(−11q) with q≥1, b≥2, 1≤g≤b^A and A fixed. Put H=nf log n. The hypotheses log r≤e^n, log f≤√n and log b≤exp(H/16) imply z≤exp(H/8) for all sufficiently large n, uniformly in the other parameters. Indeed, z≤22fq[log n+log f+log r+(1+A)log b]; for log n≥16 each of the four summands is ≤(1+A)exp(H/16) if A≥0, and log(88(1+A)fq)=o(H) uniformly. Hence zu/(1−u)²≤2 exp(−17H/24). Using exp(v)−1≤2v for 0≤v≤1 one obtains the bound 8M exp(−17H/24)≤M exp(−2H/3) for H sufficiently large.

The estimate of κ and the formula for Iⱼ are written proofs of the numerical and analytic computation that the count uses. To convert them into (C3) in the automorphic quotient the geometric inequality `FlagMeasureBound` is needed; for the nearby term of (C5) one needs in addition the pointwise count and the identification of the types that contribute. The far and deep terms of the same item have their own estimates.

**Where the geometry of (C3) enters.** One can make precise which inequality is needed before normalising. Denote by Vₙ the volume of the quotient in the Riemannian measure of the source, with the finite compact stabiliser of volume one. Let Vₘ be the volume of the complement of signature (p−J,q−J), where m=n−2J. Let Vₙ(B) be the volume of the points of type j with sizes in the Borel set B. In these normalisations, `UnfoldBound` asserts

    Vₙ(B) ≤ c_Iw^f r^(fD/2) Vₘ
             (∏ᵢ 2^(f jᵢ²) V_GL(jᵢ)) ∫_B dens,

where, with Uₖ=2^[k(k−1)/2] Sₖ, one has
c_Iw=2^(−D/2)UₚU_q/[U_{p−J}U_{q−J} ∏ᵢ(U_{jᵢ}2^(jᵢ²/2))].
The formula for the linear volume with the normalised determinant is
V_GL(j)=R(r/2)^(fj²)∏_{l=2}^j ζ_E(l). To convert the ratio of masses of (C0) into a ratio of volumes one multiplies in the step of rank M by (U_M/U_{M−1})^f. The quotient U_M/U_{M−1}=2^Mπ^M/(M−1)! cancels the factorial and the power of π of the ratio of masses. Hence

    Vₙ/Vₘ = ∏_{M=m+1}^n [r^[f(M−1/2)] L_M]
           = r^[f(n²−m²)/2] ∏_{M=m+1}^n L_M.

Dividing the inequality by Vₙ and substituting V_GL, the power of r has exponent f[D/2+∑jᵢ²−(n²−m²)/2]=−fD/2. On expanding Uₖ in c_Iw, the remaining power of two is −f(∑jᵢ²+J)/2. Exactly κ remains. This computation identifies the geometric obligation without any implicit comparison constant: the volume of the saturated flags of a type, with the factor c_Iw indicated, must satisfy the first inequality for **every** B. The source treats it in `IsotropicFlagUnfoldingBound.lean`, by means of classes of flags and volumes of their stabilisers; `IsotropicFlagProbabilityBoundOfVolumeRatio.lean` combines it with the ratio of volumes and the formula for the linear volume.

## Measure of the saturated isotropic flags

We follow Alpoge's count of flags in (C3). A choice of archimedean origin adapted to **each class of flag** allows the measure to be computed before imposing the Harder–Narasimhan condition. We use the notations of the previous section. The field E is CM and E/F is unramified at every finite prime. We put O=O_E, d_F=|disc F|, Δ=2^(−f)√d_E=(r/2)^f. At each real place of F we use an embedding of E in ℂ and the Hilbert–Schmidt norm for the measures of the groups. A positive metric compatible with the hermitian form is one for which the operator that represents the form is a self-adjoint involution. For a projective O-lattice A of rank h with those metrics, we write H(A)=covol(A)/Δ^h in its real space. The height of a quotient uses the quotient metric. Its size is H(A)^(1/(2fh)).

**Proposition.** In the adelic measure of the volume Vₙ, the integral of the *number* of saturated totally isotropic flags of jumps j and sizes in a Borel set B of the simplex is bounded by

    c_Iw^f r^(fD/2) Vₘ (∏ᵢ 2^(f jᵢ²) V_GL(jᵢ)) ∫_B dens,       m=n−2J.

Here Vₘ is the volume of the self-dual genus of the complement, with the same convention of measure. If there is no rational flag of the given type, the integral is zero. For a test that counts only the short canonical flag, the same inequality follows by pointwise domination.

**The classes of flags.** The finite points, modulo the compact stabiliser of volume one, represent lattices of the genus. After taking the quotient by the rational group, each class of lattices L gives an integral over Γ_L∖G∞, where Γ_L is its group of integral isometries. This is seen by decomposing into finite double classes: the translates of the finite fibre have measure one and the rational stabiliser of the fibre acts on the infinite factor. For a flag S of L, on summing the translates of a fundamental domain of Γ_L by representatives of Γ_S∖Γ_L one obtains a fundamental domain of its stabiliser Γ_S. Tonelli allows the sum even though the number of flags is not bounded point by point. One obtains integrals over Γ_S∖G∞, one per class of pairs (L,S). The lattices and the rational flags form countable sets, and the heights of each fixed flag depend continuously on the metric.

The class of a pair is determined by the projectives Pᵢ=Sᵢ/Sᵢ₋₁ and the hermitian lattice C=Sₐ^⊥/Sₐ. Indeed, the torsion-free quotients over O are projective and we can write Sₐ=P=⊕Pᵢ adapted to the steps. The self-duality of L and the fact that P is a direct summand give a surjection L→P† (conjugate dual), with integral section s. The form induced on s(P†) is represented by a hermitian map B:P†→P through the perfect duality between P and P†. There exists θ∈O with θ+θ̄=1: the trace O→O_F is surjective at each finite localisation, by reduction to the separable extension of residue fields in the inert case and by projection in the split case; hence its image ideal contains 1. Replacing s by s−θB the form vanishes on its image, since the two corrections sum to −(θ+θ̄)B. Thus P and the new image span a self-dual hyperbolic summand. The orthogonal projection onto this summand is integral. Its complement is isometric to C. We obtain L≃H(P)⊥C, with the steps identified, where H(P)=P⊕P† with the hyperbolic pairing. The isometries of C and the isomorphisms of the Pᵢ extend, acting on the duals by the inverse adjoint. Hence each tuple of classes contributes at most one class of pairs.

All the C thus obtained lie in one and the same rational space by Witt cancellation, since the hyperbolic spaces over E of dimension 2J are isometric. Their lattices are locally isometric. At an inert place a unimodular hermitian form has a vector of unit norm, since its non-degenerate hermitian reduction over the finite field possesses a vector of non-zero norm (if all were zero, polarisation with all the scalars and the non-degeneracy of the residual trace would give the zero form). One splits off the vector orthogonally and iterates. All the units of the base field are norms: one lifts a residual solution of the norm equation correcting at each power of the uniformiser by means of the residual trace. Thus the unit diagonals are normalised to ones, also in residue characteristic two. At the split place one is dealing with two free modules in perfect duality. This proves the assertion on the genus. Any other space of the datum of equal signature with self-dual lattice has the same local forms; the hermitian local-global principle (Landherr, the theorem cited in the annex, together with Hasse's norm theorem for E/F) identifies its rational space. In particular the volume of the genus is the Vₘ that appears in the ratio of volumes.

**Origin and unipotent radical.** In a class (L,S) fix the above decomposition. Choose a pseudobasis of each Pᵢ, that is, a sum of lines of fractional ideals 𝔞 e. Put on those lines orthogonal metrics with ∏ᵥ‖e‖ᵥ²=N_E(𝔞)⁻¹, on the dual lines the dual metrics and on C a compatible metric. It is an admissible origin of the space of compatible metrics. Changing origin is equivalent to translating the variable in the real group; its Haar measure is bi-invariant. The reference metric on the group is transported by the isometric identification between models. At this origin all the Pᵢ have height one. Also H(C)=1: for any metrised projective A duality inverts H, as is seen in a pseudobasis by the inverse determinant of the metric and the conjugate inverse ideals. The form of C induces an integral isometry C→C† by compatibility. The same argument holds for any intermediate self-dual block W.

Let us compute the covolume of the integral radical Γ_N in the real radical N for the measures induced by Hilbert–Schmidt at the chosen origin. At the first step write L=P₁⊕W⊕P₁† and j=rank P₁, k=rank W. The radical of this single step consists of the unipotent triangular matrices with blocks (1,2)=x, (2,3)=−H_W⁻¹x*, (1,3)=y, where H_W represents the form of W and

    y+y* = −x H_W⁻¹ x*.

Here * in the coordinates of the dual pairs is the conjugate adjoint. For each integral x in Hom_O(W,P₁) there is an integral y: take −θ x H_W⁻¹ x*, using the self-duality of W. The fibre of all the y is a translate of the lattice of antihermitian maps P₁†→P₁. The multiplication law adds the x, and for a fixed x translates the central block. In coordinates (x,z) with z=y+θxH_W⁻¹x* left multiplication has Jacobian one (block triangular). By Fubini the covolume is the product of the two additive covolumes with the corresponding tangent metrics.

The lattice Hom_O(W,P₁) has Euclidean covolume Δ^(jk)H(P₁)^k/H(W)^j=Δ^(jk). The formula is obtained by writing both modules in pseudobases: the norms of the ideals multiply as in the tensor P₁⊗W^∨ and the metric determinant has the same powers. In the Lie algebra there appear x and its adjoint block. In orthonormal frames H_W is an isometry, so that the square of the norm is doubled. The volume factor is 2^(fjk), giving r^(fjk) for the first block.

For the central fibre, a pair of distinct indices u<v contributes the ideal 𝔞ᵤ 𝔞̄ᵥ in one complex entry and its conjugate opposite in the other. The choice of metrics cancels the norms of the ideals; per independent entry the covolume is Δ. The metric doubling gives a factor 2^f, hence r^f per pair. On a diagonal there appears 𝔞ᵤ 𝔞̄ᵤ=N_{E/F}(𝔞ᵤ)O and one takes its anti-invariant part. In the f imaginary coordinates the lattice O⁻={b∈O:b̄=−b} has covolume √d_F. Indeed, O=θ O_F⊕O⁻ by the trace. The projection onto the real coordinates sends θ O_F onto (1/2)O_F, with covolume 2^(−f)√d_F. On dividing Δ by this there results √(d_E/d_F)=√d_F, since the relative different is trivial. Multiplying by the ideal of F multiplies the covolume by N_E(𝔞ᵤ), which cancels with ∏ᵥ‖eᵤ‖ᵥ². The diagonal factor is then √d_F=r^(f/2). Altogether the central block gives r^(f j²/2).

The radical of the whole flag is the semidirect product of the radical of this step by that of the remaining flag in W. The decomposition holds integrally because the action on W is lifted by blocks. The two Lie algebras are orthogonal in the chosen metric, and the action of the second radical on the first is unipotent, of determinant one. By induction, its exact covolume is

    covol(Γ_N,N) = ∏ᵢ r^[f(2jᵢ(n−2sᵢ)+jᵢ²)/2] = r^(fD/2).

This computation at a different origin for each pair avoids an estimate of the heights of lattices of matrices in an arbitrary frame.

**Integration over the stabiliser of a flag.** At each real place use the decomposition G=NMK associated with the origin, where M=∏GL(jᵢ,ℂ)×U(p−J,q−J) and K=U(p)×U(q). To see it, K is transitive on the isotropic complex flags of these ranks: an isotropic subspace is the graph of an isometry between its projections in the positive and negative parts; one extends adapted orthonormal bases in both parts. The stabiliser of the flag is NM by triangular elimination. In the order nmk the measure is

    c_Iw |det_R Ad(m)|_𝔫⁻¹ dn dm dk,

with dk a probability measure and dn, dm the measures of the tangent metrics. The dependence on m is obtained by translating on the left by M and using the Jacobian of conjugation on N. To check the constant, put 𝔩=Lie(K∩M). If X runs through an orthonormal basis of 𝔫, the vectors (X−X*)/√2 give an orthonormal basis of 𝔨⊖𝔩: the strictly triangular blocks and their adjoints have disjoint supports; the triangular decomposition of an antihermitian matrix in 𝔨 gives the surjectivity. In the differential of the multiplication there is for each X a pair of unit vectors of scalar product 1/√2. The other directions are orthogonal to these pairs. The Jacobian is 2^(−D/2). The passage from the quotient measure of K/(K∩M) to dk contributes vol(K)/vol(K∩M). In the block U(jᵢ) of the intersection the quadratic metric is doubled because one acts also on the dual. For this reason vol(K∩M)=U_{p−J}U_{q−J}∏ᵢ 2^(jᵢ²/2)U_{jᵢ}. Finally vol U(k)=Uₖ: the fibration by the last column over the unit sphere doubles the square of the metric in the 2k−2 directions transversal to its phase and preserves the metric in the phase. The recurrence of volumes has ratio 2^(k−1)·2π^k/(k−1)!, with initial volume one. One recovers exactly the earlier c_Iw.

Write Aᵢᵥ for the GL block of the Levi element and dᵢ=∏ᵥ|det Aᵢᵥ|². The size of the quotient Pᵢ at the point nmk is tᵢ=dᵢ^(−1/(2fjᵢ)); the inverse comes from measuring the lattice with the action of g⁻¹. The radical induces the identity on each graded piece and K preserves the norms. The product of the inverse moduli is Lⱼ(id)(t). Indeed, at a step (j,k), the action on x is x↦AxB⁻¹, where B acts on W with determinant of modulus one (it is a hermitian isometry). Its real determinant is |det A|^(2k). The action on the centre is y↦AyA*, with real determinant |det A|^(2j), as is checked in singular values of A by counting the diagonals and the pairs of entries. The other blocks in W are treated by induction. There results ∏ᵢ dᵢ^(−(n−2sᵢ+jᵢ))=∏ᵢ tᵢ^βᵢ, the monomial density computed above.

The image of Γ_S in M consists precisely of the automorphisms of the Pᵢ and the integral isometries of C. Every tuple is lifted by block diagonal matrices in the integral splitting, and the kernel is Γ_N. Conjugation by these automorphisms preserves the lattice of the radical and its measure. We can integrate first over Γ_N∖N, then over the Levi part modulo this product of groups and finally over K. This is obtained by choosing successive fundamental domains in N and in M; the compact ambiguity of the decomposition is K∩M, already included in c_Iw. Equivalently one applies the above integration to a function on G whose sum over Γ_S is the lifted indicator and unfolds the sum in the two fibres. In the blocks GL(jᵢ,ℂ), the Lie metric repeats each entry in the dual block and for this reason the measure is 2^(f jᵢ²) times ∏ᵥ dAᵢᵥ/|det Aᵢᵥ|^(2jᵢ), with dA ordinary complex Lebesgue measure. In each GL quotient the determinants of the integral automorphisms have global norm one. The measure induced on the line log dᵢ is invariant under translations, since multiplication by positive scalars realises all of them and preserves Haar measure. Its constant is precisely the volume of the slab 0≤log dᵢ<1, called slabVol(Pᵢ). Thus |d log dᵢ|=2fjᵢ dtᵢ/tᵢ. These observations give, for a class of pairs, the product of the integral of dens, the factors of c_Iw and of the radical, the volume of the quotient of C, and ∏ᵢ 2^(fjᵢ²)slabVol(Pᵢ). On summing over classes, the injection into the tuples allows one to sum over their complete Cartesian product. The sums of slabs give V_GL(jᵢ) by definition; the sum for C gives Vₘ by the same decomposition into finite double classes. This proves the proposition for every Borel set B.

**Normalisation of the linear volume.** One can use the classical theorem τ(SLⱼ/E)=1 for j≥2. With a generator of the integral invariant forms on SLⱼ, the finite measure, in additive coordinates of volume one on O_v, gives the compact group the volume ∏_{l=2}^j(1−q_v^(−l)): the reduction is smooth and |SLⱼ(𝔽_q)|/q^(j²−1) has that value. With ordinary complex Lebesgue measure at infinity, the product of these measures is converted into the Tamagawa measure by multiplying by Δ^(−(j²−1)), the factor of the additive quotient for each coordinate. On making all the finite compact groups of volume one, the covolume of SLⱼ becomes Δ^(j²−1)∏_{l=2}^j ζ_E(l). The determinant splits GLⱼ over GL₁ by a↦diag(a,1,…,1), both rationally and locally, and respects the finite compact groups. The invariant form dA/det(A)^j decomposes into the form of SLⱼ and da/a: at the identity a diagonal entry is the coordinate of the trace of the differential, with Jacobian one; translations give the equality on the whole group. The diagonal action on the fibre SL has adjoint determinant one. The slab of the adelic modulus of logarithmic length one decomposes by classes of lattices as in the definition of V_GL; in each class the determinant of its finite representative only translates the interval and preserves its length. The volume of the slab of GL₁ is ΔR. Indeed, there are h_E ideal classes; with uᵥ=log|aᵥ|² the complex measure gives 2^(−f)∏duᵥ darg(aᵥ), the angular quotient has the factor (2π)^f/w_E by the roots of unity and the parallelotope of units in the first f−1 logarithms has volume Reg_E. One completes the remaining coordinate with ∑uᵥ in an interval of length one, of Jacobian one. Thus one obtains 2^(−f)(2π)^f h_E Reg_E/w_E=ΔR by the analytic class number formula. Multiplying the two volumes gives Δ^(j²)R∏_{l=2}^j ζ_E(l), as required, also for j=1. [Tamagawa number theorem, Kottwitz](https://annals.math.princeton.edu/1988/127-3/p05)

In particular the geometric inequality previously pending is proved for the count of saturated isotropic flags, with all its factors and with the ratio of volumes of (C0) explicitly separated. The corresponding formal development goes from `ExistsAdaptedBasisSaturatedIsotropicFlag` to `IsotropicFlagUnfoldingBound`. The above proof uses in its place an integral splitting by trace one and an archimedean origin with the lines of ideals of height one.

**The canonical flag and a bound that uses a single step.** To complete the passage from the counted flags to the points of (C3), we describe the filtration. In any metrised O-projective lattice we take, among the non-zero sublattices of minimal size, a saturated one of maximal rank. It exists: saturating decreases the height by the index; moreover, in each rank, a covolume bound allows only finitely many saturated sublattices. In fact an integral basis of the sublattice gives, up to sign, a primitive vector of the exterior lattice over ℤ of the ambient space, whose norm is that covolume. In a Euclidean lattice there are only finitely many vectors of bounded norm. One repeats in the quotient by the first sublattice, with the orthogonal quotient metric. The heights multiply in exact sequences with those metrics by the triangular determinant. The successive sizes tᵢ grow strictly: a next block of size ≤tᵢ would lift, in the ambient lattice of that step, to a sublattice of size ≤tᵢ and of greater rank. Each block is semistable in the sense that every sublattice has size ≥tᵢ; by multiplicativity every torsion-free quotient of positive rank of the block has size ≤tᵢ.

Let Sᵢ be the steps constructed. Every torsion-free quotient Q of Sᵢ, with its quotient metric, has size ≤tᵢ. To see this, one filters Q by the saturations of the images of the Sₕ with h≤i. Each non-zero graded piece receives, with finite index, the image of a quotient of the block h under a contraction at each infinite place. The contraction is obtained by passing to quotient norms in the projection Sᵢ→Q and then dividing by the previous image. Its determinant has modulus ≤1 in orthonormal bases on the image; saturation reduces the covolume of the latter. Thus the size of each graded piece is ≤tₕ, and the assertion follows by multiplying heights. Conversely, if a sublattice A has all its quotients of size ≤t and the next block of L/Sᵢ has size >t, the projection A→L/Sᵢ is zero: its non-zero image would have size ≤t by contraction and at the same time ≥tᵢ₊₁ by the definition of the next minimum. This characterises Sᵢ for tᵢ≤t<tᵢ₊₁ as the sum of all the A whose quotients have size ≤t (with the conventions at the endpoints). The strict version is obtained by taking the union of the steps for t'<t; the maximal size of the quotients of any A is finite and is attained by duality with the minimal size in A†. It therefore agrees with stepLe and stepLt in the source and gives uniqueness of the flag. In a fixed class, all the necessary comparisons are made between the continuous heights of a countable family of rational sublattices. This also gives the measurability of the type conditions.

If S is the last step of size <1/2, every non-zero quotient of it has size <1/2. In its conjugate dual S† every non-zero sublattice has size >2: for a saturated one the inversion of the height is applied to its dual quotient of S, and the index only increases the size for the remaining ones. The hermitian form of L restricted to S gives an integral map S→S† of norm ≤1 at each place, by compatibility of the metric and Cauchy–Schwarz. A non-zero image would have simultaneously size <1/2 and >2. Hence S is totally isotropic and has rank ≤q. In particular the type event of (C3) is dominated by the number of flags of the proposition. With the ratio of volumes of (C0) there thus results the normalised inequality for all the types and all the Borel sets of the simplex.

For the event without prescribed type **the first minimum** suffices. If the minimal non-zero size is ≤h, with 0<h≤1/2, its first block P is semistable of size ≤h; the same argument applied to P proves its isotropy. Hence it suffices to count, for each j=1,…,q, the saturated totally isotropic sublattices of rank j and size ≤h. There is no need to count chains. The formula for a single step gives I_{(j)}(h)=h^[2fj(n−j)]/(n−j). At the endpoint t=h the measure is zero by the unfolded formula, which also holds for Borel sets in the positive orthant by the same proof. If u=exp(−5nf log(n)/6), we obtain, uniformly in h,

    Prob(minimal size ≤h) ≤ ∑_{j=1}^q u^j h^[2fj(n−j)]/(n−j)
                         ≤ u h^[2f(n−1)] / [(n−q)(1−u)].

The last inequality uses j(n−j)≥n−1 for 1≤j≤q<n. For q=0 the sum is empty. On taking h=1/2 one obtains in particular the rarity estimate of (C3). The dependence on h controls how small the region of the lattices with a very short minimum is. Integrating over that region a kernel that grows requires in addition a bound for the kernel itself.

## Pointwise count of the near terms of (C5)

We give the packing argument that completes the preceding moderate application. At a point x of the quotient let L be its Hermitian lattice with the compatible metrics of x. In each embedding we assume operator norm ‖γ−1‖≤δ for the integral isometries considered, where 0<δ<1/4. This hypothesis follows from the Hilbert–Schmidt bound used in nearSet. The short blocks of the canonical filtration have sizes t₁<⋯<tₐ<1/2. Then

    #{γ} ≤ Lⱼ(v ↦ 1+8δ/v).

The identity is included. In the absence of short blocks the only isometry that contributes is the identity.

**Proof.** The complete canonical filtration is identified by duality with the filtration of the orthogonals in reverse order. Indeed, the dual of a semistable block of size t is semistable of size 1/t, by the correspondence between saturated sublattices of the dual and quotients of the original. The dual of the exact sequence, with the induced metrics, is exact and isometric on each graded piece. The reversed filtration again has strictly increasing sizes and semistable blocks; the characterisation by bounds over all quotients proved above gives its uniqueness. The form of L identifies it isometrically with its conjugate dual. We keep the short blocks, their duals and a single central block W that gathers the sizes in [1/2,2]. In the order of the filtration the graded pieces are P₁,…,Pₐ,W,Pₐ†,…,P₁†. The central one has rank n−2J. In what follows, in each of these blocks we call l the smallest of its canonical sizes and u the largest; for W it suffices to use l=1/2, u=2.

We shall use that a non-zero integral map from a lattice whose quotients have size ≤u to another whose sublattices have size ≥l satisfies maxᵥ‖Aᵥ‖≥l/u. Indeed, if the map has rank k and maximal norm c, its determinant on the quotient by the kernel multiplies the height by at most c^(2fk). The image in the second lattice has size ≥l and the quotient of the first has size ≤u, which gives the inequality. The estimate also holds on taking the restrictions and orthogonal projections that define the graded pieces.

Write F_b for the filtered sum up to the block b. The map γ−1 carries F_b into F_{b−1}: its composition with L→L/F_{b−1} has norm ≤δ, but any non-zero integral map between these lattices has norm ≥l_b/u_b. This ratio is 1 for the semistable blocks and at least 1/4 for the central one. The size bounds used here are deduced from the extremes of the filtration, applying the estimate by graded pieces of the preceding subsection, and by duality for the smallest size in a quotient. Thus γ−1 descends at least one step. If a=0 this already proves the claim.

Let us now count the possibilities in order of superdiagonals in the triangular matrix of γ−1, beginning with the one that descends one step. An orthogonal splitting can be chosen in each embedding: the unitary involution of the metric that represents the form exchanges a graded piece with its dual block and preserves the central one, because it carries the Hermitian orthogonals of the flag to their Euclidean orthogonals. In a fibre where the first h−1 superdiagonals are already fixed, the difference Δγ of two matrices descends at least h steps. On its superdiagonal h the induced components are integral maps on the graded pieces, independent of the splitting. Moreover this superdiagonal of the difference is anti-adjoint for the form of L. Indeed, subtracting the identities (1+B)*H(1+B)=H for B=γ−1 and B'=γ'−1, one obtains that the sum of Δγ and its Hermitian adjoint consists of products of a difference of order h and a factor of order ≥1. The Hermitian adjoint preserves the order of descent, since it exchanges blocks by reflecting their order. The products descend at least h+1 steps.

For each pair of components exchanged by this adjoint we keep one. In a component fixed by the reflection, the differences are anti-Hermitian; its real space of dimension fj² corresponds to the block P†→P, with j=rank P. In a free component with block sizes k and j the real dimension is 2fkj. Each component of γ−1 has maximal norm ≤δ by orthogonal projection. In the fibre the differences of distinct values are integral and, by the preceding separation lemma, have norm ≥l_target/u_source=:b. Translating by one of the values, the possibilities lie in the ball of radius 2δ of their space of differences. The balls of radius b/2 around these values have disjoint interiors and lie in the ball of radius 2δ+b/2. Comparison of volumes in real dimension d gives at most (1+4δ/b)^d values. The proof also applies to the anti-Hermitian space with the induced norm.

The free components are Pₖ→Pᵢ for i<k with b=tᵢ/tₖ, Pₖ†→Pᵢ for i<k with b=tᵢtₖ, and W→Pᵢ with b≥tᵢ/2. The fixed ones are Pᵢ†→Pᵢ with b=tᵢ². Their dimensions are respectively 2fjᵢjₖ, 2fjᵢjₖ, 2fjᵢ(n−2J) and fjᵢ². On each superdiagonal, the retained components determine the remaining ones once the earlier ones are fixed, by the anti-adjointness of the **differences**. Multiplying the bounds by components and then by superdiagonals yields the announced product. This reasoning proves finiteness together with the estimate, without presupposing it.

For the non-identity near terms in the moderate region one can now use, in order, unipotence and distance to the identity, this pointwise estimate, the geometric inequality of (C3) and the integral over the types. In the notation of the application to (C5), each coefficient has modulus ≤M by unitarity and positivity of type of the finite projector (Cauchy–Schwarz in each normalised matrix coefficient). Since 8≤12, the upper bound computed there is preserved, M exp(−2nf log(n)/3) in probability measure, under the parameter conditions indicated there and for sufficiently large rank. The near term of the moderate region has been treated here. The far term and the deep integral of (C5) still require their estimates.

In particular, with the Cartan radius ε=10⁻⁶ n⁻⁸ f⁻² of the count, the unipotence proof allows δ=2ε. Then δfnq≤2·10⁻⁶ n^(−13/2)/f≤1/100, using q≤√n. Thus the hypothesis that limits the product of layers to two holds for the parameters of the count.

## The far term of the count

Let n=p+q, 1≤q≤√n, p≥q, f=[F:ℚ]. We use the separation constant h, the bound b on the weights, the level g≤bᴬ and the root discriminant r. We write N=n²f, X=nfrgb, ℓ=X^(−11q), ε=10⁻⁶n⁻⁸f⁻². The coefficient of the identity is M. We reconstruct the computation of (C1) from the explicit compression formula of the minimal K-type, and the estimate of the far terms of (C5) from that computation. The compression statement, called FormulaGap in Alpoge's source, is a representation-theoretic obligation distinct from the computation of minors that follows.

**Minors and separation.** Write g=k exp Y at an infinite place, with Y Hermitian and anticommuting with the sign matrix. Let d=‖Y‖ in Hilbert–Schmidt norm. For the leading principal minor Δᵢ(exp Y), let w=e₁∧⋯∧eᵢ and A the infinitesimal action of Y on the exterior power. Then Δᵢ(exp(tY))=⟨w,exp(tA)w⟩. The spectral measure of A at w is a symmetric probability: the sign involution on the exterior power fixes the line of w and conjugates A into −A. Its second moment is Vᵢ=‖Aw‖²=∑_{l≤i<m}|Yₘₗ|², since the diagonal entries of Y are zero and distinct exterior replacements are orthogonal. The support lies in [−R,R] with R=√(2q)d: the eigenvalues of Y come in pairs ±s, at most q non-zero pairs, and those of A are sums of eigenvalues distinct by their indices. If d>0, take t=min(1,1/R). By symmetry and cosh u≥1+u²/2,

    log Δᵢ(exp(tY)) ≥ log(1+t²Vᵢ/2) ≥ t²Vᵢ/4,

since t²Vᵢ≤1. The logarithm of the Laplace transform is convex and vanishes at the origin. Hence log Δᵢ(exp Y)≥tVᵢ/4. In Alpoge's product of minors Φ each separation λᵢ−λᵢ₊₁+1 is positive. On summing with these weights, the entry |Yₘₗ|² receives the weight λₗ−λₘ+m−l≥h whenever it is non-zero, since l and m must have opposite signs. One obtains ∑ᵢ(λᵢ−λᵢ₊₁+1)Vᵢ≥hd²/2. The total determinant of exp Y is one because tr Y=0. Consequently

    |Φ(exp Y)| ≤ exp[−(h/8) min(d²,d/√(2q))].

The case d=0 is immediate. The same bound holds for every conjugation by K because it preserves the norm and the anticommutation. In the compression formula, Ψ(exp Y) is the integral of Φ(k⁻¹exp(Y)k) against dim(V) times the projection onto τ(k)w₀. The integral of these weighted projections is the identity by compact irreducibility and trace. Cauchy–Schwarz in the two rank-one coefficients bounds ‖Ψ(exp Y)‖ by the supremum of |Φ|. On using the identification of Ψ with the compression of σ, and unitarity in k, the bound of (C1) for its coefficient results. In the product over places the bounds are multiplied; at the finite place one uses |e(y)|≤e(1) and the support in the stabiliser. The proof of the identification of Ψ for every discrete series of the statement still belongs to the model step of the appendix.

**Sum.** For a moderate point x, the first size of its lattice L is τ≥ℓ. The last is τ⁻¹ by self-duality. The separation inequality for integral maps of the preceding subsection, applied to End(L), gives distance at least τ² between two distinct endomorphisms for the maximal operator norm over the f places. Every γ of the finite support preserves L. If its largest Cartan distance over the various places is d, the maximal operator norm is ≤e^d. In the real space of dimension 2N, by comparison of balls, there are at most (1+2e^d/ℓ²)^(2N) possibilities up to that distance.

Divide the far terms into the shells kε<d≤(k+1)ε, k≥1. Since ε≤1/√(2q), for d≥ε one has min(d²,d/√(2q))≥εd. The decay at the place of maximal distance suffices. The absolute sum over the shell is bounded by

    M exp[2N log(3/ℓ²)+2N(k+1)ε−khε²/8].

To check the margins, let B=(A+4)n³f log(br). The hypothesis h≥8·10¹⁴(A+4)n¹⁹f⁵ log(br) gives hε²/8≥100B. For n sufficiently large, using log f≤√n and b≥2, one has q log X≤2n+(A+1)n log(br)≤(A+4)n log(br). Hence 2N log(3/ℓ²)≤45B and 2Nε≤B. The preceding exponent is ≤(46−99k)B. The geometric series sums to at most 2e^(−53B)≤e^(−N). This proves, uniformly in moderate x, the absolute bound Me^(−N), and on integrating, the relative error e^(−N) required by FarModerate. For this sum the separation of End(L) suffices; there is no need to average over the flag types. In the compact quotient q=0 there is no far Cartan term.

## The cuspidal mass in the deep region

In (C5) one needs the integral of the diagonal over the deep set. Alpoge's development passes through a pointwise bound, Sobolev derivatives and the absolute volume of (C2). We present a proof for the integral by means of the Poincaré inequality on unipotent fibres. We keep the thresholds ℓ=X^(−11q) and N=n²f. Let 1≤q≤√n, n≥8, b≥2, g≥1, and let F=T(w) be a cuspidal automorphic vector of principal level g, where T is an isometric inclusion of the archimedean representation of the count and w a unit vector of the product of its minimal K-types. The Harish-Chandra parameters satisfy |λᵥ,ᵢ|≤b². Then

    ∫_{τ(x)<ℓ} |F(x)|² dx ≤ exp(−N) ‖F‖²₂,      τ(x)=smallest size of Lₓ.

The measure is the same on both sides. The result applies to each vector of a cuspidal frame of the projection. To identify the sum over that frame with the kernel of the count one needs the cuspidal property and the identity of the projector, independently of the estimate we prove.

**Choice of the fibre.** Order the canonical sizes t₁=τ<⋯<tₐ<1/2, and add tₐ₊₁=1/2. Since a≤q and ∏ᵢ(tᵢ/tᵢ₊₁)=2τ, there exists an i with tᵢ/tᵢ₊₁≤α=(2τ)^(1/q). Choose the first. Let P be the step up to tᵢ, of rank j, and let C=P^⊥/P. The quotients of P have size ≤s=tᵢ and the sublattices of C size ≥l=tᵢ₊₁: C is a sublattice of the quotient by P with its induced metric (in the Euclidean quotient space, the orthogonal projection of P^⊥ is computed by removing P). Moreover s²≤α/4 since s≤αl, l≤1/2 and α≤1. The unipotent radical U of the stabiliser of P acts by the identity on P, C and P†. Hence the condition of having the chosen P is constant on the whole fibre of U. In detail, the metrics of these graded pieces do not change on translating the point by U on the left. The canonical filtration is recovered by refining the three graded pieces with their filtrations and lifting the steps: the sizes of the refined blocks are the original ones and remain strictly ordered at the two junctions. The characterisation by sizes of quotients shows that the lifted filtration is still the canonical one. This holds even if the central quotient has several sizes. The choice of the first jump, and hence the partition, is rationally equivariant and measurable.

**Diameter.** For two metrised lattices A,B of ranks k,j, with sublattices of A of size ≥l and quotients of B of size ≤s, the covering radius of Hom_O(A,B) in the Euclidean sum of the complex matrix spaces, with Hilbert–Schmidt norm, satisfies

    μ(Hom_O(A,B)) ≤ fkj r s/(2l).

To prove it, use the pairing Tr_{E/ℚ} tr(VU). The integral dual of Hom_O(A,B) for this pairing is Hom_O(B,A⊗𝔇_E⁻¹), where 𝔇_E is the absolute different; this is verified entry by entry in pseudobases. In the Euclidean norm the vector corresponding to V is 2V*, because the trace counts each complex place with its conjugate. All sublattices of A⊗𝔇_E⁻¹ have size ≥l/r: tensoring by a fractional ideal I multiplies the size of each saturated sublattice by N(I)^(1/(2f)), and N(𝔇_E)=d_E. The norm estimate by quotient and image of the preceding subsection gives ‖2V*‖≥2l/(rs) for V≠0. The Euclidean transference inequality μ(Λ)λ₁(Λ*)≤dim(Λ)/2, with dim=2fkj, gives the formula. [W. Banaszczyk, *New bounds in some transference theorems in the geometry of numbers*](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01445125)

Over each finite level class, the fibre is Γ_U∖U∞, with Γ_U the rational subgroup that respects the level in the lattice of the point. In the horizontal block U/[U,U] we use the coordinates x:C→P of the three-block matrix of the preceding section (the quotient by the block centre is understood if the horizontal block is zero). The projection of Γ_U contains g Hom_O(C,P). Indeed, the integral splitting of P, C and P† already proved lifts x with y=−θ x H_C⁻¹ x*, which for x divisible by g is divisible by g². In the central block there are all the anti-Hermitian maps P†→P divisible by g. The graded blocks and their lattices are intrinsic, so that this description of the projection and of the centre also holds in the orthogonal archimedean splitting at the point, even though the two splittings differ. In the latter the norm of the horizontal part in the Lie algebra is √2 times its matrix norm and the central part keeps its own.

The horizontal error after an integral translation has norm in the Lie algebra ≤√2 gfj(n−2j)rs/(2l). One corrects the central error that remains after removing the horizontal exponential. To approximate an anti-Hermitian matrix Z by the central lattice one approximates Z/(2g) by T∈Hom_O(P†,P) and uses g(T−T*). By duality the sublattices of P† have size ≥1/s. The central cost is therefore ≤gfj²rs². The radical has two steps and its central block commutes with everything: the product of the two exponentials is traversed by two segments of one-parameter subgroups in the left-invariant metric. Each class admits a representative at distance from the origin ≤4gfn²rα. It follows that the diameter of the fibre is ≤8gfn²rα. In the deep region, α≤2X⁻¹¹ and gfn²r≤X², so it suffices to have the uniform bound

    d_fibre ≤ d₀=16 X⁻⁹.

**Inequality on each fibre.** The metric is the Hilbert–Schmidt one on the real Lie algebra conjugated to the point, extended by left translations. Its measure is proportional to Haar. The quotient is a compact connected manifold: the lattice is discrete by integrality, and the representatives constructed belong to the image of a compact set in the exponential coordinates. For s₀=dim U∞≤N its Ricci curvature satisfies Ric≥−2N. Indeed, for a nilpotent algebra with orthonormal basis Eᵢ the Koszul formula gives Ric(Y,Y)=−(1/2)∑‖[Y,Eᵢ]‖²+(1/4)∑⟨[Eᵢ,Eⱼ],Y⟩²: the Killing term and the traces of ad vanish. The norm of a commutator of matrices is ≤2‖Y‖‖Eᵢ‖ in the norm considered, also in a product of places.

On a compact connected manifold of diameter d and Ric≥−K, the first positive eigenvalue λ of the non-negative Laplacian satisfies λ≥d⁻²−K. Indeed take a real eigenfunction u of mean zero, Δu=−λu. For A>λ+K, at a maximum of |∇u|²+Au² with non-zero gradient one would have Hess u(∇u)=−Au∇u. Bochner would give there Δ(|∇u|²+Au²)/2≥(A−λ−K)|∇u|²+(A²−Aλ)u²>0. Hence the maximum occurs with zero gradient and ‖∇u‖∞≤√A‖u‖∞. Between an absolute extremum and a zero there is distance ≤d, so 1≤d√A. We pass to A↓λ+K. Since X≥16 and N≤X², 2N d₀²≤1/2; for every function of mean zero on the fibre one obtains

    ∫ |u|² ≤ 2d₀² ∫ |∇u|².

**Integration and iteration.** Smooth cuspidal automorphic functions of level g have mean zero on each fibre of this rational radical. To see this on the real components, the adelic unipotent quotient with finite level is identified with Γ_U∖U∞. The reason is that U(F) is dense in the group of finite adelic points of U: in the horizontal and central coordinates the simultaneous approximation reduces to that of the additive vector space, solving first the horizontal coordinate and then the central one. One can also use the rational coordinates of the nilpotent exponential. Hence a finite compact open subgroup gives a single double coset for the radical, and its volume only multiplies the integral of the constant term by a positive factor. Cuspidality persists on differentiating by the right action in any archimedean direction, since this commutes with the left integration of the constant term.

Let E₁,…,E_N be an orthonormal basis of the ambient Lie algebra, each element supported at one place. The gradient tangent to the fibre has squared norm ≤∑ₐ|R_{Eₐ}F|², where R is the derivation of the right action. Indeed, after trivialising the tangent bundle by left translation, the metric of the fibre is the restriction of that of the group to the conjugated subspace of the radical. To integrate this inequality the points are grouped according to the chosen step P: in each class of level lattices and rational orbit of the step one integrates over the quotient by its stabiliser, as in the unfolding of (C3). The uniqueness of the step implies that these integrals count each deep point exactly once. In the decomposition of the stabiliser by U, Levi and the real compact subgroup, the depth condition and the choice of the step are constant on the fibre. The modular factor and the level factors in the measure do not depend on the variable of U. This justifies Fubini and the fibre-by-fibre application with the same constant to any cuspidal derivative. In the notation ‖·‖_deep for L² on this region, one obtains

    ‖F‖_deep² ≤ 2d₀² ∑ₐ ‖R_{Eₐ}F‖_deep².

On iterating m times the same region is retained up to the last derivative, which can be integrated over the whole quotient.

**Cost of the derivatives.** Fix at one place the invariant form Re tr(YZ). In a Hilbert–Schmidt basis adapted to the compact/non-compact decomposition, the Casimir is Ω=∑_non-comp Y²−∑_comp Y². By unitarity, on a vector u the sum of the squares of the norms of all the derivatives is twice the compact sum minus ⟨Ωu,u⟩. The eigenvalue of Ω is |λ|²−|ρ|², so its absolute value is ≤n(b⁴+n²). On each compact type of highest weight μ the positive compact Casimir is |μ|²+2⟨μ,ρ_K⟩ in this normalisation. After at most m derivatives, all the weights have coordinates of modulus ≤b²+n+m: in the minimal type the highest weight is λ+ρ_non-comp−ρ_comp, whose corrections per coordinate are ≤n, and the weights lie in the convex hull of its compact Weyl orbit. Differentiating once gives an equivariant image of the tensor product with the adjoint, whose weights are zero or eᵢ−eⱼ. This proves the bound by induction, also if some derivatives are at other places. We use here the classical formulae for the Casimir and the identification of the minimal weight with the parameter convention of the appendix. Since |(ρ_K)ᵢ|≤n/2, in the product of places one obtains

    ∑ₐ ‖dσ(Eₐ)u‖² ≤ 5fn (b²+2n+m)² ‖u‖²

for every vector of the space generated up to that order. T is isometric and intertwines the derivatives; the types involved are K-finite and smooth. On adding one word of derivatives at a time, the sum of the squared norms of all the words of length m is bounded by [5fn(b²+2n+m)²]^m‖F‖²₂. Take m=N. Since fn≤X, b²+2n+N≤4X², the product of the cost of one step with the Poincaré constant is ≤40960 X⁻¹³≤e⁻¹. The iteration proves the stated estimate.

For a projector whose diagonal kernel is ∑_{i=1}^D |Tᵢ(w)(x)|² with a frame of isometric cuspidal intertwiners, summing the estimate gives exactly D exp(−N) for the deep integral required by DeepBound. This eliminates in that application the use of (C2) and the passage through pointwise evaluation of vectors. The identification of the diagonal with this frame for the effective projector of the appendix must be justified; the energy proof only requires that identification almost everywhere in measure. The other uses of the absolute volume outside this deduction must be examined separately.

**Application to the effective kernel.** Under the local identification of compression employed in (C1), the necessary frame is obtained by convolution with the normalised archimedean coefficient and the finite projector e. We indicate the details that allow the kernel to be restricted to the diagonal and the energy estimate to be applied. The coefficient is integrable: in Cartan coordinates the radial Jacobian at each place grows at most like a constant times exp(O(n²)d) (each root has bounded norm and the sum of multiplicities is ≤n²); the linear exponent of (C1) dominates this growth in the parameters of the count. Schur orthogonality and the evaluation of intertwiners proved in "From the labels to the automorphic multiplicity" identify the convolution with the orthogonal projector Q of rank D, using the finiteness (C4) of the appendix.

Write K(x,y)=∑_γ φ(x⁻¹γy). The series converges absolutely and uniformly on each compact set of both arguments. Indeed, the finite supports as x,y vary in compact sets are covered by a finite number of classes of compact open stabilisers. On rational matrices these conditions impose one and the same ideal denominator, trivial outside a finite set of primes. In the archimedean reference models this gives a discrete lattice of endomorphisms of real dimension 2N, with positive separation depending on the compact set. The number of matrices up to maximal Cartan radius d is O_comp(exp(2Nd)): on translating x,y in compact sets the operator norm of the matrix grows at most by a fixed factor. Likewise, for a matrix of operator norm e^s at one place the distance dHS of the translated product is ≥s−O_comp(1). We can count in shells by the maximum of the logarithms of the reference operator norms and use the linear decay h/(8√(2q))>2N of (C1); the fixed local constants are admissible in this justification of continuity. The terms are continuous, hence K is continuous. Unfolding against a continuous function of compact support on the quotient gives the convolution operator Q: the support admits representatives in a compact set of the group and the series there is absolutely convergent.

To prove cuspidality one uses the classical local theorem of Harish-Chandra: a K-finite coefficient of a discrete series, and its translates on both sides, have zero integral over the unipotent radical of any proper real parabolic. The group U(p,q) has compact centre; the finite-centre version also suffices, using the finite covering SU(p,q)×U(1)→U(p,q), whose central factor does not enter the radical. For a rational radical U associated with a non-zero isotropic subspace, the quotient U(F)∖U(𝔸) is compact (successive additive coordinates). In the integral of K(ux,y) over that quotient the sum can be interchanged with the integral by uniform convergence over compact representatives. Grouping γ by U(F)∖G(F) one obtains a sum of absolutely convergent integrals over U(𝔸), with integrand φ(x⁻¹u⁻¹γy). In each one, Fubini at a real place gives zero by the local theorem. Thus Q carries the functions of compact support to vectors of vanishing constant term. These images span its whole range: their space is dense in the range by continuity of Q and is closed by finite dimension. Each vector of the range therefore admits a continuous and cuspidal representative. [Harish-Chandra, theory of the constant term](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0022123675900348)

The evaluation of intertwiners provides an orthonormal basis Fᵢ=Tᵢ(w) of the range, with Tᵢ isometric by irreducibility and Schur's lemma (the scalar Tᵢ* Tᵢ is evaluated at w). The Fᵢ are smooth at fixed level: their distributional derivatives of all orders are in L² by intertwining and smoothness of the minimal K-type; local Sobolev regularity in charts gives smooth representatives. For every function of compact support, Q also acts with kernel ∑Fᵢ(x)conj(Fᵢ(y)). The two continuous kernels coincide: integrating them against products of two test functions gives the same pairing and every open region of the quotient has positive measure. In particular the equality holds on the diagonal. This supplies the energy argument with the effective cuspidal vectors and proves DeepBound under the same local input as the moderate count.

**Balance.** If D*=M vol is the identity term on the whole quotient, then

    |D−D*| ≤ D* [exp(−2nf log(n)/3)+exp(−N)+Prob(τ<ℓ)] + D exp(−N).

One integrates the diagonal of the projector, of integral D, over the two regions: in the moderate one the identity is removed and the near and far errors are added; in the deep one the positive diagonal and the removed identity term are bounded. By (C3) the probability is ≤u/[(n−q)(1−u)], u=exp(−5nf log(n)/6). If D*>0, using D≤D*+|D−D*| and moving the last error to the left one obtains |D/D*−1|≤exp(−nf log(n)/3) for n sufficiently large, in particular the exponent 1/16 required. If M=0 the finite projector of positive type is identically zero by Cauchy–Schwarz, and both terms are zero. In the case q=0 all the archimedean factors are compact; every term with finite support is near and its unipotent and unitary matrix is the identity by the lemma of the traces. There is no need to use the deep region in that case.

In this deduction of the counting proposition, the explicit inputs that precede the errors are the normalised ratio of volumes of (C0), the local compression formula in the separation range of the count, the support and positivity of type of the admissible idempotent, and the classical results indicated, including the finiteness (C4). The energy inequality avoids recourse to (C2) for the deep error. It does not by itself establish the compression formula for the parameters of the appendix nor the other arithmetic results that enter the families theorem.

## Identification of the compression by Sun's formula

The formula needed in (C1) is the restriction to exp(𝔭) of formula (26) of Binyong Sun for the matrix coefficient of the bottom layer of a cohomologically induced representation. We give the specialisation, since two changes intervene that affect the sign and the argument of the minors. We use the published article (Theorem 6.3, §6.3, formulae (25)–(26)); in the preliminary version the theorem has the number 6.4. [B. Sun, *Matrix coefficients of cohomologically induced representations*](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/2691743B10189C85555AD30421582301/S0010437X06002508a.pdf/matrix_coefficients_of_cohomologically_induced_representations.pdf)

Let G=U(J), J diagonal with signs ε, Gℂ=GLₙ(ℂ), K=U(p)×U(q). Take the upper triangular Borel subalgebra 𝔟 of 𝔤ℂ with diagonal torus and radical 𝔫. It is stable under the Cartan involution Ad J; the real conjugation of U(J) carries the radical to the opposite one. Its real Levi is the compact torus T=U(1)ⁿ. The positive roots are eᵢ−eⱼ with i<j, ρ is their half-sum, ρ_c corresponds to the pairs of the same sign and ρ_n to those of opposite signs. In Sun's notation the **inducing** character of T has weight ν=λ−ρ. It is integral by the condition on λ of the appendix. Cohomological induction from the Borel of the compact Cartan in the good range realises the discrete series with Harish-Chandra parameter ν+ρ=λ. The good range requires λᵢ−λⱼ>0 for i<j, which holds here. Its minimal type has weight Λ=ν+2ρ_n=λ+ρ_n−ρ_c and multiplicity one. Compact dominance is verified in i3:lem-ktype. This identifies both the representation and the extremal line used in (26); w₀ can be any unit vector of that line. For the convention that defines the parameter by the minimal type, the chamber is recovered from Λ+2ρ_c=λ+ρ: its order is that of λ, and afterwards the ρ of that chamber is subtracted. The minimal type of a discrete series has precisely this weight and is unique. [Knapp–Vogan, *Cohomological Induction and Unitary Representations*](https://www.math.stonybrook.edu/~aknapp/books/kv.html) [Atiyah–Schmid, §§8–9](https://www.math.utah.edu/~ptrapa/math-library/schmid/atiyah-schmid.pdf)

Let Y∈𝔭 be Hermitian with JYJ=−Y. In (26) put k=1, x=exp(Y/2), so that the argument of the coefficient is x²=exp Y. For l∈K, the complex quotient GLₙ(ℂ)/U(n) at the point lx is represented by the positive matrix A=(lx)(lx)*=l exp(Y)l⁻¹. The decomposition that defines H_𝔟 is lx=u d v with u upper triangular unipotent, d positive diagonal and v unitary. It exists by elimination from the lower right corner in A=u d²u*. In (25), the compact conjugation on the complex group is z↦(z*)⁻¹, so the multiplicative output is d². If Dᵢ=det A_{i:n,i:n} and Dₙ₊₁=1, triangularity gives (d²)ᵢ=Dᵢ/Dᵢ₊₁.

Let Δᵢ be the **leading** minors of A, with Δ₀=1. The identity of complementary minors gives Dᵢ=(det A)det(A⁻¹)_{1:i−1,1:i−1}. Here A⁻¹=JAJ, since l commutes with J; conjugation by the diagonal restriction of J preserves the determinant of the corner. Moreover det A=1. Hence Dᵢ=Δᵢ₋₁ and (d²)ᵢ=Δᵢ₋₁/Δᵢ, including the two extremes. Sun's character in the integrand is the product of the inducing character by the determinant of the action on 𝔫, whose weight is 2ρ. Its total weight is μ=λ+ρ. The value of the character at d² comes out as

    ∏ᵢ (Δᵢ₋₁/Δᵢ)^μᵢ = ∏ᵢ Δᵢ^(−(μᵢ−μᵢ₊₁)) = Φ_λ(A),       μₙ₊₁=0.

With τ the minimal type, formula (26) then gives the equality of operators

    P_τ σ(exp Y)|_τ = dim(τ) ∫_K Φ_λ(k⁻¹ exp(Y) k)
                                τ(k) P_{w₀} τ(k)⁻¹ dk,

by the change k=l⁻¹ in probability Haar measure. At the identity the integral is I by compact orthogonality, which checks the factor dim(τ). In U(1,1), for Y with off-diagonal entries t real and zero diagonal, the right-hand side reduces to cosh(t)^(−(λ₁−λ₂+1)); here the appearance of Y/2 in x and the inverse quotient of minors are verified directly. The preceding matrix computation also covers the interlaced orders of signs. This proves the identification employed in (C1) by appeal to a published theorem of the precise scope. By polarity and compact equivariance it determines the coefficient on all of G. Other items of i3:prop-model, such as the applications to tensors with the standard representation, have additional statements.

**Formal degree in the measure of the appendix.** Atiyah and Schmid fix in §3, between (3.10) and (3.11), the compatible measures that give volume one to the dual compact group. Theorem (8.1), with the identification of the coefficients in (8.23), gives for the semisimple group the formal degree ∏_{α>0}⟨λ,α⟩/⟨ρ,α⟩ in that normalisation. The identification of tangent spaces between the two symmetric spaces multiplies the non-compact directions by i; it is the identification of densities used in i3:rem-HC-fdeg. To pass from SU(p,q) to U(p,q) one uses the finite covering SU(p,q)×U(1)→U(p,q), and the same covering of the compact dual. The measures pushed forward are the compatible ones of the semisimple factor and the probability measure of the central one. In both quotients the degree of the covering is n, so that the compatibility of the local densities persists and the compact dual keeps mass one. An irreducible representation of U(p,q) lifts to an irreducible one of the product, with a unitary character on the central factor. The quadratic integral of its coefficient is therefore that of the semisimple factor; the scalar part of the parameter disappears on pairing with the roots. In type A one obtains ∏_{i<j}(λᵢ−λⱼ)/(j−i), exactly the compact dimension of weight λ−ρ by Weyl. This verifies the normalisation and the statement of i3:thm-HC-fdeg for its regular parameters. [Atiyah–Schmid, §§3 and 8](https://www.math.utah.edu/~ptrapa/math-library/schmid/atiyah-schmid.pdf)

## Ratio of unitary volumes by means of the determinant

We compute directly the volumes used in (C0) and (C3) and in i3:prop-massratio. Let E/F be the CM extension of the datum, unramified at all finite places, r=d_E^(1/(2f))=d_F^(1/f), and L a self-dual Hermitian lattice of rank n. Write Vₙ for the volume of U(L⊗E)(F)∖U(L⊗E)(𝔸_F) with finite stabiliser of volume one and Hilbert–Schmidt density at infinity. The group of rank one is the torus T of the elements of norm one, with volume V₁ in the same convention. Then

    Vₙ = V₁ d_F^[(n²−1)/2] ∏_{j=2}^n L_F(j,η^j),                   (1)

where η is the quadratic character of E/F. In particular the ratio Vₙ/Vₘ of the section on flags is valid for any pair of ranks n≥m≥1 and the signatures compatible with the lattices. In (1) the same V₁ serves for all of them.

**Local densities.** Put G=U(L⊗E). The symmetric bilinear form B(X,Y)=tr_E(XY) on its Lie algebra takes values in F: conjugating the trace is equivalent to taking the Hermitian adjoints of Y and X, both anti-adjoint, and the trace is cyclic. At an infinite place, the Hilbert–Schmidt scalar product is −B(θX,Y), with θX=−X*. Since θ is an involution, the density at the identity, in any rational basis e, is |det B(eᵢ,eⱼ)|_v^(1/2). This holds for every signature.

At a finite place v, the unitary model of the self-dual lattice is smooth over O_{F_v}. Indeed, after the faithful étale change that splits E/F it is identified with GLₙ; both the smoothness and the identification of the form B with the usual trace of matrices descend. The latter is a perfect pairing on integral matrices, by the matrix units. Thus the additive density that gives volume one to the integral Lie lattice is also |det B(eᵢ,eⱼ)|_v^(1/2) times the additive density of the basis e (normalised by vol(O_{F_v})=1). Denote by h_{G,v} the invariant measure with this density. By the product formula, the determinant factors of B multiplied over all places equal one; hence these local measures replace globally those of one and the same invariant rational differential form. The same fact holds for T.

If Q is the residue cardinality, the measure h_{G,v} of the compact stabiliser K_{n,v} is |G(𝔽_Q)|/Q^(n²) by smoothness and reduction: each of the residue classes has volume Q^(−n²) in integral local coordinates. At a split prime, counting ordered bases gives ∏_{j=1}^n(1−Q^(−j)). At an inert prime one obtains ∏_{j=1}^n(1−(−1)^j Q^(−j)). To see this, the number of vectors of norm one in dimension j over 𝔽_{Q²} is Q^(j−1)(Q^j−(−1)^j). By orthogonality of additive characters, their count is Q⁻¹ [Q^(2j)−(−Q)^j], since for every non-trivial additive character ψ one has ∑_z ψ(z z̄)=1+(Q+1)∑_{u≠0}ψ(u)=−Q. The orthonormal bases are counted by successive columns, since the complement of a vector of norm one is another non-degenerate Hermitian form of the lower rank; all are isometric over this finite field by diagonalisation and surjectivity of the norm. For the torus one obtains the same product in rank one. Hence

    h_{T,v}(K_{1,v}) / h_{G,v}(K_{n,v})
          = ∏_{j=2}^n (1−η(v)^j Q^(−j))⁻¹.

**Global volume.** The determinant gives G→T with kernel S=SU(L⊗E). There is a rational section as groups: one chooses a non-degenerate Hermitian line, acts on it by the scalar of the torus and by the identity on its complement. The same section is applied on the adeles, integral except at a finite set of places. Conjugation by the section on S preserves Haar measure: its determinant on the derived algebra is one (after splitting, it is conjugation on the matrices of trace zero). Hence the quotient of the volume of G by that of T is computed by integrating over S(F)∖S(𝔸_F). One can choose first a fundamental domain for the torus and then for S: the changes of rational representative in the base preserve the volume of the fibre. This disintegration is made with the finite compact subgroups normalised from the outset and avoids any unregularised product at j=1.

In detail, one chooses rational invariant forms on G and T; their quotient in the sequence of Lie algebras is an invariant form of top degree on S. With the preceding additive conventions, the product of the measures of this last form on S has covolume d_F^[(n²−1)/2]. Indeed the Tamagawa measure includes the factor d_F^[−(n²−1)/2], and the Tamagawa number of S is one, because it is semisimple simply connected for n≥2. The factors |det B| of G and T already cancel globally by the product formula. On normalising the finite compact subgroups of G and T to volume one there remains at each prime exactly the quotient of the preceding formula. Their product converges absolutely because j≥2. This proves (1); for n=1 it is an identity. [R. Kottwitz, *Tamagawa numbers*](https://annals.math.princeton.edu/1988/127-3/p05)

**Conversion to the measure of the appendix.** The ratio of (1) in ranks n and n−1 is r^[f(n−1/2)] L_F(n,ηⁿ). In compact dual measure we divide Vₙ by Uₙ^f, with Uₙ/Uₙ₋₁=2ⁿπⁿ/(n−1)! as was proved on integrating along the last column. Hence the ratio in that measure is

    [((n−1)!/πⁿ)(r/2)ⁿ]ᶠ r^(−f/2) L_F(n,ηⁿ).

It is exactly R(E,n) of the appendix. For the totally definite form, decomposing by the finite classes presents the volume as the sum of the inverses of the orders of the automorphism groups, since the infinite compact group has volume one at each place. By (1) the volume in the same measure in any other signature, with self-dual level, coincides with that mass of the same rank. The condition (VT) that identifies a number recorded in the datum with the Haar volume remains the one indicated in the appendix. Should a rational line of norm +1 or −1 be needed in (C0), the form is isometric to the diagonal model with the prescribed signs: the forms defined by the two self-dual lattices are isometric at every finite place by the unramified diagonalisation used in (C3); at infinity the signatures coincide. The Hermitian local-global principle is applied. In that model one removes a coordinate of the chosen sign and another space with self-dual lattice remains.

**The absolute volume of (C2).** Although the energy argument avoids using (C2) for the deep term, (1) also gives its statement. The finite classes of the torus are fractional ideals 𝔞 obtained from ideles of norm one, modulo the principal ones generated by elements of norm one. Indeed the finite kernel of the passage to the ideal is exactly the integral compact subgroup of the torus. All of them satisfy 𝔞 𝔞̄=O_E. The kernel of the map from this class group to Cl(E) injects into O_Fˣ/N(O_Eˣ): for 𝔞=(a) one takes the class of aā, which is a unit; if its class is trivial one changes a by a unit and obtains a generator of norm one. The quotient of units has order ≤2^f by Dirichlet's theorem, since it contains in the denominator all the squares of O_Fˣ. Consequently V₁≤(2π)^f 2^f h_E in the Hilbert–Schmidt metric, counting the stabilisers with weight ≤1. The Minkowski bound gives an integral ideal of norm ≤√d_E in each class: the factor (4/π)^f(2f)!/(2f)^(2f) is ≤1 (the first f factors of the factorial divided by (2f)^(2f) are ≤1/2). Summing the coefficients of ζ_E at s=2 one obtains h_E≤d_E ζ_E(2)≤r^(2f) 2^(2f). Finally ∏_{j=2}^n L_F(j,η^j)≤∏_{j≥2}ζ(j)^f≤exp(f), since ∑_{j≥2}log ζ(j)≤∑_{k≥2}∑_{j≥2}k^(−j)=1. Thus

    Vₙ ≤ (16πe)^f r^[f(n²+3)/2] ≤ X₀^(fn²)

if X₀≥max(2,r) and n≥5, since 16πe<2⁸. In particular one obtains Alpoge's AbsoluteVolume for n≥8.

With (1) in (C3), Sun's local identification in (C1), the classical finiteness (C4) and the conditions of support, congruence and positivity of the projector, the inputs declared in "Balance" for i3:prop-counting are covered in the unramified CM family of the construction. A negative A in CH admits no levels 1≤g≤b^A with b≥2; for A≥0 one may take C₀(A)=8·10¹⁴(A+4) and the rank threshold of that section. The estimate encompasses the label cuts that satisfy the same admissibility conditions, so it does not depend on the projector being that of a single character on a subgroup. This result controls the multiplicity of the automorphic rows; the identities of the realisation, the divisors and the Selmer groups of the families theorem have additional hypotheses.

## The eight conditions on the parameters of the constructed family

The lemma i3:lem-dataB appears among the cross-references of item C. For the family constructed in E it suffices to substitute the parameters of i4:ssec-proof-realise, step 2. We indicate the eight verifications for this application. Let 0<ε<1/52 be fixed, θ=ε/100, L=x^ε and C=max(5,C_YL+1). The size data (S), level data (P) and window data are taken with the same constants for all the members. The indices in what follows start at zero.

* (B1). By i4:prop-rd, 2v=rd_E≥2^(ν+1−2^(−ν))√d with ν≥2 and d≥7. In particular v≥2^(7/4)√7>π. For the other direction, log(2v)≤log(Q_S/2)+(log q+log d)/2. Since Q_S≤4n_F x^θ and n_F≤x^(2+8ε+θ), this is O(log x)+x^(ε/5+θ)≤L eventually.
* (B2, B3). From (S), f=2n_F≤x³, k≤x⁵ and k≥x^θ m⁵fLx^ε≥m²L for x large. Hence log(2k)≤L. The bounds on m have exponents 1/2−ε/16±θ, both between 1/4 and 1, so 44≤m≤x and m≤k. The level satisfies (kb)⁸≤Y≤gᵢ≤2Y and log Y≤C_YL L, whence log gᵢ≤CL.
* (B4, B5). The formula vᵢ=4Δ(k−2−i) gives the interval [0,4kΔ] and the exact difference 4Δ between neighbours. Δ is the ceiling of a positive power of x. For 1≤l≤k−2, the formula for the letter of the word is uₗ=vₗ₋₁−1/2−Δ·letter, with letter equal to zero or one. Thus exactly the two alternatives are obtained.
* (B6). At an upper place the total difference is a_t+b_t+k−1. One has a≤a_t≤a+a/(kx¹³), b'≤b_t≤2b' and b'/a=exp(−x^(ε/3)). Moreover 4kΔ and k grow at most polynomially, whereas w''_n grows faster than any fixed power and w''_n≤b'. Hence b_t+k−1+a/(kx¹³)≤a, a≥8kΔ and b'≥4kΔ for x large. There result simultaneously u₀−uₖ₋₁≤2a, u₀−v₀≥4kΔ and uₖ₋₁≤−4kΔ. At the other places u₀−v₀=4kΔ+1/2≤8kΔ and uₖ₋₁=−1/2=vₖ₋₂−1/2.
* (B7). In the construction bⱼ=b₀+jh₀ and wⱼ=bⱼ+h₀/2, with h₀=2⌈x^cʰ⌉ positive (cʰ is the fixed exponent of the separation). The scale estimate in (R2) of item E gives x(4kΔ+h₀)≤b₀ and b₀+mh₀≤2b. Thus 0<wⱼ−bⱼ≤bⱼ/x, wⱼ<bⱼ₊₁ where there is a successor, 4kΔ≤b₀/x and wₘ₋₁≤2b.
* (B8). The size and node conditions give bⱼ₊₁−bⱼ≥η/m≥x^θ r¹⁹f⁵L≥r¹⁹f⁵L/C. Here r=k+m−1 is the rank of the row, distinct from the root discriminant that was denoted by r in the computation of volumes.

All the preceding thresholds depend on the fixed exponents and constants of the family. In particular, the interval of the interior weights and the common extremes are obtained before choosing an individual word. The interior interlacing required in the Vandermonde comparison is also read off directly: vₗ₋₁−uₗ∈{1/2,Δ+1/2} and uₗ−vₗ≥3Δ−1/2>0. This completes the use of i3:lem-dataB for these data. With the formal degree, the ratio of masses and the identification of the initial volume (VT) in the datum, the three inputs to which the end of the written proof of i3:prop-V refers become available. The realisation of the datum with all its simultaneous identifications is a distinct obligation of item E.

## The pairing at the lower vertex of (O3)

Fix a word u and the spectral datum of vertex zero. We identify by the isometry of groups of the datum the two presentations of the ambient group G. Denote by S=N⋊H its Bessel subgroup, with H definite unitary, by K the compact open subgroup of the level of the row, by χ its unitary character and by a=xᵤ the finite representative of the word. We write [S]=S(F)∖S(𝔸). We use the measure ds of the definition of the Bessel period (product on N and H), and dν the measure transversal to the real Bessel group that appears in the datum. The action of H on N preserves the measure: in the coordinates of the roots of the radical, H is unitary, its determinants have norm one, and the fixed central coordinates have determinant one. Thus the product gives Haar. Having chosen ds∞ on S∞ which in the quotient formula with dν gives dg∞, take ds_f so that ds=ds∞ ds_f. The comparison constant in the following formula is positive and is determined by the measures:

    b(f,C) = ⟨conj(f), Pᵤ C⟩
           = cᵤ ∫ dν(y) ∫_[S] f(s(y,a)) Φ_C(s(y,a)) ds,
    cᵤ = vol_Gf(K) / vol_Sf(S_f ∩ a K a⁻¹).

Here the first variable of the inner product is antilinear, f is the row function before conjugation, PᵤC is the Poincaré series of the column C in L² and Φ_C is its summand before periodising. Assumptions of the formula: the finite support of the summand lies in S_f a K, on the right it transforms by χ, and f by χ̄. On the section s=n h, its value is conj(ψ_B(n)) C(Wᵤ(y))(h). Indeed, in the finite definition Φ_C(n_f h_f a) one takes the level factor k=1. The real equivariance Wᵤ(n∞h∞y)=ψ∞(n∞)π(h∞)Wᵤ(y) transfers the action of h∞ to C; the identification of the characters in the datum says ψ_f(n_f)ψ∞(n∞)=conj(ψ_B(n)). This is the convention of FourierCoefficientBessel, ph_colForm. In particular, the inner integral is the Bessel period of the same column against the translate of the same row. Only a enters as finite representative. The scalar cᵤ may vary with u.

**Proof of the formula and of its convergence.** For a non-negative function on G(𝔸), with finite support in S_f a K, we decompose this last double coset into the disjoint cosets s a K with s∈S_f/(S_f∩aKa⁻¹). On each coset one integrates over K by left invariance of Haar. Replacing the sum over s by the integral ds_f introduces the inverse of the volume of the intersection: the integral in k does not change under s↦s h for h in the intersection, by the change k↦(a⁻¹ha)k. At infinity one disintegrates dg∞ into ds∞ and dν, as nuQ_weil specifies. This gives the integral over s∈S(𝔸), y∈S∞∖G∞ and k∈K with the preceding density. The argument also holds if dν is written on a measurable section. For integrands invariant on the left by S(F) one first takes a cut-off on a fundamental domain of S(F) in G(𝔸) and periodises the cut-off by S(F). Since the left translates of that domain partition the ambient space up to null sets, in the disintegrated formula the sum of cut-offs over S(F) equals one; there remains s∈[S]. For the product f Φ_C the integral over K gives vol(K) because χ̄χ=1. The same reduction holds for |Φ_C|. This proof identifies the scalar even where Alpoge's development introduces it as an existential complex number.

The quotient [N] has compact representatives by unipotent coordinates; [H] has them by definiteness. Their products give compact representatives in [S]. The column ξ↦C(ξ)(h) is linear on a space of finite dimension and continuous in h, so that sup_[H]|C(ξ)(h)|≤A_C‖ξ‖ (take a basis). By the formula for |Φ_C|, the integral of its absolute value over S(F)∖G(𝔸) is bounded by cᵤ vol([S]) A_C ∫‖Wᵤ(y)‖dν(y), finite by the L¹ property of Wᵤ of the datum. The row f considered in PerLaw is bounded, so the same argument applies to f Φ_C. Finally the union of the translates of a fundamental domain for G(F) by representatives of S(F)∖G(F) is a domain for S(F); absolute convergence allows the sum that defines Pᵤ to be unfolded. This proves the equality in the inner-product convention indicated. The identification of PᵤC with its series in L² is the condition InHH of the realisation. The unfolding does not require a comparison of constants between two words.

**Choice of the row to normalise the multiplicity.** In the same word, let E be the space of columns with its spectral metric, M⊂E the image of the ramified space, d=dim E, r=dim M>0. The Gram block is Kᵤᵤ=Pᵤ*Pᵤ/c₀ with c₀=vol_N vol_H/dim V_π>0, which is a normalisation distinct from cᵤ. Put H₀=‖Kᵤᵤ−I‖²_HS. If H₀<r there exists a unit column x∈M that satisfies

    ‖Pᵤx‖² ≥ c₀ (1−√(H₀/r)) > 0.

To prove it, let x₁,…,xᵣ be an orthonormal basis of M, completed to a basis of E. Cauchy–Schwarz gives |∑ⱼ⟨xⱼ,(Kᵤᵤ−I)xⱼ⟩|≤√(r H₀). Dividing by r one obtains the bound for at least one xⱼ. In fact a column of any prescribed orthonormal basis will do. In Alpoge's source the exact proportion of M is r/d=((l−2)/(l−1))ᶠ, where l is the prime of the word: in each free orbit of the product of the f multiplicative residue groups, each factor has l−1 positions and the zero-sum condition leaves l−2 dimensions. The averages of the factors commute; in the tensor product their common kernel is the tensor of those kernels. The orbits are summed with the same multiplicities of the coefficients. This uses the freeness of the initial action with its transports. The sufficient condition for this part of (O3) is then H₀/d<((l−2)/(l−1))ᶠ. The case of equality does not guarantee any column: Kᵤᵤ equal to the projection onto M⊥ gives H₀=r.

Let us explain why the vector Pᵤx is an available row in the original normalisation. Under the conjugate archimedean identification of the models, write c for the antilinear isometry of their tensors and v for the test with cv=w (the unit line fixed in the projector of rows). If p∈HHX, the matrix-coefficient projector described in the counting section expresses p=I(w), where I is an intertwiner to the automorphic space of level K and character χ. In the notation of the datum let t=z t_S be a finite shift that verifies K=t J t⁻¹ after identifying the groups and χ(t j t⁻¹)=conj(χ_J(j)). For the smooth vector h of the input representation we define

    T(h)(g)=conj( I(c h)(g t⁻¹) ).

The double conjugation makes T linear in h. Multiplying g by j∈J gives exactly χ_J(j); the finite shift commutes with the real actions and the conjugation of models transports its equivariance. The functions are invariant on the left by G(F) and cuspidal: those of the space of I are, and right translation and conjugation preserve the constant term. In detail, the projector argument shows this for I(w); its real translates span the irreducible copy of the discrete series, and the cuspidal subspace is closed in L². At fixed level the I(c h) for h smooth have smooth representatives: locally, the Sobolev bound on the real group applies to their L² norm and the L² norms of their derivatives, which are bounded by the continuous operator I applied to the derivatives of c h. The same bound in a chart around a point gives the continuity of the evaluation in the seminorms of derivatives of the smooth factor when all the other factors are fixed. If the definition of the functional on that factor requires an algebraic linear extension to the whole Hilbert space, it is extended from the smooth vectors by a basis; the bound on derivatives is required on the smooth ones. The Sobolev order m₀ can be fixed greater than half the real dimension of the quotient, independently of the point (the local constant does depend on it). For each right derivative D, evaluating R_D I(h) at xg is equivalent to evaluating I(σ(g)dσ(D)h) at x. In the local bound up to m₀ derivatives are commuted to the right of σ(g), by means of Ad(g⁻¹). In the matrix coordinates of normInf each entry of Ad(g⁻¹) is bounded by a constant times normInf(g)². Unitarity eliminates σ(g) from the norm, giving an exponent 2m₀ common to all the D; only the constant depends on the additional derivatives of h. The central Gelfand operators act by the infinitesimal character of each discrete series. The conditions of growth and central finiteness of IsAutFunSm are thus satisfied. The decay of smooth cuspidal forms of moderate growth gives the boundedness of the row on each arithmetic component; at fixed level there is a finite number of them. [Miller–Schmid, *On the rapid decay of cuspidal automorphic forms*](https://arxiv.org/abs/1106.2149)

It results that conj(T(v)(g t))=p(g) in L². For p=Pᵤx the corresponding pairing gives b₀=‖Pᵤx‖². This is the step of AnchorPairs that avoids normalising with an unknown value of an integral of variable sign. For the choice of T the surjectivity of the evaluation suffices, at the specified translated level.

One can even fix T before choosing the word, for each finite datum in which the strict hypothesis is verified in all of them. For each u choose xᵤ as above and put pᵤ=Pᵤxᵤ in the common space HHX. In the space spanned by the pᵤ, the hyperplanes ⟨p,pᵤ⟩=0 are proper. Their finite union is not the whole space: the product of their conjugate linear forms is a non-zero polynomial and ℂ is infinite. Choosing p of unit norm outside the union and taking its row T we obtain bᵤ≠0 for every u at once. This argument only needs that the shift t and the evaluation be the same for the words, the condition of JointCondition; it allows distinct values bᵤ. The preceding quantitative bound for b₀ refers to the individual choice per word.

**How the unfolding applies to the lower descent.** The identification of columns of the adapted datum provides one and the same unitary change R for the word and a family ξ(y₀) that spans the finite type V_π. In the coordinates of BotRelTwo the reading of φ against the row T at a test (y₀,v') is χ_J(k) times the Bessel period with column C(ξ(y₀)) and row R_ℓ T(v'), where C corresponds to Rφ, R_ℓ is right translation, ℓ=(1,a)t and k∈J is fixed. The order is seen by means of the simultaneous change by the element h=a₀a' of the group of the column (a₀ corrects the frame, a' corrects the representative). The argument of the column is gh⁻¹, which returns to g; in the row one inserts emb(h)b=ℓk by the identity of the adapted datum, and the right action of k gives χ_J(k). The measure of the quotient of the period is preserved in this translation by H; the character of N is stable under H. The same phase holds for all the columns and rows of that word.

If a combination of two lower readings is zero for every (y₀,v'), we cancel χ_J(k) and extend by linearity from ξ(y₀) to all of V_π. For each y of the real transversal one takes ξ=Wᵤ(y) and replaces v' by the real translate of v by y.
