The finite factors a and t commute with y, so the row function obtained is precisely g↦T(v)(g(y,a)t), the one that occurs above for f(g)=T(v)(gt). Integrating the relation we obtain the same vanishing combination of spectral pairings, with a single factor cᵤ. This proves the implication BottomRelation under the identification of columns and the identity of the level that we have just specified. The rank of the evaluation map by itself supplies neither of those two compatibilities.

**Use of the row in the identity.** With the word fixed, denote by A=c(t') perColᵤ(t',φ,F) the period of the arithmetic column and row at the variable tuple t', and by B its spectral pairing in the translated model. The change of coordinates of columns R U is surjective: R is unitary and ‖Uφ‖=r₀‖φ‖ with r₀>0 in finite dimension. Hence the chosen spectral pair (T,C₀) of value b₀≠0 has an input column φ₀. The descent condition d.Rel gives for the row T a functional p on the columns. Write p₀=p(φ₀). The two implications of Alpoge used here are concrete: d.Rel carries every two-term relation α p₀+β A=0 to the same relation of the lower periods for all their tests; BottomRelation carries that last relation to α b₀+β B=0. With the zero arithmetic row and (α,β)=(1,0) one sees that p₀=0 would force b₀=0. With (α,β)=(A,−p₀) one obtains at each tuple

    A b₀ = p₀ B,          p₀ b₀ ≠ 0.

Thus a single proportionality factor per word is determined, even where the period of the variable tuple vanishes. In this deduction nobody divides by that period. The transport to the coordinate metrics applies the isometry RU/r₀ and absorbs r₀ into the constant. The unit property of the quotients c(t')/c(μ₀) at the service primes is an arithmetic condition of the chain and does not result from this complex normalisation.

**Scope and provenance.** The integral formula proves PerLaw for the Bessel datum with the identifications, the transformation law of the summand, the transversal measure and the membership in L² specified above. The common choice of row depends on the diagonal bounds for the realisation, and the final equality still uses the descent identity of the periods of the lifts and the link of the lower model. Alpoge's references for collating the signs and the application are HaarFiniteAdeleDisintegrationStabilizerCompact, FoldIntegralBesselQuotientSupportedFunctions, FourierCoefficientBessel, ConjugateRowPairingPoincareSeriesBesselPeriods, NonvanishingFromTruePack, AnchorPairs and MiStar2AtRealisation (anchor_word_alt). The role of the Bessel periods in the chain is also indicated in [Alpoge, remark, p. 4, point 12](https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf).

## The last two arrows and the order of the three integrals

At the end of the chain j=m−2, the hyperbolic rank of the period is zero. Let A be the definite group of the lower column, S the intermediate group and T the group of the terminal row. We write i for the inclusion of A and [A],[S],[T] for the respective rational quotients with their measures of the datum. At this end the three quotients are compact: for S the Witt decomposition without hyperbolic planes identifies it by a rational isometry with the compact group modulo its rational points of the intermediate datum. If K₁(a,s)=(ω₁(i(a),s)Φ₁)(0) and K₂(s,t)=(ω₂(s,t)Φ₂)(0), the terminal value is exactly

    ∫_[T] F(t) ∫_[S] K₂(s,t) ∫_[A] φ(a) K₁(a,s) da ds dt.

Indeed, the flag radical for rank zero consists of the identity and the character there equals one. The measure of its quotient in the definition of the period has mass one. On inserting into this period the definitions of both lifts one obtains at first the integral in the order t,s,a, from the inside outwards. The product of φ, F and both kernels is continuous and invariant under the rational points in each variable (for the kernels this is the invariance of the theta evaluation at zero). It is bounded on the product of the three quotients, of finite measure. Fubini gives the formula written without any scalar, for all the test functions admitted here. If the rank-zero isometry is used to change the presentation of [S], the measure is transported with it: this is precisely the clause VolLast, and the fundamental domains can be changed by invariance. Likewise, for a single lift Λ_Φ F(g)=∫K_Φ(g,h)F(h)dh and right translations R, one has Λ_{ω(b,b')Φ}(R_{b'} F)=R_b(Λ_Φ F) by the change of variable h↦hb'. This computation explains why the same intermediate translation has to appear in the two tests of the terminal formula.

The first partial reduction of the Weil model does not add a factor here either. Its two additive integrals are taken over spaces of matrices with one dimension zero and its unipotent average is over the trivial group. The three measures have quotient of mass one. Consequently the reduced test Ξ is exactly Φ₁ transported by the rank-zero isometries. These are Alpoge's LastLaw and flatBF_rankZero; this part requires neither the Siegel–Weil formula nor the diagonal bounds.

It still remains to identify the kernel of the terminal reading with the product of kernels K₁K₂. Alpoge's pointwise reduction is especially precise. Let V₀=A⊕L, X=T⊕L'' be the orthogonal decompositions of the spaces and κ the map between the variables of the two lines induced by the adjoint of τw, with τ*H_Lτ=−|ξ₀|²H_L''. In orthogonal coordinates the candidate is the function of two variables C(u,(v,w))=Φ₂(v) Ξ(u,κw). If one has the tensor rules of Weil on both sums and the identity on the line

    [ω_{S,L''}(s,1)(η∘κ)](0) = [ω_{L,S}(1,s)η](0),

for the splitting character χ_z at the source and χ_z⁻¹ at the target and for η(·)=Ξ(u,·), the check is direct. First one acts on (v,w) and evaluates at zero. The tensor rule at the target gives K₂(s,t) times the left-hand term of the identity on the line; this is replaced by the right-hand one. Then one acts on u by ω_{A,S}(a,s) and evaluates at zero. By the rule at the source one obtains K₂(s,t)K₁(a,s). The identity is pointwise before Fubini, so it holds inside the same pairing, with φ,F and the measures already fixed. A phase depending on s in the identity on the line would remain inside the integral: its elimination requires the specified character. The material that verifies that phase is found in WeilRepSourceLineEqOppositeTargetLine and WeilRepConjSourceLineEqTargetLine; the application to the two orthogonal sums is in LastSeeSawThetaIdentity. The present reduction proves the integrated value of the terminal pair and isolates the comparison of models that remains to be transcribed in order to identify it with the arithmetic reading. In the interior pairs the radical has positive rank and the additional measures intervene effectively.

## The phase on the common line of two lifts

This step belongs to the new part of i4:A-prop-open (O3), in the descent of periods employed for the multiplicity row. We work with the Weil data of both sides, including the splitting character of each space. The existence of the simultaneous data at the witness points is another input of (O3).

Let E/F be the CM extension and let ξ₀∈E have conjugate −ξ₀, non-zero. For Hermitian spaces P,Q with matrices Hₚ,Hq, write W(P,Q)=Hom_E(P,Q). On this space the group U(P)×U(Q) acts by (g,h)x=hxg⁻¹. The two forms of the model are

    hₚq(x,y)=tr(Hₚ⁻¹ y* Hq x),       Bₚq(x,y)=Tr_{E/F}(ξ₀ hₚq(x,y)).

Let τ from L' to L between the two lines, with τ*H_Lτ=δ |ξ₀|² H_L', δ=±1, where |ξ₀|²=ξ₀ conj(ξ₀). In the interior link the marked vector of L' is carried to the opposite of the marked one of L and their norms have ratio |ξ₀|². Since that vector is a basis, this gives δ=+1. At the end the Gram condition gives δ=−1. The rational and invertible map from W(S,L') to W(L,S) is

    κx=−ξ₀⁻¹ H_S⁻¹ x* τ* H_L.

By the cyclic rule of the trace, h_{L,S}(κx,κy)=δ tr(H_S⁻¹ x* H_L' y)=δ conj(h_{S,L'}(x,y)). On taking the trace with ξ₀ one obtains B_{L,S}(κx,κy)=−δ B_{S,L'}(x,y). Moreover κ(xs⁻¹)=sκx for s unitary. Define Jf=f∘κ if δ=−1 and Jf=conj(f∘κ) if δ=+1. Then J carries a theta test of the source to one of the target. Indeed, it preserves the quasi-periodicity law and intertwines the Heisenberg translations R_v f(w)=ψ(B(w,v)/2) f(w+v), since in the second case the conjugation changes the character into its inverse. The open level and the smoothness are transported by κ. We equip the additive quotients with measure of mass one, so that J is unitary or antiunitary, respectively.

**Operator identity.** Let χ_z be the character of L. On L' one takes χ_z⁻¹ at the end and χ_z in the interior, as the datum prescribes. For every s∈U(S)(𝔸_F) one has

    ω_{S,L'}(s,1) J = J ω_{L,S}(1,s).                                (⋆)

To prove it, both operators on the target, ω_{S,L'}(s,1) and Jω_{L,S}(1,s)J⁻¹, realise the same action on the Heisenberg translations. Their quotient is a unitary scalar. Indeed, on the L² sections of the theta line over the compact W(F)∖W(𝔸_F), R_γ with γ rational is multiplication by ψ(B(w,γ)). By additive duality and by non-degeneracy of B, these are all the characters of the compact group. A bounded operator that commutes with them commutes with all the bounded multipliers (spectral theorem). In a measurable trivialisation of the line it is, therefore, multiplication by a function. Commuting with all the R_v implies that all its translates are equal in L². By Fourier it is constant. This can be applied in L² because the theta tests are dense and the representations preserve the norm. For continuous tests the equality holds at each point by the full support of Haar.

The scalars form a character b of U(S)(𝔸_F) by the group law. It is continuous: near each s both operators are evaluated at one and the same test and point where the denominator is non-zero. On U(S)(F) it is one by the rational invariance of the evaluation at zero, choosing a test that is non-zero there. Let us show the same at each split finite place v. Let e and ē be its two idempotents. If x records the e component of det(s) and equals one in the other components, the truncation at ē of that determinant is conj(x)⁻¹. Since x conj(x) lies in the diagonal copy of F_vˣ and the restriction of χ_z there is trivial (the local quadratic character of E/F is trivial), χ_z(conj(x)⁻¹)=χ_z(x).

To read the split phase we take a non-zero functional on the tests, invariant under R_v for v in the half ēW of the local space. Such a functional is obtained in the polarised Schrödinger model with ēW acting by modulations: one evaluates at zero in that variable and pairs the remaining variables with a fixed test. It is non-zero on a tensor of tests. Recall the identification of the models. In a rational polarisation W=X⊕Y, with ⟨x,y⟩=B(x,y), the transform of a Schwartz–Bruhat function is

    Zφ(x,y)=ψ(⟨x,y⟩/2) ∑_{r∈X(F)} ψ(⟨r,y⟩) φ(x+r).

The quasi-periodicity is checked by changing the index r. Removing the first factor and taking the zero Fourier coefficient in y one recovers φ(x); the other coefficients are φ(x+r). Parseval and integration in x modulo X(F) give the isometry in L². For the vectors of finite level, invariance under a compact open subgroup of modulations restricts the finite variable to the compact annihilator of the level, and the level translations give local constancy. At infinity the derivatives of the translations and modulations give the ordinary derivatives and the products by coordinates, respectively. On the compact quotient the smooth sections have those derivatives in L² to every order; the Sobolev inequality gives the Schwartz seminorms of their preimage. Thus the indicated local evaluation can be used on the space of tests. To adapt the polarisation only at v one uses the change of coordinates, the multiplication by a quadratic character and the partial Fourier transform that implement the change of Lagrangian in the local model. See [Gan, notes on the Weil representation, §§2.3–2.5](https://swc-math.github.io/aws/2022/2022GanNotes.pdf).

At the target, the law at split v multiplies that functional by a positive real number and by the phase χ_z(x) at the end, χ_z(x)⁻¹ in the interior. At the source one takes the opposite idempotent, since κ exchanges the halves. The phase there is χ_z(conj(x)⁻¹)=χ_z(x). In the interior case, J is antilinear: transporting the functional and conjugating its value so that it is linear again also conjugates this phase. In both cases the two phases coincide. On comparing at a test where the functional does not vanish, b(s) is positive and has modulus one. Hence it equals one.

To finish the proof of (⋆), if n=dim S≥2, SU(S) is simply connected and absolutely almost simple. At a split finite place v₀ it is SLₙ(F_v₀), non-compact. By strong approximation outside v₀, the rational points together with SU(S)(F_v₀) generate a dense subgroup of SU(S)(𝔸_F). Hence b is trivial there. The determinant of U(S) admits a rational section given by the action of the norm-one torus on a non-degenerate line and the identity on its orthogonal. It suffices to treat that torus, also when n=1 (n=0 is immediate). Outside the split finite places, each finite factor of the torus consists of units by the norm-one equation, so the product has the product topology. The rational points are dense in that projection: at any finite set of places one writes the local point as z=a/conj(a), by Hilbert 90, and approximates the a simultaneously by an element of Eˣ. At the split finite places, the elements of finite support are dense in the restricted product. Since b is already trivial on them and on the rational points, it is trivial on the whole torus. This proves (⋆) with the precise hypotheses of the strong approximation theorem cited in i4:Ast-thm-sa.

**Application to the end and to the interior contraction.** In an orthogonal sum P=P₁⊥P₂ the space W(P,S) is the orthogonal symplectic sum of the two spaces and its rational lattice is the product. The theta line decomposes as the exterior product of the two lines, so that its L² space is the Hilbert tensor product. The restricted large representation and the product of the two representations realise the same Heisenberg action, hence differ by a scalar character. This equals one on the rational points by evaluation at zero on product tests. At a split place, if x₁,x₂,y are the truncated determinants of the three elements, the large factor is χ_S(x₁x₂)⁻¹(χ₁χ₂)(y), exactly the product of the small factors. The local evaluation functional described above factorises on taking the polarisations in direct sum. The same argument of positivity and approximation for each group makes the character trivial. For an orthogonal sum at the target the factor is (χ₁χ₂)(x)⁻¹χ_S(y₁y₂) and one concludes in the same way. These rules use the product character on the large summand.

At the end we apply (⋆) with δ=−1 and evaluate at w=0. With the two preceding tensor rules, the computation of the preceding section gives exactly K₂(s,t)K₁(a,s), for each (a,s,t). The identity is also obtained after integrating over the three compact quotients with their measures already fixed. For a joint test Ξ it suffices first to do the computation on sums of products. Those sums are dense in the Schwartz topology of the polarisation: in the finite factor each function of fixed level and support decomposes into a finite number of rectangles, and in the real one the partial sums of the series in Hermite functions converge in all the seminorms by application of powers of the oscillator. The unitary action at a fixed element respects that topology by covariance with Heisenberg (it transports the level and the derivatives of the real action). The evaluation and the restriction to fixed variables in the theta coordinates are continuous by the series of Z. The pointwise identity thus passes to the original test.

In the interior, for a section f of W(L,S) and a section g of W(S,L'), the bilinear pairing in the shared variable is

    I(f,g)=∫_{W(S,L')(F)∖W(S,L')(𝔸_F)} f(κw) g(w) dw
          = ⟨Jf,g⟩,           ⟨a,b⟩=∫ conj(a)b.

The quasi-periodicity phases cancel because δ=+1. By (⋆) and unitarity, I(ω_{L,S}(1,s)f, ω_{S,L'}(s,1)g)=I(f,g) for every s. This applies in particular to the two sections in w obtained by fixing u,v in the joint tests of contr. The argument eliminates the phase of the interior contraction; to identify the periods of the groups in the interior links their unfolding formulae and convergence are still needed. At the end there remain the specialisations at the finite places and their compatibility with the witness points of (O3). The result of this section is the analytic identity for the Weil data indicated.

The source formulae are those of Alpoge in LocalNewformFunction (hO, symplO, IsWeilDatumO), SkewGramHalfModule (ofAdj), IsThetaFam (Link), WeilRepSourceLineEqOppositeTargetLine, WeilRepConjSourceLineEqTargetLine and LastSeeSawThetaIdentity. The classification of the appendix of 46 refers the deduction of the multiplicity row in the realisation to the new block of (O3), and cites the Weil representation and strong approximation among its published inputs.

**The interior integral after the contraction.** Let us make precise what is obtained for ContrLaw. Denote by a,s,b the variables in U(A), U(S), U(A') of the three residual spaces. Given the two already reduced tests Ξ₁,Ξ₂, put C(u,v)=I(Ξ₁(u,·),Ξ₂(v,·)), writing first the residual coordinate v of the second test. With ω_a and ω_b the Weil actions on W(A,S) and W(S,A'), respectively, the interior reading at b is

    ∫_[S] ∫_[A] φ(a) [ω_b(s,b)(v ↦ [ω_a(a,s)(u ↦ C(u,v))](0))](0) da ds.

Here the brackets [S],[A] denote the adelic quotients of the residual unitary groups. Indeed, take first Ξ₁(u,z)=p(u)f(z) and Ξ₂(v,w)=q(v)g(w). In handBF there appears the conjugate of the integral in a of the product φ(a)[ω_a(a,s)p](0)[ω_{L,S}(1,s)f](κw). In pairKer this whole expression is conjugated again on introducing it as second test in thPair. The other test evaluated at the residual variable zero gives [ω_b(s,b)q](0) times [ω_{S,L'}(s,1)g](w). Integrating in w produces I(ω_{L,S}(1,s)f,ω_{S,L'}(s,1)g)=I(f,g). Thus the two readings have the same integrand in a,s and C(u,v)=p(u)q(v)I(f,g).

To pass to joint tests, the contraction on the line is continuous: for each pair of derivatives acting on u,v, Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the value by the product of the two L² norms in w of the differentiated sections. At fixed level the additive domains have compact representatives, the level translations in the residual variables pass under the integral, and all the real orders pass by differentiation in charts of the theta line. The two test properties of ContrLaw are obtained, even after applying ω_a and evaluating u=0. The computation on products extends by the preceding density. To justify the integrals as well, [A] and [S] have compact representatives by the conditions of BesLev and JacLev. On compact sets of unitary elements the actions transport a given level to levels that contain one and the same compact open subgroup (cover by a finite number of cosets of its stabiliser). For the Heisenberg derivatives, the coefficients of the real changes of basis are uniformly bounded on that compact set. By unitarity and Sobolev, the evaluations in the integrands are then uniformly continuous as functionals of the tests in their smooth seminorms. The function φ is bounded on [A]. This allows passage to the limit under the integrals of finite mass and the application of Fubini. The integrated expressions descend: the evaluation at zero is rationally invariant and in the common variable the phases cancel inside I. The conclusion concerns both tests after the reduction to the residual spaces. The identity DoubleStep for the original periods involves in addition the radicals of positive rank and their quotients.

## The rational orbit that survives in the two periods of (O3)

Let N be the unipotent of a flag of r isotropic lines, in Witt coordinates X⊕V₀⊕Y, with X,Y in duality. Write n=n_C(c)n_B(b)l(U), where c is anti-Hermitian, b is a matrix r by dim V₀ and U is upper unitriangular of order r. The rational parameter of the mixed theta series is a matrix x of r columns in the Hermitian space W of the other group. Let G_x=x*H_W x. For a fixed test, the integration over [N]=N(F)∖N(𝔸_F) of the theta kernel with the weight of the period selects exactly the set of indices

    O={x rational : rank_E(x)=r, G_x=G₀}.

To select an index means to keep its summand in the formula of the average; its value may vanish for some test. In the direction Bessel to Fourier–Jacobi, G₀=0 and the base frame is r Witt vectors in W. In the reverse direction, r=σ+1, the base frame consists of σ Witt vectors and of the marked vector z on the residual line. Its Gram matrix is diag(0,…,0,z*H z) with z*H z non-zero. The Fourier–Jacobi weight allows here any continuous residual test with the prescribed quasi-periodicity law. We prove the selection for both directions.

**Coordinates and convergence of the average.** In the mixed model let M(x,w) be the test, where w is the theta variable of W(V₀,W). The summand with index x after acting by n and evaluating at zero is

    T_x(n)=ψ(q_x(c)) M(xU,xb),       q_x(c)=−½ Tr_{E/F}(ξ₀ tr(G_x c)).       (1)

These are the unipotent formulae of the Weil representation employed in the appendix. To make the normalisation precise, in the partial polarisation the central operator multiplies by ψ(B(extX(x),extY(xc))/2), which gives q_x by the Witt matrix. The operator of b is R_{xb} on w and that of U substitutes xU for x; its determinant is one. On evaluating R_{xb} at w=0 the Heisenberg phase equals one. The composition law is also checked in the quadratic term: the upper right block of n_B(b) is −bH₀⁻¹b*/2, and on multiplying two such elements there appears the additional central block c=(b'H₀⁻¹b*−bH₀⁻¹b'*)/2. Then q_x(c)=B₀(xb,xb')/2, exactly the phase of the product of the translations R. The model input used in (1) is the Weil action in mixed coordinates for the same splitting. In Alpoge's notation, the three actions are FormulasS.centre, .unip, .levi and the evaluation is MixedThetaSeriesTermsLawsUnipotentRational.

The expansion in x is obtained by applying the Fourier formula of the preceding section only in the hyperbolic directions and evaluating at zero. The residual value M(x,w) admits in turn the expansion of that section in a rational residual polarisation. In particular, at w=0 each term is bounded in absolute value by the sum, over the rational residual indices y, of the absolute values of a single Schwartz–Bruhat function in (x,y). This gives absolute and uniform convergence for tests ranging over a bounded set of Schwartz seminorms at fixed finite level and support: the rational points of that support lie in a fixed lattice at infinity and the sum of (1+|l|)⁻ᴹ over it converges for M greater than its real dimension. The translates of a fixed test by a compact set of N(𝔸_F) satisfy those bounds by the Heisenberg covariance, unitarity and Sobolev of the preceding section. Each unipotent group considered here admits compact representatives modulo its rational points: in the triangular coordinates the successive coordinates are reduced modulo the rationals in the successive additive fibres, with compact representatives in each. The weight of the period is bounded on those representatives by continuity. Thus the summands can be grouped and integrated in any order in the compact average.

**Integration in c.** The subgroup C of the n_C(c) is normal in N. On the fibre [C], on the left, the weight contributes the conjugate character of ψ(q₀(c)). For Bessel this is read off from the sum of simple coordinates, which ignores c. For Fourier–Jacobi the action of the centre on the test of the line is ψ(q_{z e_rᵀ}(c)); in the integrand it is conjugated. The equality G_{z e_rᵀ}=G₀ gives the indicated character. On each fibre only the terms with G_x=G₀ survive, by orthogonality of characters of the additive compact group [C]. Indeed the pairing (H,c)↦Tr_{E/F}(ξ₀ tr(Hc)) between Hermitian and anti-Hermitian matrices is non-degenerate. Multiplying c by ξ₀ identifies them with the Hermitian ones, and the trace pairing on these detects the diagonal with the diagonal units and each remaining entry with the two coordinates over F of a reciprocal pair. A non-zero rational coefficient therefore gives a non-trivial adelic character as c varies. To integrate by fibres one uses that N(F) projects onto the rational points of the quotient that erases c, as the blocks b,U show. At this stage it is not needed that the subsum with an arbitrary G_x be a function on [N]. The condition G_x=G₀ is, however, stable under x↦xU for U unitriangular, since G₀ depends at most on the last row, which U preserves.

**Elimination of the dependent frames.** Number the columns from 1 to r. For a dependent frame, let j be the first column that is a combination of the preceding ones. There is a unique vector a such that xa=0, a_j=1 and a_i=0 for i>j, by independence of the first j−1 columns. One has G₀a=0 in the part that passed the average in c. The value of j is stable under rational unitriangular changes. Let M_j be the normal subgroup of N that acts trivially on the first j columns of the flag of X. It is the kernel of the homomorphism to the block U of order j. On M_j one has U a=a. The subsum with j and a fixed is periodic under M_j(F): for γ rational the covariance in (1) reindexes x by xU_γ. The rational residual translations preserve the value evaluated at zero by quasi-periodicity, and the purely rational phases equal one. The weight is likewise periodic; in the Fourier–Jacobi case this uses the same quasi-periodicity of the test of the line. One can, therefore, first integrate this subsum over [M_j] and then sum over a. For the set with j fixed one finally integrates over the quotient by M_j. The section of this last quotient is obtained by inserting the block U of order j with the other blocks trivial. A change of rational representative of that section permutes the a and preserves their sum.

If j<r, take t=l(I+s a e_{j+1}ᵀ) with s∈𝔸_E. The matrix is unitriangular, t∈M_j and its sum of coordinates of the first superdiagonal is s. On the fibre we write n=m n' with m∈M_j(𝔸_F) and n' fixed. In (1) we incorporate n' into the test. On substituting m t for m its blocks c,b remain the same, and xU_m(I+s a e_{j+1}ᵀ)=xU_m because U_m a=a and xa=0. Consequently each theta term of the subsum has the same value. For Bessel the weight is multiplied by conj(ψ_E(s)), with ψ_E=ψ∘Tr_{E/F} in the simple convention of the character. For Fourier–Jacobi the flag factor gives that same scalar and the residual action of t is the identity: the model of the line has index z e_rᵀ, fixed by every upper unitriangular matrix, and a unitriangular Levi has determinant one. The Jacobi action is multiplicative on the stabiliser of that index, by the Heisenberg relation, so that the insertion between m and n' does not alter its residual coefficient either. We choose s with ψ_E(s)≠1. The measure of [M_j] is invariant under right translations, whence the integral of the subsum equals zero.

If j=r, in the Fourier–Jacobi case one would have 0=(G₀a)_r=(z*H z)a_r≠0, so this subsum is empty. In Bessel it can indeed appear. We choose a row λ with λz=s over the adeles, possible because the marked vector is non-zero, and take t=n_B(aλ). On the fibre m t changes b into b+U_m aλ=b+aλ. One still has x(b+aλ)=xb, the block U remains fixed and the correction of the block c is invisible since G_x=0. In the Bessel character only the term of the last row applied to z changes, by a_r λz=s. The same right translation annihilates the integral. This includes the case r=1, where there is no first superdiagonal. For r=0 there is a single empty index and no exclusion. The argument proves the two selections simultaneously using only the blocks that appear in the summand.

**A single set of conjugate frames.** In both directions the base frame is independent by the Witt decomposition and, in the second, because the residual vector has non-zero norm. Given two frames of O, the map that carries one to the other in order is an isometry of their subspaces by equality of Gram matrices. Witt's extension theorem for Hermitian forms over E/F also applies to degenerate subspaces of a non-degenerate ambient space. Hence O is a single orbit of U(W)(F). To distinguish the stabilisers, the stabiliser of the ordered frame fixes each column. On allowing in addition unitriangular changes of the frame, the stabiliser R is given by h x₀=x₀ U. In the first direction it is the Fourier–Jacobi group: the condition on X leaves the prescribed unitriangular action on X, the dual action on Y and any unitary on X⊥/X. The rest of the block is the Witt radical, by the equation h*Hh=H. In the second direction one applies this first to the r−1 isotropic columns and observes that the last is transformed into z plus a combination of them. On X⊥/X the unitary part has to fix z, hence is U(z⊥). This condition is sufficient because the radical only adds to z a vector of X. One obtains the Bessel subgroup with its marked line. These descriptions also hold over F, on reading the same rational blocks. Identifying the orbit under U(W)(F) with cosets by the stabiliser of the *ordered* frame and then gathering the unitriangular changes avoids counting an orbit more than once.

The selection is proved inside the average of the radical for any fixed test and in both directions. To employ it in the lift there is in addition an integral over the quotient of the group U(W), which may be non-compact. We explain next how that exchange of sums and integrals is controlled. The source statements are ThetaKernelMeanEqOrbitSumMean, UnfoldingOnlyBaseGramRationalPointsContribute, DependentFramesFirstRelationMeanZero, ConjBesselCharacterIsUnfoldingThetaFunctionOfGra, FourierBesselThetaIntegrandIsUnfoldingTheta and UnfoldingBasePointFourierJacobiGroup, in Alpoge's development.

**Convergence on integrating over the other group.** Write H(h) for the product of the local matrix heights of h and h⁻¹ of the datum and H_q(h)=inf_{γ∈U(W)(F)}H(γh). For each test Φ there are constants C,A, depending on the test and on the spaces, such that, for n in a compact set of representatives of [N],

    ∑_x |T_x(n;ω(1,h)Φ)| ≤ C H_q(h)^A.                              (2)

We give the details of the loss of level on moving h. In rational coordinates over ℚ choose a Heisenberg lattice whose finite completion leaves the test fixed; it can be reduced by a fixed integer and taken in direct sum in the complete polarisation. The translate by (n,h) is fixed by the transported lattice, by covariance. This contains k times the original lattice, for an integer k≤C₁H(h)^a with a fixed. Indeed one only has to clear denominators of the entries of the inverse action on the lattice. That action on Hom is matrix multiplication by the matrices of the two groups and their inverses. The fixed changes of bases and the n in the compact set introduce a fixed denominator. For each prime p, if the largest negative part of the valuations normalised by v(p)=1 is d_p>0, p^{⌈d_p⌉} clears those denominators in the local integral bases. The denominators of the valuations are bounded by [E:ℚ], so that ⌈d_p⌉≤[E:ℚ] d_p. The factor p^{d_p} is at most the product of the local heights over p: in the normalisation by the norm of the prime ideal, the local absolute value is the value that extends |p|_p=p⁻¹ raised to the local degree of F. Passing from integral bases to the chosen rational bases only costs another fixed integer, by commensurability. Multiplying over the primes gives the bound for k.

In the complete Schrödinger model, invariance under translations of level k makes the function constant on finite cells of measure c k⁻ᵈ, with d the dimension over ℚ of the polarisation. Invariance under modulations supports it on k⁻¹ times a fixed lattice in the finite variables. For each cell, the archimedean L² norm of a weighted derivative is therefore bounded by c⁻¹ᐟ² kᵈᐟ² times the global L² norm of that weighted derivative. The derivatives and products by coordinates are infinitesimal generators of Heisenberg. On commuting a word of length l with the action of (n,h) one obtains a combination of words of the same length with coefficients bounded by C_l H(h)^{a_l}, because that action and its inverse are matricial and n is in a compact set. Unitarity controls its norm by the corresponding norms of the fixed test. By Sobolev in ℝᵈ, taking sufficiently many derivatives and weights one obtains, for any fixed M, the pointwise bound C_M kᵈᐟ² H(h)^{a_M}(1+|t|)⁻ᴹ in the real variable t, uniform on the cell. The double expansion used above is, in absolute value, less than or equal to the sum of this bound over t∈k⁻¹Λ for a fixed lattice Λ. For M>d that sum of weights is O(kᵈ), counting points in balls of radius kR in Λ and summing in dyadic shells. Thus (2) results first with H(h). On changing h into γh, γ rational, the action on the mixed variable permutes the rational indices by x↦γ⁻¹x, and in the residual variable the evaluation at zero is rationally invariant. The absolute sum is the same. Taking the infimum one obtains (2).

A rapidly decreasing row G satisfies |G(h)|≤C_N H_q(h)⁻ᴺ for every integer N. With N≥A the resulting absolute integrand is bounded on [N]×[U(W)], of finite volume; the second factor has finite volume by the volume theorem for the unitary group (its centre is the anisotropic norm-one torus). The same argument admits a continuous residual weight on a compact set of parameters. This justifies Tonelli for the absolute values and Fubini for each regrouping of the selection, even on passing to the non-compact quotient of the other group. No uniformity between realisations is needed for this exchange, which is carried out before the arithmetic estimates.

**Unfolding formula.** Put H=U(W) and U=U_r the group of unitriangular matrices of the flag. For each representative δ of R(F)∖H(F), the indices δ⁻¹x₀ q, with q∈U(F) (here U is the group over F obtained by restriction of scalars from E), are distinct and run through O as δ and q vary. Indeed, by independence x₀ q determines q. Witt extension applied to x₀ and x₀q shows that R(F) acts on the base frame by all those q, and the equality of two classes of frames modulo U(F) is precisely equivalent to the equality of the left cosets of δ. By rational covariance, the change h↦δ h in the period transforms the sum over δ into the integral over R(F)∖H(𝔸_F). For q fixed, the substitution n↦l(q)n in (1) gives the index x₀q in place of x₀ and preserves the rational weight. Summing over q opens up the variable U: the fibre passes from [N] to N₀(F)∖N(𝔸_F), where N₀ is the kernel of N→U. These two unfoldings are understood using measurable representatives; if δ changes by an element of R(F), the q are permuted. The absolute convergence just proved allows the two changes of domain.

In that last quotient the variable U runs through U(𝔸_F), b runs through its rational additive quotient and c through its own. The product of the Haar measures on the blocks is that of N, since the group operations are triangular with determinants one. The constant factor is one on normalising the three rational quotients and [N] to volume one, as the datum requires. The central phases of the weight and of T_{x₀} cancel, so that the fibre of c integrates to one. If Θ denotes the original weight and M_h the mixed test of ω(1,h)Φ, there remains

    ∫_{R(F)∖H(𝔸_F)} G(h) ∫_{U(𝔸_F)} ∫_[b]
          Θ(n_B(b)l(U)) M_h(x₀U,x₀b) db dU dh.                       (3)

The integral in (3) is absolutely convergent. The integral in U is taken over the entire adelic group, whereas that in b keeps a fundamental domain. This explains the difference of measures in the definition fl. In the reverse direction, if b is written by its first σ rows and its last row, one obtains x₀b=e b_first+z b_last. The residual function of the line is evaluated at ζ b_last. In the product with its conjugate the periodicity phases cancel: the block e is totally isotropic and orthogonal to z and the two last coordinates have the same Hermitian form. Integrating first in b_first gives the mixed Fourier coordinate at zero of that block; afterwards one pairs on the line with the given quotient measure. The change b_last↦ζ b_last is an isomorphism of rational additive groups onto the line variable in its marked basis and preserves the adelic measure of covolume one (equivalently, the product formula). This identifies the contraction that occurs in HeisenbergIntegralFourierBesselBasePointEqPairin.

Formula (3) gives the unfolding of the average of the radical, including its convergence for the rapidly decreasing row. To convert it into the two identities of complete periods the integrand on the residual quotient and the exact translations at each step must still be identified. At the arithmetic level the realisation of the columns at the witness points remains necessary. The two references to (2) and (3) in Alpoge's source are ThetaTermSeriesSummableModerateGrowthUniform and UnfoldOverFlagUnipotentThetaLift.

**Link of the two Fourier–Jacobi readings.** Here a residual integrand can be identified before any integration over the radical. With the interior link τζ'=−ζ and τ*H_Lτ=|ξ₀|² H_L', write x=ζ'e_rᵀ, y=−ζe_rᵀ=τx. If r=0 the empty matrices are understood. Denote by D₀ and D_Y the blocks of n from the residual space and from Y to X, respectively. The Heisenberg shifts p_S,p_T in the models with the line at the target and at the source, and their central arguments c_S,c_T, satisfy

    p_S=xD₀,       p_T=−ξ₀⁻¹ H₀⁻¹ (yD₀)* H_L=κp_S,
    −c_S=ℓ(tr(x*H_L' x D_Y)),       c_T=ℓ(tr(y*H_L y D_Y)/|ξ₀|²),
    ℓ(t)=½ Tr_{E/F}(ξ₀ t).

Indeed, p_S is the residual block of the row (x,0,0)n. In the opposite orientation one projects to the residual space n⁻¹ applied to the vector in Y of coordinate −ξ₀⁻¹ y*H_L; the identity n⁻¹=H⁻¹ n* H and the duality between X,Y give the block H₀⁻¹D₀* applied to that coordinate. For the central terms one evaluates B/2 at the index vector and its translate. In the first orientation the Hermitian trace of the product in the inverted order is tr(x*H_L' x D_Y), whence the sign by alternation. In the second, if η=−ξ₀⁻¹ y*H_L are the coordinates on Y, the trace is tr(H_L⁻¹ η*D_Y η); by cyclicity it equals tr(y*H_L y D_Y)/|ξ₀|². The Gram matrix of y equals |ξ₀|² times that of x, hence c_T=−c_S. This comparison, JacobiActionsMatchThroughLinkMap, fixes the phase even on the elements with non-zero block D_Y.

Let F_h(w)=∫_[A] φ(a)[ω(a⊕1,h)Ξ](0,w) da and let Y_h(w')=conj(F_h(κw')). This is handBF. Joint continuity results by integration over compact representatives of [A]; the quasi-periodicity in w' follows from the antisymmetry of κ for δ=+1 and from the conjugation. If γ∈U(S)(F), the rational action on the residual variable on a theta section is the change of argument by γ⁻¹. Indeed R_v f(0)=f(v); by covariance with Heisenberg and invariance of the evaluation at zero, [ω(γ)f](v)=f(γ⁻¹·v). Since κ(w'γ)=γ⁻¹κ(w'), one obtains Y_{γh}(w')=Y_h(w'γ), the required family law. In the reading of the second model there appears conj(ψ(c_S)[R_{p_S}Y_h](0)); it equals ψ(−c_S) F_h(κp_S)=ψ(c_T) F_h(p_T). This is also the kernel of the first model: the Heisenberg translation is made only in the coordinate L and commutes with the action of a on A, and one evaluates finally at zero. The other two functions of the integrand, the translated row and the conjugate of the flag character, coincide without change of variables. One obtains the pointwise equality that gives LinkLaw and, by integration, the equality of both periods in that link.

## The two complete periods in an interior step

We fix the spaces, compatible characters and measures of a step of the chain. We use the Weil representation of the intermediate spaces with the same characters: removing hyperbolic planes decreases the dimension by an even number and preserves the restriction condition of the character. This is the application of the existence of the Weil splittings to the datum already fixed. The following computation gives the analytic identities of periods, before specifying the value of the tests at each finite place.

Denote by DΦ(x,y) the residual theta section obtained by taking the successive mixed coordinates of the source in x and of the target in y. On a unitriangular group of order r we put χ(U)=ψ_E(∑_{i<r} U_{i,i+1}). It is a character because, in the product of two unitriangular matrices, each entry of the first superdiagonal is the sum of the two corresponding entries. The residual test of both steps is written

    PΦ = ∫_{U_r(𝔸_E)} conj(χ(U)) DΦ(x₀U,y₀) dU.                    (4)

In the direction Bessel to Fourier–Jacobi, x₀ is the isotropic frame of the target and y₀=−z e_rᵀ, z being the marked vector of the residual source A⊥L. To verify (4) on evaluating at zero, let v be the inclusion of the variable b in the isotropic summand of the target, v=x₀b, and let d be the rational adjoint inclusion of y₀ in the dual summand. In the definition of the mixed coefficient one evaluates R_v R_d f at zero. By the Heisenberg law and the quasi-periodicity in d its value is ψ(B(v,d)) f(v): the two half-phases have the same sign. The pairing in Witt coordinates gives B(v,d)=Tr_{E/F}(tr(y₀ b)), that is −Tr_{E/F}(e_rᵀ b z). Indeed d lies in the dual block with coordinate −ξ₀⁻¹ y₀*H, and in the Hermitian form h(v,d)=tr(H⁻¹d*H_W v) the conjugate coefficient cancels ξ₀. Thus one obtains the conjugate of the Bessel character of the block b in (3). In the direction Fourier–Jacobi to Bessel one takes r=σ+1, x₀=(e₁,…,e_σ,z) and y₀=0; the separation of the rows of b proved after (3) gives the second mixed coordinate and the pairing on the line with the Fourier–Jacobi test.

The integral (4) exists in the space of residual tests. In the complete Schrödinger model, DΦ corresponds to a Schwartz–Bruhat function in (x,y,t), with t the residual polarised coordinate. It is obtained by the preceding partial Fourier identifications; the passage from the theta section in t to the periodic coordinates is made at the end. Since x₀ has a rational left inverse, U↦x₀U identifies the strictly upper entries with a rationally defined closed affine subspace. Restricting a Schwartz–Bruhat function to this subspace and to y=y₀ produces a Schwartz–Bruhat function of those entries and of t: at infinity the linear inverse on the image suffices, and at the finite places the support and the level are preserved in a compact set and an open set respectively. The measure dU is an additive product of Haar measures in the free entries by triangularity. The partial integration against χ̄ preserves all the weights and derivatives in t by decay in U. In particular one can apply a residual Weil operator, a Heisenberg translation or an evaluation after integrating, by continuity in the Schwartz topology. This also gives the continuity in the translation parameters: the Weil action is continuous on the Schwartz–Bruhat vectors, locally at common finite level, and the preceding operations of restriction and integration are continuous on each of those spaces.

**Translating the test in the direction Fourier–Jacobi to Bessel.** If n is in the radical N_B at the target, n x₀=x₀γ(n) with

    γ(n) = [ U   b z ]
           [ 0    1  ],

where U and b are the blocks of n in X and from the residual space to X. In particular χ(γ(n))=ψ_B(n), since the last entry of the first superdiagonal is (b z)_σ and the others come from U. For σ=0 both characters equal one. For a∈U(A) the element emb(a) fixes the frame x₀ since it fixes X and z. The action on the first mixed coordinate substitutes (n emb(a))⁻¹x for x. On the second, of index zero, the block b induces the Heisenberg translation by zero and the central block has quadratic argument zero. The unitriangular Levi fixes zero and its determinant and its modulus equal one. There remains the residual action ω₀(s,a⊕1) when the element s of the residual group of the source is inserted as well. Thus, substituting U=γ(n)U' in (4),

    P(ω(emb(s),n emb(a)) Φ)=conj(ψ_B(n)) ω₀(s,a⊕1) PΦ.            (5)

No stabiliser index intervenes in this substitution because one integrates over the whole unitriangular group with left Haar measure.

**Translating the test in the direction Bessel to Fourier–Jacobi.** Write n=l(γ)n_B(b)n_C(c) in the flag radical of the target. The element l(γ) carries x₀ to x₀γ and fixes the index y₀ under right multiplication, since the last row of a unitriangular matrix is e_rᵀ. The same change of variable in (4) contributes the factor conj(χ(γ))=conj(ψ_flag(n)). The rest of the radical and the residual group emb(s) fix x₀. By the Weil formulae at the second index y₀, n_B(b) acts on the residual test by R_{ofAdj(y₀ b)} and n_C(c) by ψ(q_T(c,y₀)). In the notation of the link this is J_T(n)=ψ(c_T(n))R_{p_T(n)}: in the block n_B(b)n_C(c) the formula for p_T gives ofAdj(y₀b), and the part −bH₀⁻¹b*/2 of the upper right block does not contribute to c_T because the trace of its product with the Hermitian Gram matrix is real and Tr_{E/F}(ξ₀ t)=0 for t real over F. On prefixing l(γ), y₀γ=y₀ preserves both formulae. The action of a∈U(A) fixes y₀; its residual action commutes with the translation on the line. From this one obtains, in the order of operators of the period,

    P(ω(emb(a),n emb(s)) Φ)
       = conj(ψ_flag(n)) ω₀(a⊕1,1) J_T(n) ω₀(1,s) PΦ.              (6)

The identities used in (5) and (6) are the formulae in the mixed Witt model: the group that acts on the other space substitutes the index by its inverse image and acts on the residual representation; the group of the Witt kernel acts only on the residual representation. These two rules can be read off in the orthogonal product of the kernel with the hyperbolic planes. The trivial character is attributed to the hyperbolic part in the decomposition, possible by its even dimension; in its polarisation the group of the other space acts on the matrices of indices by change of variables. Its determinant has norm one, and the action of its group on the residual model carries the original character. In the Levi that changes the basis of X only the character of the determinant and the half-modulus appear; both are evaluated at one in this computation. The directions b and c are the translations and phases of formula (1); in the orientation of the target one uses the adjoint inclusion in the second polarisation. Hence the character in (5) and the flag part of (6) come from the character χ̄ in the integral and are preserved for joint tests.

**Measure of the second integration.** Let H be the group of the translated row and R the subgroup obtained from the orbit in (3). Denote by K(h) the unfolded integrand after integrating over the residual group of the starting period, with the row G(h) included. Formula (3) and its majorant give ∫_{R(F)∖H(𝔸)} |K|<∞: the residual parameters are integrated over compact representatives, using the same bound (2), uniform in them. K is continuous by (4) and invariant on the left under R(F). For this last assertion the row is automorphic; in the remaining part, (5) or (6) gives trivial character on the rational elements, rationally invariant evaluation and, in the case of J_T, rational translation with phase that equals one in the evaluation at zero. In the case of the Fourier–Jacobi family one uses in addition the law Y_{γs}(w)=Y_s(wγ). It is equivalent to the periodicity obtained on permuting the q in the preceding unfolding.

Take an exact Borel domain D for R(F) on the left in H(𝔸). It exists because this subgroup is discrete and countable, and is obtained by covering by a countable family of open sets injective in the quotient and assigning each class successively to the first that contains it. The function 1_D K is integrable on H(𝔸). For each h, the r such that rh∈D constitute an exact domain of the same rational group in R(𝔸). Since [R] is compact (the residual group of R is cocompact in the Bessel and Jacobi data and the radical has compact quotient), one obtains with the measures of the datum

    ∫_{R(𝔸)} (1_D K)(rh) dr = ∫_[R] K(rh) dr,       for each h.
    ∫_{H(𝔸)} 1_D K = ∫_{R(F)∖H(𝔸)} K.

The first integral is absolutely convergent for each h by continuity and compactness in that fibre. R is the semidirect product of its flag radical and its residual group. On the radical, the action of the residual group has modulus one: the block b is transformed by a unitary matrix and the central block and the flag Levi remain fixed. The rational points decompose by the same coordinates, so that the measures of the two groups in the period give precisely dr. The preceding equalities verify the condition IsQuotIntL1 even when the functions are evaluated at an individual representative. It is not necessary to assign a finite volume to R(𝔸).

To see the integrand of the fibre, write r=n emb(a) in the case of (5). The reading of the reduced column at a is handFB(Y,P(ω(1,h)Φ))(a); multiplying it by conj(ψ_B(n)) G(n emb(a)h) one obtains exactly the integrand of the Bessel period of the translated row. In the case (6), for r=n emb(s) there remains G(n emb(s)h) conj(ψ_flag(n)) times the integral in a of φ(a)[ω₀(a⊕1,1)J_T(n)ω₀(1,s)P(ω(1,h)Φ)](0). This is the Fourier–Jacobi period identified in the section on the link. Thus (3), (5), (6) and the two Haar equalities give the two complete identities UnfBF and UnfFB for the analytic datum.

**Composition.** Apply the first identity with the row of the second lift. For each intermediate translation h₁, changing the variable h of the integral that defines the lift shows that R_{h₁}(Λ₂G) is computed with the second test ω₂(h₁,1)Φ₂. With the convention R_b f(s)=f(sb), that kernel is ω₂(s h₁,t)Φ₂ = ω₂(s,t)ω₂(h₁,1)Φ₂. The interior link identifies the intermediate period with the one that uses Y=handBF(φ,P₁ω₁(g,h₁)Φ₁). The second identity, applied to that Y and to ω₂(h₁,1)Φ₂, gives at each h₂ the column handFB(Y,P₂ω₂(h₁,h₂)Φ₂) against the row R_{h₂}G. The condition of continuity and automorphy of this column results from the integral in the residual variable and from the law of Y: the residual quotients are compact and the conjugate phases cancel in the pairing on the line. One obtains the two *iterated* quotient integrals of DoubleStep for every translation. The decay hypotheses are those of the identity: they are required for G and for its first lift on the intermediate group.

The induction on the length of the chain replaces a period of rank σ+1 by that of rank σ with two translations and the preceding tests. In rank zero the terminal identity already proved is applied. The order of each quotient integral is preserved: the assertion does not require permuting integrals over two non-compact quotients. This justifies the analytic descent of the chain for the compatible Weil data and the lifts with the indicated decay. Afterwards the special tests must be evaluated at the finite places and the data realised at the witness points. Alpoge's references for this computation are FlagUnipotentUnfoldingBesselFourierJacobiEq, DoubleMixedModelBaseValueEquivariant, DoubleMixedModelValueAtZeroEquivariant, FlatSchwartzTargetUnipotentConjBesselCharacter, BesselDoubleStepUnfoldingOfUnfoldingsAndLink and IteratedThetaUnfoldingOfDoubleStepLastTwoSteps.
